     1          % ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
     2          % CHATSV - the chat room server, as a SINTRAN RT program.
     3          %
     4          % COMPILED AND RUN ON D100, 2026-08-18. It served a real room to two real
     5          % clients on two terminals, and the round trip both ways is in the log:
     6          %
     7          %     routed letter    rcv st= 0  t= 2  len= 25   hdr ends= 16   parse k=1 nlen=5
     8          %     ordinary msg     rcv st= 0  t= 1  len= 14   parse k=4 nlen=5   slot now= 1
     9          %
    10          % Those two lines came from diagnostics that have since been taken out; what
    11          % each of them proved is written beside the code it proved it about.
    12          %
    13          % Every XMSG routine below, and the way it is CALLED, is taken from the routine
    14          % reference and worked examples in
    15          %     Operations/Cosmos/ND-60164-3-EN COSMOS Programmer Guide.md
    16          % rather than from analogy. Four things that reference corrected, each of which
    17          % would have cost a compile or worse:
    18          %
    19          %   XMPOPNM -> XMPOPCN   a room has SEATS, and the free-connection counter
    20          %                        belongs to a CONNECTION port. With XMPOPNM the limit is
    21          %                        silently absent while the C# side still enforces it.
    22          %   XMPFFRE -> XMPFREL   XMPFFRE does not exist.
    23          %   XMPFREA/XMPFWRI      take NO message identifier - they act on the CURRENT
    24          %                        message, as XMPFSND sends it.
    25          %   ADDR(buf)            is not enough. The guide's own examples write
    26          %                        addr(buf(0)) FORCE XMUSERADDRESS.
    27          %
    28          % And every call RETURNS its status - the examples all end "=: returnStatus".
    29          % That is how the statuses below are checked; XMOK is zero, from XMP-B02:DEFS.
    30          %
    31          % THE MESSAGE FORMAT IS OURS. We chose it; no ND ever sent one, so no capture
    32          % will ever confirm it and Xmsg.Chat/ChatMessage.cs plus its tests are its only
    33          % specification. The rules below are a transcription of Xmsg.Chat/ChatRoom.cs,
    34          % which has the tests that say what "correct" means.
    35          %
    36          %     byte 0      kind          1 Join 2 Welcome 3 Reject 4 Say 5 Said
    37          %                               6 Leave 7 Joined 8 Left 9 Rename 10 Renamed
    38          %     byte 1      nickname length
    39          %     bytes 2..   nickname
    40          %     then        2 bytes text length, big endian
    41          %     then        the text
    42          %
    43          % THE BYTE LAYOUT IS PINNED IN C#, and this file must agree with it by hand.
    44          % SRC/Xmsg.Chat.Tests/ChatMessageGoldenBytesTests.cs holds the exact bytes of a
    45          % Said, a Renamed, a Join and a 300-character line. There is no shared code
    46          % between that and this - only those goldens - so if the layout ever changes,
    47          % both sides change in the SAME commit or they disagree in silence.
    48          %
    49          %
    50          % THREE CORRECTIONS FROM APPENDIX G, the guide's own sample CLIENT and SERVER:
    51          %
    52          %   flags are 2**BIT      XFWTF is a BIT POSITION (15), not a flags word. The
    53          %                         samples both write "2**XFWTF =: FLAGS". Passing 15
    54          %                         would set bits 0,1,2,3 - four unrelated options - and
    55          %                         the wait would simply not happen.
    56          %   XMMSGIDENTIFIER       the message-identifier type, with two M's. Written
    57          %                         "XMMSGIDENTIFIER: MSGIDENT" in the sample.
    58          %   XMUSERADDRESS         and the address idiom is
    59          %                         ADDR(BUF(0)) FORCE XMUSERADDRESS. ROUND brackets -
    60          %                         the guide prints square ones and PLANC F rejects
    61          %                         them with EXPECTS ")" ILLEGAL SYNTAX "[".
    62          %
    63          % ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    64          
    65          MODULE chatsv
    66          
    67          % ---- HANDED TO CHATVT, the offline server-side test -----------------------
    68          % EXPORT allocates nothing - it only makes the name visible across the BRF
    69          % boundary, so CHATVT.PLNC can drive buildFromSlot and broadcast with
    70          % synthetic members and inspect outBuf, the way CHATCTST drives the client.
    71          % The block sits HERE, before the includes, because EXPORT must follow the
    72          % MODULE statement - after an ordinary declaration it is MISPLACED STATEMENT,
    73          % one error per line (R119; it cost CHATLIB ten errors that still linked).
    74          %
    75          % SEVEN CHARACTERS is all a BRF entry carries. No two of these agree in their
    76          % first seven, and planc-lint checks that across the whole set of sources.
    77          EXPORT buildFromSlot
    78          EXPORT broadcast
    79          EXPORT sendTo
    80          EXPORT outBuf
    81          EXPORT inBuf
    82          EXPORT memberUsed
    83          EXPORT memberName
    84          EXPORT memberLen
    85          EXPORT memberMagic
    86          EXPORT mbrRoom
    87          EXPORT lenMbrRoom
    88          EXPORT myPort
    89          EXPORT sendFlags
    90          
    91          % THE REAL COSMOS BINDING, from ND-10609, and INSIDE the module. Above MODULE
    92          % the declarations land in the outer scope, the body cannot see them, and every
    93          % call fails NOT PREVIOUSLY DECLARED while the include's own lines draw no
    94          % diagnostic at all - so they look accepted and simply are not there.
    95          %
    96          % Do NOT also include XMSG-PL-VALUES-L:INCL: it shares 184 constant names with
    97          % XMP-B02:DEFS. DEFS is the authority for this library layer, VALUES-L for the
    98          % raw MON 200B interface underneath it.
    99          $INCLUDE XMP-B02:DEFS
   100     (1)  %=============================================================================%

   105     (6)  %                                                                             %
   106     (7)  %                              F O R                                          %
   107     (8)  %                                                                             %
   108     (9)  %                   P L A N C    I N T E R F A C E                            %

   113    (14)  %                symbols prefixed by XM-                                      %
   114    (15)  %                                                                             %

   124    (25)  %                                                                             %
   125    (26)  %                         XMSG FUNCTION VALUES                                %
   126    (27)  %                                                                             %
   127    (28)  %-----------------------------------------------------------------------------%
   128    (29)  
   129    (30)  CONSTANT XFDUM=0             % Dummy function
   130    (31)  CONSTANT XFDCT=1             % Disconnect from message system
   131    (32)  CONSTANT XFGET=2             % Get message space
   132    (33)  CONSTANT XFREL=3             % Release message space
   133    (34)  CONSTANT XFRHD=4             % Read header from a message (6 bytes)
   134    (35)  CONSTANT XFWHD=5             % Write header to a message (6 bytes)
   135    (36)  CONSTANT XFREA=6             % Read from message to user buffer
   136    (37)  CONSTANT XFWRI=7             % Write from user to message
   137    (38)  CONSTANT XFSCM=8             % Set current message
   138    (39)  CONSTANT XFMST=9             % Get message status
   139    (40)  CONSTANT XFOPN=10            % Open port
   140    (41)  CONSTANT XFCLS=11            % Close port
   141    (42)  CONSTANT XFSND=12            % Send message to a remote port
   142    (43)  CONSTANT XFRCV=13            % Receive a message on a given port
   143    (44)  CONSTANT XFPST=14            % Get local port status
   144    (45)  CONSTANT XFGST=15            % General status or wait
   145    (46)  CONSTANT XFSIN=16            % Service initialisation function
   146    (47)  %        XFSRL=17            % Service release function (obsolete)
   147    (48)  CONSTANT XFABR=18            % Absolute read block from POF area
   148    (49)  %        XFABW=19            % Absolute write block to POF area (obsolete)
   149    (50)  %        XFMLK=20            % Lock message system (obsolete)
   150    (51)  %        XFMUL=21            % Unlock message system (obsolete)
   151    (52)  CONSTANT XFM2P=22            % Magic number to port id.
   152    (53)  CONSTANT XFP2M=23            % Port to magic number
   153    (54)  %        XFRIN=24            % Routing initialise (obsolete)
   154    (55)  CONSTANT XFCRD=25            % Create driver with context
   155    (56)  CONSTANT XFSTD=26            % Start driver
   156    (57)  %        XFDIB=27            % Define indirect buffer (obsolete)
   157    (58)  %        XFRIB=28            % Read from indirect buffer (obsolete)
   158    (59)  %        XFWIB=29            % Write to indirect buffer (obsolete)
   159    (60)  CONSTANT XFPRV=30            % Request privilege
   160    (61)  CONSTANT XFRTN=31            % Write word 0 and return message
   161    (62)  CONSTANT XFRRH=32            % Receive message and read word 0
   162    (63)  CONSTANT XFDUB=33            % Define user buffer area for current message
   163    (64)  CONSTANT XFWDF=34            % Define wake-up context (drivers only!)
   164    (65)  CONSTANT XFDBK=35            % Define bank no (drivers only)
   165    (66)  CONSTANT XFSMC=36            % start multi-function call
   166    (67)  CONSTANT XFDMM=37            % define max memory for a task
   167    (68)  CONSTANT XFALM=38            % allocate messages to a task
   168    (69)  CONSTANT XFFRM=39            % free allocated messages
   169    (70)  CONSTANT XFLMP=40            % list messages and ports
   170    (71)  CONSTANT XFRRE=41            % receive and read message 
   171    (72)  CONSTANT XFCPV=42            % check system and/or user privileges
   172    (73)  CONSTANT X5FUN=43            % == END MARKER == LEAVE ME HERE PLEASE
   173    (74)

   181    (82)  CONSTANT XFWTF=15  % If set then wait if operation not terminated.
   182    (83)  CONSTANT XFWAK=14  % Wake up task on status change.
   183    (84)  %        XFPRM=13  % (Obsolete).
   184    (85)  CONSTANT XFPON=13  % Driver to run with paging on.
   185    (86)  %        XFWOK=13  % (Obsolete).
   186    (87)  CONSTANT XFUSG=13  % (Not yet implemented).
   187    (88)  CONSTANT XFHIP=13  % High-priority message.
   188    (89)  CONSTANT XFRRO=13  % If XFROU then non-local XROUT.
   189    (90)  CONSTANT XFEXC=13  % Allocate/reserve/release exclusive message buffer(s).
   190    (91)  CONSTANT XFOPS=12  % (Not yet implemented).
   191    (92)  CONSTANT XFRES=12  % Reset message length to zero.
   192    (93)  CONSTANT XFBNC=12  % Bounce message.
   193    (94)  CONSTANT XFFWD=11  % Forward message.
   194    (95)  CONSTANT XFROU=10  % Message to be sent to XROUT (see XFRRO).
   195    (96)  CONSTANT XFSEC=9   % Secure message (Return if not delivered).
   196    (97)  CONSTANT XFSYS=7   % Bit set to indicate system mode call (system domain).
   197    (98)

   229   (130)  CONSTANT XENOT= -1     % No more XT-blocks free
   230   (131)  CONSTANT XEIRM= -2     % Non-local remote port illegal here
   231   (132)  CONSTANT XETMM= -4     % Task is not allowed any more memory
   232   (133)  CONSTANT XENIM= -5     % Facility not yet implemented
   233   (134)  CONSTANT XEIBP= -6     % Illegal message buffer pointer
   234   (135)  CONSTANT XEBNY= -7     % Message buffer not yours
   235   (136)  CONSTANT XEIRT= -8     % Illegal function for RT-programs (only drivers)
   236   (137)  CONSTANT XENOP= -9     % No more ports available
   237   (138)  CONSTANT XEIDR= -10    % Function not available to drivers
   238   (139)  CONSTANT XENDM= -11    % No default message
   239   (140)  CONSTANT XEMCH= -12    % Message is already chained
   240   (141)  CONSTANT XEBFC= -13    % Message is in a queue
   241   (142)  CONSTANT XEAIN= -14    % XMSG Kernel already initialised
   242   (143)  CONSTANT XEBNC= -15    % Return of a bounce message
   243   (144)  CONSTANT XEWNA= -16    % Write Not Allowed (Indirect buffer)
   244   (145)  CONSTANT XENVI= -17    % No Valid Indirect buffer defined
   245   (146)  CONSTANT XEILF= -18    % Illegal function code in monitor call
   246   (147)  CONSTANT XEIMA= -19    % Invalid magic number
   247   (148)  CONSTANT XEMFL= -20    % Message space full
   248   (149)  CONSTANT XEILM= -21    % Illegal message size or not enough space left
   249   (150)  CONSTANT XEIPN= -22    % Illegal port number
   250   (151)  CONSTANT XEPRV= -23    % Privileged function called without privilege
   251   (152)  CONSTANT XEPVR= -24    % Privilege request refused
   252   (153)  CONSTANT XERNA= -25    % Remote system not available
   253   (154)  CONSTANT XEROV= -26    % Remote task space overflow
   254   (155)  CONSTANT XEXBF= -27    % Message already has XMSG buffer (XFDUB)
   255   (156)  CONSTANT XEDRI= -28    % Driver called XMSG before return from previous
   256   (157)  CONSTANT XENDP= -29    % No port open (so 'default port' param invalid)
   257   (158)  CONSTANT XEITL= -30    % Illegal transfer length for read/write
   258   (159)  CONSTANT XEIDP= -31    % Illegal displacement in read/write
   259   (160)  CONSTANT XEILR= -32    % Illegal use of reentrant segment in XFDIB
   260   (161)  CONSTANT XENOS= -33    % Indirect Buffer not on valid segment
   261   (162)  CONSTANT XENSE= -34    % Network sequencing error
   262   (163)  CONSTANT XERND= -35    % Remote system not defined
   263   (164)  CONSTANT XEPCL= -36    % Remote port closed while message queued
   264   (165)  CONSTANT XENRU= -37    % XMSG not running 
   265   (166)  CONSTANT XENTO= -38    % Timeout detected by network layer
   266   (167)  CONSTANT XENUS= -39    % No user segment info defined (XFUSG option)
   267   (168)  CONSTANT XEREJ= -40    % Network remote reject (request retransmit)
   268   (169)  CONSTANT XEIXT= -41    % Driver called XMSG with illegal XT-block
   269   (170)  CONSTANT XETMU= -42    % Too many multi calls
   270   (171)  CONSTANT XECRA= -63    % XMSG crash (Info in Basefield)

   391   (292)  CONSTANT XSDDF=3       % Define/remove dump-files
   392   (293)  CONSTANT XSGDF=4       % Get defined dump-files
   393   (294)  CONSTANT XSDUX=5       % Dump XMSG onto files
   394   (295)  CONSTANT XSGRF=6       % Get defined restart-files
   395   (296)  CONSTANT XSGAR=7       % Get auto-restart definition
   396   (297)  
   397   (298)  % Sub-service definitions for XSGAT
   398   (299)  
   399   (300)  CONSTANT XSGXV=1       % Get XMSG version and revision
   400   (301)  CONSTANT XSCMG=2       % Check magic number
   401   (302)  CONSTANT XSGCN=3       % Get complete system or port name (deabbreviate name)
   402   (303)  CONSTANT XSGFR=4       % Get friend information (privileged!)
   403   (304)  
   404   (305)  % Sub-service definitions for XSDAT (privileged)
   405   (306)  
   406   (307)  CONSTANT XSDFR=1       % Define friend system
   407   (308)  CONSTANT XSRFR=2       % Remove friend system
   408   (309)  
   409   (310)  %-----------------------------------------------------------------------------%
   410   (311)  %                                                                             %
   411   (312)  %          X R O U T  /  F I L E   S E R V E R   -    E R R O R S             %
   412   (313)  %                                                                             %
   413   (314)  %             Error values returned in byte 1 of return message               %
   414   (315)  %                                                                             %
   415   (316)  %                                                                             %
   416   (317)  %      These are the values in the message header itself which the            %
   417   (318)  %      user sees when the XROUT header is manipulated directly by the         %
   418   (319)  %      user.                                                                  %
   419   (320)  %      The PLANC XMSG library returns the STANDARD values (see later)         %
   420   (321)  %                                                                             %
   421   (322)  %-----------------------------------------------------------------------------%
   422   (323)  
   423   (324)  CONSTANT XRXXX=16960   % Base for XROUT errors: 41100B
   424   (325)  CONSTANT XRSOK=0       % OK - not an error!
   425   (326)  CONSTANT XRISN=1       % Illegal service number
   426   (327)  CONSTANT XRUNN=2       % Unknown name (of server or system)
   427   (328)  CONSTANT XRDDF=3       % Another port already has this name
   428   (329)  CONSTANT XRNSP=4       % No space left for names
   429   (330)  CONSTANT XRIPT=5       % Illegal parameter type
   430   (331)  CONSTANT XRMMP=6       % Missing mandatory parameter
   431   (332)  CONSTANT XRUNM=7       % Unknown magic number
   432   (333)  CONSTANT XRMTL=8       % Too short message (or message buffer)
   433   (334)  CONSTANT XRSMF=9       % Standard message format not handled
   434   (335)  CONSTANT XRPRV=10      % Caller was not privileged
   435   (336)  CONSTANT XRISY=11      % Illegal system number parameter
   436   (337)  CONSTANT XRNRO=12      % No access to remote system
   437   (338)  CONSTANT XRIIV=13      % Illegal integer value
   438   (339)  CONSTANT XRNEI=14      % Cannot define route to a neighbour
   439   (340)  CONSTANT XRNXM=15      % Invalid service request - no intersystem XMSG
   440   (341)  CONSTANT XRILN=16      % Illegal/Reserved Log. unit no. for link
   441   (342)  CONSTANT XRNXL=17      % No more XL-Blocks (Link Descriptors)
   442   (343)  CONSTANT XRNXD=18      % Not enough XD-Blocks for LKINI
   443   (344)  CONSTANT XRNTR=19      % No trace generated
   444   (345)  CONSTANT XRTRA=20      % Trace already active
   445   (346)  CONSTANT XRTRP=21      % Trace passive
   446   (347)  CONSTANT XRTFE=22      % Trace/dump file open error (see param 1)
   447   (348)  CONSTANT XRTRT=23      % Trace RT-prog (XTRACE) not found
   448   (349)  CONSTANT XRTIS=24      % Illegal trace system number
   449   (350)  CONSTANT XRBLK=25      % Bad link - open unsuccessful
   450   (351)  CONSTANT XRSYD=26      % Local system number already defined
   451   (352)  CONSTANT XRNLS=27      % No local system number defined yet
   452   (353)  CONSTANT XRTRE=28      % Too many remote names to this system
   453   (354)  CONSTANT XRRNA=29      % Old service calls (<64) cannot go remote
   454   (355)  CONSTANT XRBUS=30      % Service busy - try later!
   455   (356)  CONSTANT XRNSE=31      % This is not a service port
   456   (357)  CONSTANT XRRPN=32      % Remote port statically declared
   457   (358)  CONSTANT XRUKS=33      % Unknown remote system name
   458   (359)  CONSTANT XRMFL=34      % Not enough message table space in remote system
   459   (360)  CONSTANT XRROV=35      % Remote task space overflow when forwarding
   460   (361)  CONSTANT XRRFU=36      % Routing table space full (too many systems def.)
   461   (362)  CONSTANT XRNRB=37      % No remote batch service available here
   462   (363)  CONSTANT XRURT=38      % Unknown RT program name
   463   (364)  CONSTANT XRSNR=39      % This server is not running
   464   (365)  CONSTANT XRRND=40      % Netserver: remote system is not defined
   465   (366)  CONSTANT XRNNA=41      % Netserver: network not available
   466   (367)  CONSTANT XRISE=42      % Netserver: internal server error
   467   (368)  CONSTANT XRIRQ=43      % Netserver: invalid request
   468   (369)  CONSTANT XRNGA=44      % XMSG not congfigurated with gateway code
   469   (370)  CONSTANT XRRNL=45      % Remote system not on same LAN
   470   (371)  CONSTANT XRNCO=46      % No connection to this system (unknown status)
   471   (372)  CONSTANT XRAMB=47      % Ambiguous name
   472   (373)  CONSTANT XRFFU=48      % Friend system table full (too many friends)
   473   (374)  
   474   (375)  %-----------------------------------------------------------------------------%
   475   (376)  %                                                                             %
   476   (377)  %         X R O U T  /  F I L E   S E R V E R   -    E R R O R S              %
   477   (378)  %                                                                             %
   478   (379)  %                             A S                                             %
   479   (380)  %                                                                             %
   480   (381)  %                  S T A N D A R D   V A L U E S                              %
   481   (382)  %                                                                             %
   482   (383)  %             These are returned by the PLANC XMSG library                    %
   483   (384)  %             when the routine involves an XROUT service.                     %
   484   (385)  %                                                                             %
   485   (386)  %                 base value = XRXXX = 16960 = 41100B                         %
   486   (387)  %                                                                             %
   487   (388)  %-----------------------------------------------------------------------------%
   488   (389)  
   489   (390)  CONSTANT XMXRXXX= 16960      % BASE for STANDARD ERROR system
   490   (391)  
   491   (392)  CONSTANT XMXRISN= 16961      % Illegal service number
   492   (393)  CONSTANT XMXRUNN= 16962      % Unknown name (of server or system)
   493   (394)  CONSTANT XMXRDDF= 16963      % Another port already has this name
   494   (395)  CONSTANT XMXRNSP= 16964      % No space left for names
   495   (396)  CONSTANT XMXRIPT= 16965      % Illegal parameter type
   496   (397)  CONSTANT XMXRMMP= 16966      % Missing mandatory parameter
   497   (398)  CONSTANT XMXRUNM= 16967      % Unknown magic number
   498   (399)  CONSTANT XMXRMTL= 16968      % Too short message (or message buffer)
   499   (400)  CONSTANT XMXRSMF= 16969      % Standard message format not handled
   500   (401)  CONSTANT XMXRPRV= 16970      % Caller was not privileged
   501   (402)  CONSTANT XMXRISY= 16971      % Illegal system number parameter
   502   (403)  CONSTANT XMXRNRO= 16972      % No access to remote system
   503   (404)  CONSTANT XMXRIIV= 16973      % Illegal integer value
   504   (405)  CONSTANT XMXRNEI= 16974      % Cannot define route to a neighbour
   505   (406)  CONSTANT XMXRNXM= 16975      % Invalid service request - no intersystem XMSG
   506   (407)  CONSTANT XMXRILN= 16976      % Illegal/Reserved Log. unit no. for link
   507   (408)  CONSTANT XMXRNXL= 16977      % No more XL-Blocks (Link Descriptors)
   508   (409)  CONSTANT XMXRNXD= 16978      % Not enough XD-Blocks for LKINI
   509   (410)  CONSTANT XMXRNTR= 16979      % No trace generated
   510   (411)  CONSTANT XMXRTRA= 16980      % Trace already active
   511   (412)  CONSTANT XMXRTRP= 16981      % Trace passive
   512   (413)  CONSTANT XMXRTFE= 16982      % Trace/dump file open error (see param 1)
   513   (414)  CONSTANT XMXRTRT= 16983      % Trace RT-prog (XTRACE) not found
   514   (415)  CONSTANT XMXRTIS= 16984      % Illegal trace system number
   515   (416)  CONSTANT XMXRBLK= 16985      % Bad link - open unsuccessful
   516   (417)  CONSTANT XMXRSYD= 16986      % Local system number already defined
   517   (418)  CONSTANT XMXRNLS= 16987      % No local system number defined yet
   518   (419)  CONSTANT XMXRTRE= 16988      % Too many remote names to this system
   519   (420)  CONSTANT XMXRRNA= 16989      % Old service calls (<64) cannot go remote
   520   (421)  CONSTANT XMXRBUS= 16990      % Service busy - try later!
   521   (422)  CONSTANT XMXRNSE= 16991      % This is not a service port
   522   (423)  CONSTANT XMXRRPN= 16992      % Remote port statically declared
   523   (424)  CONSTANT XMXRUKS= 16993      % Unknown remote system name
   524   (425)  CONSTANT XMXRMFL= 16994      % Not enough message table space in remote system
   525   (426)  CONSTANT XMXRROV= 16995      % Remote task space overflow when forwarding
   526   (427)  CONSTANT XMXRRFU= 16996      % Routing table space full (too many systems def.)
   527   (428)  CONSTANT XMXRNRB= 16997      % No remote batch service available here
   528   (429)  CONSTANT XMXRURT= 16998      % Unknown RT-program name
   529   (430)  CONSTANT XMXRSNR= 16999      % this server is not running
   530   (431)  CONSTANT XMXRRND= 17000      % Netserver: remote system is not defined
   531   (432)  CONSTANT XMXRNNA= 17001      % Netserver: network not available
   532   (433)  CONSTANT XMXRISE= 17002      % Netserver: internal server error
   533   (434)  CONSTANT XMXRIRQ= 17003      % Netserver: invalid request
   534   (435)  CONSTANT XMXRNGA= 17004      % XMSG not configured with gateway code
   535   (436)  CONSTANT XMXRRNL= 17005      % remote system not on same LAN
   536   (437)  CONSTANT XMXRNCO= 17006      % no connection to this system(unknown status)
   537   (438)  CONSTANT XMXRAMB= 17007      % ambiguous name
   538   (439)  CONSTANT XMXRFFU= 17008      % friend system table full (too many friends)
   539   (440)  
   540   (441)  %-----------------------------------------------------------------------------%
   541   (442)  %                                                                             %
   542   (443)  %                    format of XROUT messages                                 %
   543   (444)  %                                                                             %
   544   (445)  %-----------------------------------------------------------------------------%
   545   (446)  
   546   (447)  %
   547   (448)  % maximum XMSG name length
   548   (449)  %
   549   (450)  CONSTANT XMMAXNameLength = 32          % actually installation-dependent!
   550   (451)  
   551   (452)  
   552   (453)                                         % XROUT header format
   553   (454)  TYPE XROUTHDR = RECORD PACK            % base record for variants
   554   (455)         BYTE: XRSerialNumber            % serial number for user's use
   555   (456)         BYTE: XRServiceNumber           % code for required service which is
   556   (457)         BYTE: XRStatus = XRServiceNumber% replaced with XROUT status code
   557   (458)         INTEGER2: XRBufferLength = XRServiceNumber % overlay for append routines
   558   (459)         INTEGER2: XRLengthMsg           % total length of following parameters
   559   (460)  ENDRECORD
   560   (461)                                         % parameter formats
   561   (462)  TYPE XROUTPARAMHDR = RECORD PACK       % base record for parameter variants
   562   (463)         BYTE: XRPCode                   % parameter code
   563   (464)         BYTE: XRPLength                 % parameter length
   564   (465)  ENDRECORD
   565   (466)  TYPE XROUTSTRING = XROUTPARAMHDR RECORD PACKED % string parameter format
   566   (467)         BYTES: XRPString(0:XMMAXNameLength-1)   % MAXIMUM length shown
   567   (468)  ENDRECORD
   568   (469)  TYPE XROUTINTEGER = XROUTPARAMHDR RECORD PACKED % integer parameter format
   569   (470)         INTEGER2: XRPInteger
   570   (471)  ENDRECORD
   571   (472)  TYPE XROUTDOUBLE = XROUTPARAMHDR RECORD PACKED % integer4 parameter format
   572   (473)         INTEGER4: XRPDouble
   573   (474)  ENDRECORD
   574   (475)  
   575   (476)  %-----------------------------------------------------------------------------%
   576   (477)  $EOF

   761   (101)  
   762   (102)  % ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   763   (103)  % FILE MONITOR CALLS, for the log. Same three CHAT.PLNC uses for CHAT:CNFG,
   764   (104)  % declared the same way, because that pair has been working on D100 since
   765   (105)  % 2026-08-19 and a second spelling of a proven call is a second thing to get
   766   (106)  % wrong.
   767   (107)  %
   768   (108)  %   MON50  OpenFile   name, type, access -> device number. RAISES ROUTINEERROR
   769   (109)  %                     on failure, and leaves 46 in the device number when the
   770   (110)  %                     file is not there - NOT zero. Never test the number.
   771   (111)  %   MON2   OutByte    the byte is the IN-VALUE: "b MON2(dev)".
   772   (112)  %   MON43  CloseFile  and it is 43, not 54 - 54 is DELETE.
   773   (113)  %
   774   (114)  % They come from MON-CALL-1B-A00, which this program already links for
   775   (115)  % MONITOR_CALL - see the LOAD line in CHATSV:MODE.
   776   (116)  % ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   777   (117)  IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES, BYTES, INTEGER) : MON50 )
   778   (118)  IMPORT ( ROUTINE BYTE, VOID (INTEGER) : MON2 )
   779   (119)  IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER) : MON43 )
   780   (120)  
   781   (121)  % MN113 CLOCK - seven elements, and it is the ND's OWN date and time.
   782   (122)  % 0 basic units, 1 sec, 2 min, 3 hour, 4 day, 5 month, 6 year - and the
   783   (123)  % year is the FULL 1998, not 98. Nothing here converts it to an epoch or
   784   (124)  % to text: the words are stored as the machine gives them and are read
   785   (125)  % back the same way, which is Ronny's call and also the only form that
   786   (126)  % cannot drift with somebody's idea of a date format.
   787   (127)  IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER ARRAY WRITE) : MN113 )
   788   (128)  
   789   (129)  % MON11 TIME - basic time units, a fiftieth of a second each, as an
   790   (130)  % INTEGER4. Signature from ND-60.117.5 page 13445: ROUTINE VOID,INTEGER4.
   791   (131)  % THE TICK HAS TO COME OFF THE CLOCK once the sleep can be cut short by a
   792   (132)  % wake-up - counting sleeps only measures seconds while every sleep runs
   793   (133)  % its full length. See the main loop.
   794   (134)  IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER4 : MON11 )
   795   (135)  
   796   (136)  % ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   797   (137)  % RANDOM BLOCK ACCESS, for the per-room history ring.
   798   (138)  %
   799   (139)  %   MN117  ReadFromFile  (RFILE 117B)
   800   (140)  %   MN120  WriteToFile   (WFILE 120B)
   801   (141)  %
   802   (142)  %     (dev, zero, dadr, bl, words)
   803   (143)  %      dev    the device number MON50 gave back
   804   (144)  %      zero   the WAIT flag: 0 suspends until the transfer is done, which is
   805   (145)  %             what we want - anything else means checking with AwaitFileTransfer
   806   (146)  %      dadr   the buffer, an INTEGER ARRAY. WORDS, not bytes.
   807   (147)  %      bl     the block to start at. The FIRST BLOCK IS 0, and -1 means "the
   808   (148)  %             next one".
   809   (149)  %      words  how many words to move.
   810   (150)  %
   811   (151)  % THE NAME LOSES ITS "O" ABOVE 76, and this is not a typo. Asked the library
   812   (152)  % itself rather than guessed:
   813   (153)  %
   814   (154)  %   Brl: LIST-BRF-ENTRIES PLANC-1BANK-F00,,
   815   (155)  %        ... MON73 MON74 MON75 MON76  MN104 MN113 MN114 MN117 MN120 MN122 ...
   816   (156)  %
   817   (157)  % "MON104" would be six characters and an ND symbol is five, so the O goes.
   818   (158)  % The PLANC manual's appendix looks self-contradictory for the same reason -
   819   (159)  % its HEADING says "MON117" and the routine it declares is "MN117". The
   820   (160)  % routine name is the one to import. One command settled it; the alternative
   821   (161)  % was a guessed name and a four-minute compile to find out.
   822   (162)  %
   823   (163)  % WHY RANDOM AND NOT THE SEQUENTIAL PAIR ABOVE: MON1/MON2 move one byte and
   824   (164)  % cannot overwrite in place, so a ring built on them means rewriting the whole
   825   (165)  % file - on every message, or on every N with a compaction step. One message
   826   (166)  % per 512-byte block makes an append a SINGLE MN120 at block (id MOD N): no
   827   (167)  % read-modify-write, no compaction, and a file whose size is fixed the day the
   828   (168)  % room is created.
   829   (169)  % ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   830   (170)  IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER, INTEGER, INTEGER ARRAY, INTEGER, &
   831   (171)      INTEGER) : MN117 )
   832   (172)  IMPORT ( ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER, INTEGER, INTEGER ARRAY, INTEGER, &
   833   (173)      INTEGER) : MN120 )
   834   (174)  
   835   (175)      % ----- message kinds, mirroring ChatMessageKind.cs ----------------------
   836   (176)      % ---- THE MESSAGE KINDS COME FROM CHATLIB -------------------------
   837   (177)      %
   838   (178)      % They used to be declared here AND in the other program - twenty of
   839   (179)      % them, agreeing only because somebody kept checking. Nothing made
   840   (180)      % them agree, and an unhandled kind is SILENT, which is the worst
   841   (181)      % failure this product has. Imported from one place, a drift is a
   842   (182)      % LINK error instead, and LIST-ENTRIES-UNDEFINED already runs in every
   843   (183)      % link.
   844   (184)      %
   845   (185)      % TWO NAMES CHANGED because they could not cross a BRF boundary -
   846   (186)      % names are unique in SEVEN characters there, so kRename/kRenamed both
   847   (187)      % read KRENAME and kDirect/kDirected both read KDIRECT. kRenamed is now
   848   (188)      % kRenOk and kDirected is now kDirGot. The WIRE NUMBERS are unchanged.
   849   (189)      IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES, INTEGER, BYTES, INTEGER, BYTES, &
   850   (190)              INTEGER, INTEGER, INTEGER) : cmEncAt)
   851   (191)  
   852   (192)      % THE DECODERS, so the header is read in ONE place rather than parsed by
   853   (193)      % hand at each arrival. See the same note in CHAT.PLNC.
   854   (194)      %
   855   (195)      % THESE ARE FOR THE CHAT HEADER ONLY. The server also parses a TRUNK RELAY
   856   (196)      % header (origin, hops, relay id) and an XROUT LETTER header (service byte,
   857   (197)      % payload offset), and those are DIFFERENT FORMATS that happen to be read
   858   (198)      % out of the same buffer. Do not reach for cmNmLen there - inBuf(1) means
   859   (199)      % something else in each of them.
   860   (200)      IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES) : cmKind)
   861   (201)      IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES) : cmNmLen)
   862   (202)      IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES) : cmTxAt)
   863   (203)      IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES) : cmTxLen)
   864   (204)  
   865   (205)      % THE RELAY HEADER. trHLen answers how many bytes a kind carries - 0 for
   866   (206)      % anything that is not relayed - so the receive loop asks it about every
   867   (207)      % message and needs no special case. trStrip takes the header off and
   868   (208)      % leaves an ordinary chat frame behind, which is why cmNmLen is correct
   869   (209)      % AFTER it and would be nonsense before.
   870   (210)      IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (INTEGER) : trHLen)
   871   (211)      IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES) : trOrig)
   872   (212)      IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES) : trHops)
   873   (213)      IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES) : trId)
   874   (214)      IMPORT (ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES, INTEGER, INTEGER) : trStrip)
   875   (215)  
   876   (216)      % THE TRUNK KINDS. Declared in CHATLIB so the relay codec and this
   877   (217)      % server cannot disagree about a number that crosses a wire between
   878   (218)      % MACHINES - the far end may be running an older build.
   879   (219)      %
   880   (220)      % THEY MUST BE HERE, not beside the comments that explain them further
   881   (221)      % down: PLANC answers MISPLACED STATEMENT for an IMPORT that follows an
   882   (222)      % ordinary declaration, and every IMPORT belongs in this one block.
   883   (223)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kTrkHello)
   884   (224)      IMPORT (INTEGER : trkAsk)
   885   (225)      IMPORT (INTEGER : trkAnswer)
   886   (226)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kTrkWho)
   887   (227)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kTrkMbrs)
   888   (228)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kTrkSaid)
   889   (229)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kTrkRly)
   890   (230)      IMPORT (INTEGER : maxHops)
   891   (231)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kTrkRlId)
   892   (232)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kTrkDir)
   893   (233)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kTrkDBad)
   894   (234)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kJoin)
   895   (235)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kWelcome)
   896   (236)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kReject)
   897   (237)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kSay)
   898   (238)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kSaid)
   899   (239)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kLeave)
   900   (240)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kJoined)
   901   (241)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kLeft)
   902   (242)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kRename)
   903   (243)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kRenOk)
   904   (244)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kWho)
   905   (245)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kMap)
   906   (246)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kRooms)
   907   (247)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kTopic)
   908   (248)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kAllWho)

   913   (253)      IMPORT (INTEGER : kDirBad)
   914   (254)  
   915   (255)  
   916   (256)      % ONE KIND, BOTH DIRECTIONS: the member asks with an empty text, the room
   917   (257)      % answers with the same kind and the names in the text. See buildWho.
   918   (258)  
   919   (259)      % ALSO ONE KIND BOTH DIRECTIONS, same shape as kWho.
   920   (260)      %
   921   (261)      % kTopic  ask: empty text.  set: the new topic in the text.
   922   (262)      %         answer: the ROOM NAME in the name field, the topic in the text.
   923   (263)      %         A SET is broadcast to the room; an ASK goes to the asker alone.
   924   (264)      % kRooms  ask: empty text. answer: "NAME(count)" per room, space separated.
   925   (265)      %
   926   (266)      % Room kinds live in 1..31 and admin starts at 32 - see the note there.
   927   (267)      % These MUST match DOC/protocols/chat-wire.json and ChatMessageKind.cs, and
   928   (268)      % all three move in the same commit.
   929   (269)      % 12 IS NOT FREE - it is Map, reserved for /map (task #77). The registry
   930   (270)      % had already allocated all three when this was written, and taking 12 for
   931   (271)      % the topic would have put /topic and /map on the same number. Read
   932   (272)      % DOC/protocols/chat-wire.json BEFORE picking a kind, not after.
   933   (273)      % /map - the machines and their trunks, for an ordinary member. A ROOM kind
   934   (274)      % and not an admin one on purpose: SEEING how the network is wired is not
   935   (275)      % the same right as REWIRING it. Reserved here since 2026-08-20; built
   936   (276)      % 2026-08-22. See buildMap.
   937   (277)  
   938   (278)  
   939   (279)      % THE ANSWER TO /who WHEN THE ASKER IS IN NO ROOM.
   940   (280)      %
   941   (281)      % Ronny, 2026-08-22: "running /who when not in a room should list all rooms
   942   (282)      % and all people in the rooms." A person who has left, or who was refused a
   943   (283)      % nickname, has no seat and therefore no room to filter by - and the useful
   944   (284)      % answer to them is the whole house, not silence.
   945   (285)      %
   946   (286)      % A SEPARATE KIND, NOT kWho, because the client LABELS a kWho answer with
   947   (287)      % the room it thinks it is in - "in (LOBBY): ..." - and putting every room's
   948   (288)      % people under that label would print something FALSE, which is the exact
   949   (289)      % mistake /who itself made until 2026-08-22 (see buildWho). A different kind
   950   (290)      % costs one number and makes the label impossible to get wrong.
   951   (291)      %
   952   (292)      % ONE DIRECTION ONLY, unlike its neighbours: the client never sends 15. It
   953   (293)      % sends kWho and the SERVER decides which answer fits, because the server is
   954   (294)      % the one that knows whether the asker has a seat.
   955   (295)  
   956   (296)      % REPLAYED HISTORY - one of these per remembered message, sent to a joiner
   957   (297)      % before the room hears they arrived.
   958   (298)      %
   959   (299)      % A DISTINCT KIND AND NOT kSaid, decided 2026-08-22 and written into the
   960   (300)      % registry with the reason. Replaying kSaid would be less code and the
   961   (301)      % client already renders it - but then a client CANNOT TELL history from
   962   (302)      % live traffic, and the difference matters the moment it timestamps, beeps
   963   (303)      % or counts unread. Taking the cheap option here would be choosing to make
   964   (304)      % those features wrong later.
   965   (305)      %
   966   (306)      % Same shape as kSaid otherwise: the speaker in the name field, what they
   967   (307)      % said in the text.
   968   (308)  
   969   (309)      % ----- DIRECT MESSAGES, one person to one person -----------------------
   970   (310)      %
   971   (311)      % These four are 17..20, inside the ROOM block (1..31), because a direct
   972   (312)      % message is ordinary member traffic on the service port - it configures
   973   (313)      % nothing, so it is not an admin kind.
   974   (314)      %
   975   (315)      % kDirect   client to server. The TARGET rides in the TEXT before a '/',
   976   (316)      %           exactly as kSay's room does on a trunk - one layout for the
   977   (317)      %           whole family rather than a special case per direction. The
   978   (318)      %           target is either a bare alias (RONNY) or one qualified by
   979   (319)      %           machine (D102!RONNY).
   980   (320)      % kDirGot server to the TARGET. The name field holds the sender
   981   (321)      %           QUALIFIED - D102!KARI, never a bare KARI - because what is
   982   (322)      %           shown has to be what can be typed back, and a bare name is
   983   (323)      %           ambiguous the moment two machines each hold one. The text is
   984   (324)      %           the message alone: the target is whoever is reading it.
   985   (325)      % kDirSent  server to the SENDER, naming who it actually went to. Sent on
   986   (326)      %           EVERY delivery and not only on ambiguous ones - refusing an
   987   (327)      %           ambiguous alias protects the sender when the collision is
   988   (328)      %           visible, this protects them when it is not, and it costs one
   989   (329)      %           line to make a wrong delivery visible at once.
   990   (330)      % kDirBad   server to the SENDER: not delivered, and why. The name field
   991   (331)      %           holds the target that was tried, the text the reason - and for
   992   (332)      %           an ambiguous alias the CANDIDATES, so they can be retyped
   993   (333)      %           straight off the screen.
   994   (334)      %
   995   (335)      % THERE IS DELIBERATELY NO STORE AND FORWARD. "not logged in" is a refusal,
   996   (336)      % not a queue. A message that waits for somebody is a mailbox - a different
   997   (337)      % product with different rules, and SINTRAN already has mail for that.
   998   (338)  
   999   (339)      % ADMIN kinds - 32 and up, and they belong on the command port. These MUST
  1000   (340)      % match DOC/protocols/chat-wire.json and ChatMessageKind.cs; all three move
  1001   (341)      % in the same commit, which is constraint 5 of the federation design.
  1002   (342)      INTEGER : kAdmStat := 32
  1003   (343)      INTEGER : kAdmRepl := 33
  1004   (344)      INTEGER : kAdmStop := 34
  1005   (345)  
  1006   (346)      % Trunk configuration, from CHAT-MON on the command port. These CONFIGURE the
  1007   (347)      % network, which is why they are admin kinds and not room kinds - seeing how
  1008   (348)      % the network is wired (/map, kind 12) is not the same right as rewiring it.
  1009   (349)      % All three answer with kAdmRepl, so CHAT-MON needs no new decoding.
  1010   (350)      INTEGER : kAdmTrunk   := 35
  1011   (351)      INTEGER : kAdmUntrnk  := 36
  1012   (352)  
  1013   (353)      % INITIALIZE <room> <n> - how many messages a room remembers.
  1014   (354)      %
  1015   (355)      % An ADMIN kind because it configures the server, and it is the one thing
  1016   (356)      % the history design asked for that the ring itself did not provide: "a
  1017   (357)      % CONSTANT, default around 50 messages per room, CONFIGURABLE PER ROOM ...
  1018   (358)      % not a hard-coded 50 buried in the source".
  1019   (359)      INTEGER : kAdmInit    := 38
  1020   (360)  
  1021   (361)      % SET-NAME <name> from CHAT-MON. What this machine calls itself.
  1022   (362)      %
  1023   (363)      % An ADMIN kind because it configures the server. Answered with kAdmRepl
  1024   (364)      % like every other admin verb, so CHAT-MON needs no new decoding.
  1025   (365)      INTEGER : kAdmName    := 39
  1026   (366)      INTEGER : kAdmTrunks  := 37
  1027   (367)  
  1028   (368)      % LOCK-PEERS and OPEN-PEERS from CHAT-MON.
  1029   (369)      %
  1030   (370)      % A server learns a peer from any system that reaches its trunk port, which
  1031   (371)      % is what makes a lab easy to set up and is the default. It also means the
  1032   (372)      % server believes whatever that system says about who is speaking on it - a
  1033   (373)      % trunk line is delivered as NAME@thatsystem - so an operator needs a way to
  1034   (374)      % say "these are the machines, take no more".
  1035   (375)      %
  1036   (376)      % TWO KINDS RATHER THAN ONE WITH AN ARGUMENT. Every other admin verb here is
  1037   (377)      % its own number, and a verb whose meaning depends on parsing its text is
  1038   (378)      % the one that gets typed wrong at three in the morning.
  1039   (379)      INTEGER : kAdmLock    := 40
  1040   (380)      INTEGER : kAdmOpen    := 41
  1041   (381)  
  1042   (382)      % DROP-MEMBER <name> - RELEASE A SEAT AND TELL NOBODY.
  1043   (383)      %
  1044   (384)      % THIS EXISTS TO TEST kTrkDBad, and it is the only way to reach that code
  1045   (385)      % path on purpose. kTrkDBad fires when THIS machine is asked to deliver a
  1046   (386)      % private message to somebody a PEER still believes is seated here. Every
  1047   (387)      % ordinary way of ending a session keeps the two machines in step, which is
  1048   (388)      % exactly right for the product and leaves the refusal unreachable by hand:
  1049   (389)      %
  1050   (390)      %   /quit          sends kLeave, which broadcasts kLeft and calls tellMbrs
  1051   (391)      %   /nick          crosses the trunk, so the peer follows the new name
  1052   (392)      %   an RT-load     empties the table, but the orphaned client REJOINS
  1053   (393)      %   a peer restart makes the other side purge what it knew
  1054   (394)      %
  1055   (395)      % MEASURED 2026-08-30: all four were tried against D100/D102 and every one
  1056   (396)      % of them self-healed, so all four refusals came back as the LOCAL one
  1057   (397)      % ("nobody of that name is here now") and the trunk path never ran. The
  1058   (398)      % reason string is the only thing that tells the two apart.
  1059   (399)      %
  1060   (400)      % So this verb frees the seat and does NOTHING ELSE - no kLeft to the room,
  1061   (401)      % no tellMbrs to the peers. That is precisely the disagreement kTrkDBad was
  1062   (402)      % written for, and it is a state the machine can otherwise only reach by
  1063   (403)      % losing a race.
  1064   (404)      INTEGER : kAdmDrop    := 42
  1065   (405)  
  1066   (406)      % LIST-MEMBER - who is seated here right now, and which room. The other
  1067   (407)      % half of the DROP-MEMBER pair Ronny asked for: DROP-MEMBER acts on a seat,
  1068   (408)      % LIST-MEMBER shows them. Answered with kAdmRepl, same as LIST-TRUNKS.
  1069   (409)      INTEGER : kAdmMbrs    := 43
  1070   (410)  
  1071   (411)      % SERVER TO SERVER, on the trunk port. 48 and up, left clear of the admin
  1072   (412)      % block so a future admin verb cannot collide with a trunk one.
  1073   (413)      %
  1074   (414)      % ONE KIND, BOTH DIRECTIONS, and the TEXT'S FIRST BYTE says which way: 0 is
  1075   (415)      % "I am here, are you?" and 1 is "I am here, and I am answering you". Without
  1076   (416)      % that byte two servers answering each other's HELLO would say hello for
  1077   (417)      % ever. An ANSWER is never answered.
  1078   (418)      % (imported at the top of the module - see the IMPORT block)
  1079   (419)      % (imported at the top of the module - see the IMPORT block)
  1080   (420)      % (imported at the top of the module - see the IMPORT block)
  1081   (421)  
  1082   (422)      % Phase C - "tell me who is on you" and the answer.
  1083   (423)      %
  1084   (424)      % TWO KINDS, NOT ONE, unlike the hello. The hello could share a kind because
  1085   (425)      % its two directions carry the same thing; these do not - the ask is empty
  1086   (426)      % and the answer is a list - so a receiver would have to look at the length
  1087   (427)      % to know which it had. A kind per direction is cheaper to read and cannot
  1088   (428)      % be got wrong.
  1089   (429)      %
  1090   (430)      % THE ANSWER'S TEXT is "NAME/ROOM NAME/ROOM ...", space between people and a
  1091   (431)      % slash between a person and their room. Readable on a trace, and it needs
  1092   (432)      % no length table - the same shape /who already sends to a client.
  1093   (433)      % (imported at the top of the module - see the IMPORT block)
  1094   (434)      % (imported at the top of the module - see the IMPORT block)
  1095   (435)  
  1096   (436)      % Phase D - somebody spoke on another machine.
  1097   (437)      %
  1098   (438)      % NAME field = the speaker, unqualified, as their own machine knows them.
  1099   (439)      % TEXT = "ROOM/what they said". The receiving machine adds the "@Dnnn",
  1100   (440)      % because only IT knows which system the letter came from - taken from the
  1101   (441)      % magic, not from anything the sender claimed. A speaker cannot forge the
  1102   (442)      % machine they are on.
  1103   (443)      %
  1104   (444)      % NOT RELAYED ONWARD. A message received on a trunk is delivered to local
  1105   (445)      % members and stops. With two machines that is complete; with three it will
  1106   (446)      % need a hop count and an origin, which is the rest of phase D and is not
  1107   (447)      % built. Forwarding blindly now would loop the moment a third machine
  1108   (448)      % appeared.
  1109   (449)      % (imported at the top of the module - see the IMPORT block)
  1110   (450)      % A line being RELAYED onward. Same shape as kTrkSaid with three bytes in
  1111   (451)      % front: the origin system, big-endian, then how many hops it may still
  1112   (452)      % take. A SEPARATE kind rather than extra fields on kTrkSaid so a server
  1113   (453)      % that has not been upgraded keeps handling its direct trunks unchanged -
  1114   (454)      % these machines are upgraded one at a time.
  1115   (455)      % (imported at the top of the module - see the IMPORT block)
  1116   (456)      % How far a line may travel from the machine it was typed on. Three is
  1117   (457)      % ample for D100/D102/D103 and bounds the damage if a future mesh ever
  1118   (458)      % loops: the count is what makes the loop terminate, not the topology.
  1119   (459)      % (imported at the top of the module - see the IMPORT block)
  1120   (460)      % A relayed line that also carries the number its ORIGIN gave it. Same
  1121   (461)      % shape as kTrkRly with two more bytes after the hop count.
  1122   (462)      %
  1123   (463)      % THE ID MUST COME FROM THE ORIGIN. It cannot be handed out by a relay: a
  1124   (464)      % machine with two peers sends one line to both, each would stamp its own
  1125   (465)      % number, and a machine further out would see two names for one line and
  1126   (466)      % show it twice - which is the very thing this exists to stop.
  1127   (467)      %
  1128   (468)      % A SEPARATE kind rather than a longer kTrkRly header, for the same
  1129   (469)      % reason kTrkRly is separate from kTrkSaid: a server that has not been
  1130   (470)      % upgraded would read the two extra bytes as a name length and a name and
  1131   (471)      % show its users rubbish. A kind it does not know it just ignores.
  1132   (472)      % (imported at the top of the module - see the IMPORT block)
  1133   (473)  
  1134   (474)      % A DIRECT MESSAGE CROSSING A TRUNK. Same six-byte header as kTrkRlId -
  1135   (475)      % origin system, hops remaining, line id - so it is relayed and de-duplicated
  1136   (476)      % by the machinery that already exists rather than by a second mechanism.
  1137   (477)      %
  1138   (478)      % The difference is what the text holds: kTrkRlId packs ROOM/message, this
  1139   (479)      % packs TARGET/message, which leaves the name field free for the SENDER -
  1140   (480)      % the one thing the far end has to display.
  1141   (481)      % (imported at the top of the module - see the IMPORT block)
  1142   (482)      % How many recent lines we remember in order to spot a repeat. A second
  1143   (483)      % copy arrives within a hop or two of the first, so a short window is
  1144   (484)      % enough, and a FIXED one cannot be made to grow by a peer sending us
  1145   (485)      % traffic.
  1146   (486)      INTEGER : maxSeen     := 64
  1147   (487)  
  1148   (488)      % Set by an AdminStop. Checked at the bottom of the loop rather than acted
  1149   (489)      % on where it arrives, so the reply path and the reaping finish first - a
  1150   (490)      % stop that abandons a half-sent message is not a clean stop.
  1151   (491)      BOOLEAN : stopWanted := FALSE
  1152   (492)  
  1153   (493)      % THERE IS NO ELEVENTH KIND, and the reason is worth keeping.
  1154   (494)      %
  1155   (495)      % A kHello was invented here on 2026-08-18 to answer the connect on its own,
  1156   (496)      % on the belief that a connection port is handed "4 bytes with byte 0 = 0"
  1157   (497)      % and that the joiner's nickname could only follow as a second, ordinary
  1158   (498)      % message. That belief was wrong, and it was wrong because this program was
  1159   (499)      % reading the WRONG BYTES: the connect arrives with the XSLET letter header
  1160   (500)      % still in front of it, and byte 0 of that header is the serial number.
  1161   (501)      %
  1162   (502)      % The nickname was in the very first arrival all along. See the header skip
  1163   (503)      % in the receive loop for the layout and the manual reference. The join is
  1164   (504)      % ONE step, exactly as the Programmer Guide describes it: the letter carries
  1165   (505)      % the user data, the server answers it, and "thereby gives away its own
  1166   (506)      % magic number" - the welcome IS the answer, so no extra kind is needed.
  1167   (507)  
  1168   (508)      % XFWTF is the BIT POSITION (15). The flags word is 2 to that power - the
  1169   (509)      % sample programs in Appendix G both write "2**XFWTF =: FLAGS". Passing 15
  1170   (510)      % itself would set bits 0 to 3, four unrelated options, and the wait would
  1171   (511)      % not happen at all.
  1172   (512)      % XFWTF COMES FROM XMP-B02:DEFS - do NOT declare it here. Declaring our own
  1173   (513)      % drew IDENTIFIER ALREADY SPECIFIED/DECLARED "XFWTF", and the compiler then
  1174   (514)      % fell over with ASSERT VIOLATION AT 136747B and took the batch job with it.
  1175   (515)      % A redeclaration is not a warning you can carry on past in this compiler.
  1176   (516)      % ZERO, and it stays zero - see the PROGRAM body for the measurement. Flags
  1177   (517)      % zero is XMSG's own no-wait: XFRCV returns at once whether or not a message
  1178   (518)      % was there, which is the only way one loop can poll TWO ports.
  1179   (519)      INTEGER : waitFlags := 0
  1180   (520)  
  1181   (521)      % 2**XFSEC, computed at start. SECURE, and the reason is a measurement.
  1182   (522)      %
  1183   (523)      % These sends used to carry flags ZERO, and the guide is explicit about what
  1184   (524)      % that costs: "Non-secure messages are discarded and released by XMSG if they
  1185   (525)      % cannot be delivered." So a member whose terminal died was written to for
  1186   (526)      % ever, in silence.
  1187   (527)      %
  1188   (528)      % THE OBVIOUS ALTERNATIVE WAS TRIED FIRST AND IS REFUTED. XMPSEND is handed a
  1189   (529)      % magic number, and a magic carries a generation, so it looked as though XMSG
  1190   (530)      % would refuse one naming a closed port on the spot - XMXEIMA, "Invalid magic
  1191   (531)      % number". MEASURED 2026-08-18 on D100: two clients in the room, one killed
  1192   (532)      % with ESC ("USER BREAK AT 17202B"), the other spoke. The broadcast to the
  1193   (533)      % dead port returned XMOK. Not one status printed, the seat stayed spent, and
  1194   (534)      % LIST-NAMES held at 14. XMPSEND DOES NOT VALIDATE THE DESTINATION.
  1195   (535)      %
  1196   (536)      % So the return has to come back as a MESSAGE, which is what XFSEC buys:
  1197   (537)      % "The message will be returned to the sending port if it cannot be
  1198   (538)      % delivered, or if the receiving port is closed (e.g., if the receiving task
  1199   (539)      % terminates)". It arrives as msgType XMTRE and is handled in the receive
  1200   (540)      % loop.
  1201   (541)      %
  1202   (542)      % MEASURED WITH THE FLAG SET, same two clients, same kill: the bounce came
  1203   (543)      % back carrying reason 16915 - which is XMXEIMA, the very code the refuted
  1204   (544)      % attempt was watching for. It was never the send's status; it is the reason
  1205   (545)      % on the return. CHAT-LOBBY went 16 free, 14 with both joined, back to 15
  1206   (546)      % once the dead one was reaped.
  1207   (547)      INTEGER : sendFlags := 0
  1208   (548)  
  1209   (549)      % ----- the room ---------------------------------------------------------
  1210   (550)      % maxSeats is what XMPOPCN is given, so XROUT turns the SEVENTEENTH joiner
  1211   (551)      % away before this program sees them. The array bounds and that argument are
  1212   (552)      % the same number on purpose.
  1213   (553)      %
  1214   (554)      % THE CLIENT DOES NOT KNOW THIS NUMBER AND MUST NOT. Neither CHAT.PLNC nor
  1215   (555)      % ChatClient.cs mentions it anywhere - XROUT does the admission itself, before
  1216   (556)      % this program is woken, so a client that is turned away simply gets a refusal
  1217   (557)      % it has to handle in any case. The room's size is the room's business, and
  1218   (558)      % raising it is this file alone. The C# ChatRoom has no seat limit at all.
  1219   (559)      %
  1220   (560)      % Raised from 8 to 16 on 2026-08-18. Nothing else has to move with it: the
  1221   (561)      % four arrays below and every FOR loop are written in terms of maxSeats, and
  1222   (562)      % the only literal bounds are the array declarations, which are next to it.
  1223   (563)      INTEGER : maxSeats := 16
  1224   (564)      INTEGER : nameLen  := 16
  1225   (565)  
  1226   (566)      INTEGER4 ARRAY : memberMagic(1:16)
  1227   (567)  
  1228   (568)      % A two-dimensional BYTES array: sixteen names of sixteen bytes. Legal PLANC -
  1229   (569)      % the reference gives BYTES ARRAY : b1(1:2,0:3) directly.
  1230   (570)      %
  1231   (571)      % Note that memberName(slot) with the LAST subscript omitted refers to the
  1232   (572)      % WHOLE string, which is tempting for copying a name in one statement. It is
  1233   (573)      % NOT used here, because a name is variable length and a whole-row copy would
  1234   (574)      % carry the sixteen bytes including whatever follows the name. The explicit
  1235   (575)      % loops are longer and right.
  1236   (576)      % SIXTEEN BYTES PER NAME, and the number is named because the wire decides
  1237   (577)      % how many bytes arrive. A nickname length is one byte, so a peer can ask for
  1238   (578)      % up to 255 - and PLANC checks no array bound. Copying an unchecked length in
  1239   (579)      % here writes straight through the other seven members' names and out the far
  1240   (580)      % side of the array. See the guard at the top of handleMessage.
  1241   (581)      INTEGER        : maxNameLen := 16
  1242   (582)  
  1243   (583)      % LAYOUT OF A LISTING, and both of these are named rather than typed.
  1244   (584)      %
  1245   (585)      % idWide is the column the machine NUMBER sits in - six, which holds the
  1246   (586)      % five digits of 19999 and a space after it. The NAME column is not here:
  1247   (587)      % it is maxNameLen above, asked of the buffer the names live in, so the two
  1248   (588)      % cannot drift apart.
  1249   (589)      INTEGER        : idWide := 6
  1250   (590)  
  1251   (591)      % THE LINE BREAK INSIDE A REPLY. A listing travels as ONE message and
  1252   (592)      % CHAT-MON prints it byte by byte, so a break between elements has to be a
  1253   (593)      % byte the printer knows: LF, which showAnswer turns into a CRLF. 10 is
  1254   (594)      % written once, here, instead of appearing as a bare number in the middle
  1255   (595)      % of a builder.
  1256   (596)      INTEGER        : lineBreak := 10
  1257   (597)      % NAMES ARE UNIQUE IN THEIR FIRST TEN CHARACTERS, and that is not a style
  1258   (598)      % note - it is what the compiler enforces. memberName and memberNameLen both
  1259   (599)      % start MEMBERNAME, so the second silently became a redeclaration of the
  1260   (600)      % first: IDENTIFIER ALREADY SPECIFIED/DECLARED "MEMBERNAME", and then every
  1261   (601)      % memberName(slot, i) drew MORE SUBSCRIPTS THAN IN THE ARRAY DECLARATION,
  1262   (602)      % because the name now referred to the one-dimensional array. Four errors,
  1263   (603)      % one cause, and none of them mention the length. The listing gives it away:
  1264   (604)      % it prints the TRUNCATED names - BUILDRENAM, BUILDFROMS, REASONNICK.
  1265   (605)      BYTES ARRAY    : memberName(1:16, 1:16)
  1266   (606)      INTEGER ARRAY  : memberLen(1:16)
  1267   (607)      INTEGER ARRAY  : memberUsed(1:16)
  1268   (608)  
  1269   (609)      % Seats whose member has VANISHED - the terminal was killed, the client was
  1270   (610)      % aborted with ESC, the task died. Marked by sendTo when a send comes back
  1271   (611)      % saying the port is gone, and cleared by reapDead.
  1272   (612)      %
  1273   (613)      % A MARK IS NOT AN EVICTION, and the separation is the whole point of this
  1274   (614)      % array rather than a nicety. sendTo is called from inside broadcast's loop,
  1275   (615)      % and evicting there would have to announce the departure - which means
  1276   (616)      % building a message into outBuf while broadcast is still part way through
  1277   (617)      % sending the outBuf it built. One shared buffer, so the message everybody
  1278   (618)      % else is waiting for would be overwritten mid-broadcast and the room would
  1279   (619)      % receive the departure notice instead of what was said. Marking is the only
  1280   (620)      % thing safe to do at that depth; the acting happens back at the top.
  1281   (621)      INTEGER ARRAY  : memberDead(1:16)
  1282   (622)  
  1283   (623)      % ---- WHICH ROOM EACH MEMBER IS IN ------------------------------------
  1284   (624)      %
  1285   (625)      % The room used to be a PORT NAME - the client built "CHAT-" plus the room
  1286   (626)      % and addressed that - so the server never had to know: whoever reached
  1287   (627)      % this port was, by definition, in this room. One port now, so the room is
  1288   (628)      % a field in the Join and the server has to remember it per member.
  1289   (629)      %
  1290   (630)      % Sixteen characters, same as a nickname. A room is a name a person types.
  1291   (631)      BYTES ARRAY    : mbrRoom(1:16, 1:16)
  1292   (632)      INTEGER ARRAY  : lenMbrRoom(1:16)
  1293   (633)  
  1294   (634)      % Where a member goes when the Join names no room - see copyRoomIn.
  1295   (635)      BYTES          : homeRoom := 'LOBBY'
  1296   (636)      INTEGER        : lenHomeRoom := 5
  1297   (637)  
  1298   (638)      % ---- WHICH BUILD EACH MEMBER IS RUNNING -------------------------------
  1299   (639)      %
  1300   (640)      % Added 2026-08-31 chasing a bug that turned out NOT to be a version
  1301   (641)      % mismatch - but there was no cheap way to see that without reading
  1302   (642)      % FILE-STATISTICS on the machine by hand. A client now rides its own
  1303   (643)      % build stamp in on the Join, after the room name and a '|'; an older
  1304   (644)      % client that has never heard of this sends no separator and the whole
  1305   (645)      % text is read as the room, exactly as before - see findJoinSep.
  1306   (646)      %
  1307   (647)      % EIGHT characters, same as clientVersion in CHAT.PLNC. The two must
  1308   (648)      % agree or a version this long gets clipped and LIST-MEMBER lies.
  1309   (649)      INTEGER : maxVerLen := 8
  1310   (650)      BYTES ARRAY    : memberVer(1:16, 1:8)
  1311   (651)      INTEGER ARRAY  : memberVerLen(1:16)
  1312   (652)  
  1313   (653)      % This server's OWN build stamp, printed by STATUS - so a stale CLIENT
  1314   (654)      % and a fresh SERVER (or the other way round) show up side by side in one
  1315   (655)      % command instead of two FILE-STATISTICS calls compared by eye.
  1316   (656)      %
  1317   (657)      % BUMP THIS ON EVERY BUILD THAT SHIPS - it is a hand-set constant, and it
  1318   (658)      % read S31-0854 across the presence fix, the history fix and the putNumber
  1319   (659)      % fix, so STATUS could not tell any of those servers apart. Format: one
  1320   (660)      % letter, day of month, dash, hour and minute.
  1321   (661)      BYTES : serverVersion := 'S02-1340'
  1322   (662)      INTEGER : lenServerVer := 8
  1323   (663)  
  1324   (664)      % Same byte as CHAT.PLNC's verSepByte - the two must agree or a version
  1325   (665)      % never parses out of the room field at all.
  1326   (666)      BYTES : verSepByte := '|'
  1327   (667)  
  1328   (668)      % ---- ROOM TOPICS -----------------------------------------------------
  1329   (669)      %
  1330   (670)      % A SEPARATE TABLE, NOT A COLUMN IN THE MEMBER TABLE, and the reason is
  1331   (671)      % that a topic OUTLIVES the people who set it. Hanging it off a seat would
  1332   (672)      % throw it away the moment the last person left, which is exactly when a
  1333   (673)      % topic is doing its job: telling the next person what the room is for.
  1334   (674)      %
  1335   (675)      % Keyed by ROOM NAME, because that is all a room is here - there is no room
  1336   (676)      % id and no room record. An entry is made the first time somebody sets a
  1337   (677)      % topic, and once made it stays for the life of the server.
  1338   (678)      %
  1339   (679)      % EIGHT rooms, not sixteen. Sixteen seats could in principle be sixteen
  1340   (680)      % rooms of one person each, so this table CAN fill up - and when it does,
  1341   (681)      % setTopic refuses in the open rather than silently dropping the topic. A
  1342   (682)      % refusal a user can read beats a feature that quietly does nothing.
  1343   (683)      INTEGER        : maxTopics := 8
  1344   (684)      BYTES ARRAY    : topicRoom(1:8, 1:16)
  1345   (685)      INTEGER ARRAY  : topicRmLen(1:8)
  1346   (686)  
  1347   (687)      % 64 characters. The whole message must fit outBuf's 250-byte text field
  1348   (688)      % alongside the room name, and a topic is a headline, not an essay.
  1349   (689)      INTEGER        : maxTopicLen := 64
  1350   (690)      BYTES ARRAY    : topicText(1:8, 1:64)
  1351   (691)      INTEGER ARRAY  : topicTextLen(1:8)
  1352   (692)  
  1353   (693)      % ----- the per-room history ring ---------------------------------------
  1354   (694)      %
  1355   (695)      % Ronny, 2026-08-20: somebody joining should see what was just said instead
  1356   (696)      % of an empty screen, and it should survive the server being stopped.
  1357   (697)      %
  1358   (698)      % IT SHARES THE TOPIC TABLE'S SLOTS, AND THAT IS THE WHOLE STRUCTURE. The
  1359   (699)      % design note used to say this needed a room table first, because rooms are
  1360   (700)      % DERIVED from the seats and there is no per-room anything. There already
  1361   (701)      % is one: topicRoom/topicRmLen with findTopic and claimTopic is a room
  1362   (702)      % table under another name. Using the same index for history means the two
  1363   (703)      % can never disagree about what a room is - and a room that has neither a
  1364   (704)      % topic nor a history needs no entry at all.
  1365   (705)      %
  1366   (706)      % ONE 512-BYTE BLOCK PER MESSAGE, block = id MOD histSize. An append is a
  1367   (707)      % single MN120 - no read-modify-write, no compaction, and the file's size is
  1368   (708)      % fixed the day the room is created. See the note beside the imports.
  1369   (709)      %
  1370   (710)      % THE FILE IS NAMED FROM THE INDEX, NEVER THE ROOM NAME: CHATH1:DATA up to
  1371   (711)      % CHATH8:DATA. Room names are chosen by users and a SINTRAN file name is
  1372   (712)      % short, case-folded and must be unique - deriving one from arbitrary text
  1373   (713)      % invites collisions and illegal names with no good failure mode. The room
  1374   (714)      % NAME is written into block 0 so the file is still self-describing.
  1375   (715)      INTEGER        : maxHistRooms := 8
  1376   (716)  
  1377   (717)      % The next id to be written, per room. IT ONLY EVER GOES UP: it is what
  1378   (718)      % makes "block = id MOD size" a ring, and it is the same number the unread
  1379   (719)      % count will need - the server reports the room's latest, the client
  1380   (720)      % subtracts its own anchor. One number doing two jobs, so it has to be right.
  1381   (721)      INTEGER ARRAY  : histNext(1:8)
  1382   (722)  
  1383   (723)      % How many messages this room keeps. Per room, not one constant for the
  1384   (724)      % whole server, because that is what was asked for. 50 until CHAT-MON can
  1385   (725)      % set it.
  1386   (726)      % 200, Ronny 2026-08-23. It is also maxHistSize, so the default IS the
  1387   (727)      % ceiling and INITIALIZE can only ever lower a room - which is the right way
  1388   (728)      % round: the limit exists to stop a typo asking for megabytes, not to stop
  1389   (729)      % anybody keeping what they meant to keep.
  1390   (730)      %
  1391   (731)      % 200 messages is 200 blocks is 100KB a room, and eight rooms is 800KB -
  1392   (732)      % the same figure the no-total decision was taken against, so the bound has
  1393   (733)      % not moved. It is now the ordinary case rather than the worst one.
  1394   (734)      INTEGER        : histDefault := 200
  1395   (735)  
  1396   (736)      % AND A CEILING, because INITIALIZE takes a number from an operator and a
  1397   (737)      % ring of 30000 blocks is 15 megabytes of disk asked for by a typo. 200 is
  1398   (738)      % well past anything a room needs to show a new arrival and still only
  1399   (739)      % 100KB a room.
  1400   (740)      INTEGER        : maxHistSize := 200
  1401   (741)      INTEGER ARRAY  : histSize(1:8)
  1402   (742)  
  1403   (743)      % CreateFile's page count. INTEGER4 because that is what 221B's parameter
  1404   (744)      % is - startAddr beside it is INTEGER4 for the same reason, and passing a
  1405   (745)      % plain INTEGER to a call that wants a double word is the kind of mistake
  1406   (746)      % that compiles and then writes somewhere unintended.
  1407   (747)      INTEGER4       : histPages := 0
  1408   (748)  
  1409   (749)      % The open file, or 0 for "not opened yet". Opened once and left open: a
  1410   (750)      % room is written to on every message, and opening per message would put a
  1411   (751)      % file open and close in the path of every line anybody types.
  1412   (752)      INTEGER ARRAY  : histDev(1:8)
  1413   (753)  
  1414   (754)      % One block, as WORDS, because that is what MN117 and MN120 move.
  1415   (755)      % 256 words = 512 bytes = one SINTRAN block.
  1416   (756)      INTEGER        : histWords := 256
  1417   (757)      INTEGER ARRAY  : histBlock(0:255)
  1418   (758)  
  1419   (759)      % ---- WHEN A LINE WAS SAID, AND HOW AN OLD FILE STILL READS -------
  1420   (760)      %
  1421   (761)      % A replayed line used to be stamped with the moment it was REPLAYED,
  1422   (762)      % so a whole backlog shared one time and could not say what happened
  1423   (763)      % when. The block had nowhere to put the real one: word 0 is the id,
  1424   (764)      % 1 and 2 are the two lengths, and the name started at 3.
  1425   (765)      %
  1426   (766)      % THE OLD FILES ON THE THREE MACHINES MUST STILL REPLAY, so the new
  1427   (767)      % layout is marked rather than assumed. Word 3 is now a MARKER:
  1428   (768)      %
  1429   (769)      %   histBlock(3) = histStamp  ->  words 4..9 are the ND calendar and
  1430   (770)      %                                 the name starts at 10
  1431   (771)      %   anything else             ->  an OLD block: no time, name at 3
  1432   (772)      %
  1433   (773)      % IT IS SAFE BECAUSE WORD 3 USED TO HOLD THE FIRST CHARACTER OF A
  1434   (774)      % NAME. Names are ASCII, so that word was always small and positive;
  1435   (775)      % 27127 cannot be a character and so cannot be mistaken for one. No
  1436   (776)      % migration, no history thrown away, and a machine still running the
  1437   (777)      % old build writes blocks this one reads correctly as timeless.
  1438   (778)      INTEGER        : histStamp := 27127
  1439   (779)      INTEGER        : histHdrNew := 10     % name starts here when stamped
  1440   (780)      INTEGER        : histHdrOld := 3      % ...and here when it is not
  1441   (781)  
  1442   (782)      % The ND calendar as MN113 fills it - see the IMPORT at the top.
  1443   (783)      INTEGER ARRAY  : histCal(0:6)
  1444   (784)  
  1445   (785)      % The file name being built.
  1446   (786)      %
  1447   (787)      % IT NAMES THE USER, AND THAT IS NOT DECORATION: "(SYSTEM)CHATH1".
  1448   (788)      %
  1449   (789)      % MEASURED ON D100, 2026-08-23. With a bare "CHATH1" the server created
  1450   (790)      % nothing and opened nothing - and after (SYSTEM)CHATH1:DATA was created BY
  1451   (791)      % HAND from the terminal, 50 pages, FILE-STATISTICS still said
  1452   (792)      % "OPENED 0 TIMES". The program was looking somewhere that is not SYSTEM.
  1453   (793)      %
  1454   (794)      % Which somewhere is not settled: an RT program does not run as the user
  1455   (795)      % who loaded it, and "CREATE-FILE (RT)CHATH1:DATA,50" typed as SYSTEM is
  1456   (796)      % refused with NOT DIRECTORY ACCESS, so that guess cannot even be tested
  1457   (797)      % from a terminal.
  1458   (798)      %
  1459   (799)      % NAMING THE USER REMOVES THE QUESTION rather than answering it. A server
  1460   (800)      % that keeps its files somewhere definite is better than one that depends
  1461   (801)      % on whatever user SINTRAN decides an RT program is - and the files then
  1462   (802)      % sit beside CHATSV:PROG where anybody looking for them would look.
  1463   (803)      %
  1464   (804)      % 22 bytes: "(SYSTEM)CHATH1" is 14, and the quoted form that means CREATE
  1465   (805)      % is 16. Room to spare rather than a count that has to be re-derived if the
  1466   (806)      % stem ever changes.
  1467   (807)      BYTES          : histName(0:21)
  1468   (808)      BYTES          : histUser := '(SYSTEM)'
  1469   (809)      INTEGER        : lenHistUser := 8
  1470   (810)      BYTES          : histStem := 'CHATH'
  1471   (811)      BYTES          : histType := 'DATA'
  1472   (812)  
  1473   (813)      % Is there anything to reap? Kept so the common case - nothing died - costs
  1474   (814)      % one compare per message instead of a walk of the whole room.
  1475   (815)      INTEGER        : deadFound := 0
  1476   (816)  
  1477   (817)      % ----- knowing when to give up ------------------------------------------
  1478   (818)      % How many receives in a ROW have failed. Cleared by any good one, so this
  1479   (819)      % counts a run of trouble rather than trouble over a lifetime.
  1480   (820)      INTEGER        : badRuns := 0
  1481   (821)  
  1482   (822)      % Twenty in a row is not a bad moment, it is a broken machine. Small enough
  1483   (823)      % that a spin is caught in under a second, large enough that a burst of
  1484   (824)      % something transient is ridden out rather than treated as fatal.
  1485   (825)      INTEGER        : maxBadRuns := 20
  1486   (826)  
  1487   (827)      % Print at most this many of them. The fault that made all this necessary
  1488   (828)      % was ONE message repeated as fast as the terminal could take it, and a
  1489   (829)      % diagnostic with no limit buries the line that says what happened.
  1490   (830)      INTEGER        : maxNoisy := 5
  1491   (831)  
  1492   (832)      INTEGER ARRAY : stack(0:1000)
  1493   (833)  
  1494   (834)      % SIZE OF inBuf, named because the read must clamp against it. XMSG reports
  1495   (835)      % the SENDER'S message size; nothing stops that being larger than this
  1496   (836)      % array, and PLANC checks no bound.
  1497   (837)      INTEGER : inBufSize := 256
  1498   (838)      BYTES : inBuf(0:255)
  1499   (839)      % A MEMBER'S NAME, FLAT. memberName is two-dimensional and the library
  1500   (840)      % takes BYTES, so the name is copied here on the way out - see
