  1501   (841)      % buildFromSlot for why a row subarray is avoided.
  1502   (842)      BYTES : nameFlat(0:15)
  1503   (843)  
  1504   (844)      BYTES : outBuf(0:255)
  1505   (845)  
  1506   (846)      % ONE SPACE, AS A BYTE. buildWho puts a separator between names, and
  1507   (847)      % "' ' =: outBuf(at)" does not compile: 'x' is a STRING and an element of a
  1508   (848)      % BYTES array is a BYTE. Held as a one-element BYTES so the copy is
  1509   (849)      % byte-to-byte.
  1510   (850)      BYTES : spaceByte := ' '
  1511   (851)  
  1512   (852)      % Same trick, for the room counts /list writes - "LOBBY(2)". Brackets are
  1513   (853)      % held as one-element BYTES for exactly the reason spaceByte is.
  1514   (854)      BYTES : openParen  := '('
  1515   (855)      BYTES : closeParen := ')'
  1516   (856)  
  1517   (857)      % '@', for qualifying a remote member - "ANNA@D102". Two machines can each
  1518   (858)      % have an ANNA, and a bare name would make them look like one person.
  1519   (859)      BYTES : atByte     := '@'
  1520   (860)  
  1521   (861)      % '0' as a NUMBER, because a digit is computed rather than copied: the
  1522   (862)      % count is arithmetic, so the character is digitZero + the value. Held as
  1523   (863)      % an INTEGER and not a BYTES for that reason - it is added to, not copied.
  1524   (864)      INTEGER : digitZero := 48
  1525   (865)  
  1526   (866)      % Same reason as spaceByte: a string literal cannot be stored into a BYTES
  1527   (867)      % element, so every literal character this program writes into a buffer
  1528   (868)      % lives in a one-element BYTES and is copied byte to byte.
  1529   (869)      % A LOCAL CANNOT CARRY AN INITIALISER. "BYTES : oneByte := ' '" inside
  1530   (870)      % showName drew *** ERROR - INITIAL VALUE ILLEGAL HERE, while the identical
  1531   (871)      % line at MODULE level compiles - which is why the same declaration works in
  1532   (872)      % CHATMON and failed here. Module level it is, beside the others.
  1533   (873)      BYTES : oneByte   := ' '
  1534   (874)      BYTES : slashByte := '/'
  1535   (875)      BYTES : zeroByte  := '0'
  1536   (876)  
  1537   (877)      % '!', between a MACHINE and a PERSON in a direct message - D102!RONNY.
  1538   (878)      %
  1539   (879)      % NOT '@', and the choice is deliberate rather than a matter of taste. '@'
  1540   (880)      % is the client's short command prefix, so @RONNY@D100 would need a parser
  1541   (881)      % that counts at-signs and decides which one it meant. 'machine!alias'
  1542   (882)      % reads left to right as route-then-person and has precedent in the UUCP
  1543   (883)      % addressing of the same era. The byte is pinned as 0x21 by the C# test
  1544   (884)      % AQualifiedTargetKeepsItsMachineSeparator, so the two ends cannot drift.
  1545   (885)      %
  1546   (886)      % Note that '@' still qualifies a name for DISPLAY - "ANNA@D102" in /who.
  1547   (887)      % Display and addressing are different jobs: one is read by a person, the
  1548   (888)      % other is typed by one.
  1549   (889)      BYTES : bangByte  := '!'
  1550   (890)  
  1551   (891)      % ':', for the all-rooms /who - "LOBBY: TESTER OLAV". Same reason as every
  1552   (892)      % other single character in this list: a string literal cannot be stored
  1553   (893)      % into a BYTES element, so it lives in a one-element BYTES and is copied.
  1554   (894)      BYTES : colonByte := ':'
  1555   (895)  
  1556   (896)      % '"', and it is not decoration: quoting a file name is how you say CREATE
  1557   (897)      % to MON50, which would otherwise require the file to exist already. See
  1558   (898)      % histOpen, where the plain name is tried first and the quoted one only if
  1559   (899)      % that fails.
  1560   (900)      BYTES : quoteByte := '"'
  1561   (901)  
  1562   (902)      % The fixed part of the status line. Held as bytes for the same reason.
  1563   (903)      BYTES : statusWord := 'SEATS '
  1564   (904)  
  1565   (905)      INTEGER  : myPort
  1566   (906)      INTEGER  : msgType
  1567   (907)  
  1568   (908)      % ---- COUNTED, NOT PRINTED ---------------------------------------------
  1569   (909)      % These three used to print a line each, every time, straight to terminal 1.
  1570   (910)      % Two of them are EXPECTED events, so the terminal filled with them and the
  1571   (911)      % rare interesting line was buried:
  1572   (912)      %
  1573   (913)      %   nEmptyLet  a routed letter with no payload. XROUT's ANSWER to a seat
  1574   (914)      %              return arrives on our own port looking exactly like this, so
  1575   (915)      %              there is one per seat returned - see the note by the drop.
  1576   (916)      %   nBounced   a message that came home because nobody was there. One per
  1577   (917)      %              dead client, and clients die in bursts when a terminal goes.
  1578   (918)      %   nRefused   a join we turned away, whatever the reason.
  1579   (919)      %
  1580   (920)      % A count answers the question the flood was trying to answer - "is this
  1581   (921)      % happening more than it should" - and STATUS shows all three on demand.
  1582   (922)      %
  1583   (923)      % WHAT A RISING nEmptyLet ACTUALLY MEANS - measured 2026-08-24 from a hub
  1584   (924)      % capture, and it is NOT a fault. The trunk exchanges a burst of about 24
  1585   (925)      % frames every 2 to 3 minutes when nobody is typing at all: kTrkHello, then
  1586   (926)      % kTrkWho, then kTrkMbrs, both directions. Every letter we send that way
  1587   (927)      % draws an XROUT answer back onto our own port with no payload, so the count
  1588   (928)      % tracks the TRUNK's idle cycle, not the clients. 712 on D100 after an
  1589   (929)      % afternoon with one idle client is normal.
  1590   (930)      %
  1591   (931)      % So read it as a liveness number: climbing steadily means the trunk is up.
  1592   (932)      % FLAT while a trunk is supposed to be up is the interesting case.
  1593   (933)      INTEGER  : nEmptyLet
  1594   (934)      INTEGER  : nBounced
  1595   (935)      INTEGER  : nRefused
  1596   (936)      % How many lines this machine has passed ON to a further peer. A machine in
  1597   (937)      % the MIDDLE of a chain should show this climbing; an end machine should
  1598   (938)      % show it flat at zero, and a non-zero count on an end machine means the
  1599   (939)      % topology is not what somebody thinks it is.
  1600   (940)      INTEGER  : nRelayed
  1601   (941)  
  1602   (942)      % LINES A LOCAL SPEAKER SENT ONWARD TO A PEER, and how many of those sends
  1603   (943)      % were REFUSED.
  1604   (944)      %
  1605   (945)      % These did not exist, and their absence cost a whole evening. nRelayed
  1606   (946)      % counts only lines arriving from a trunk and passed on - so when a locally
  1607   (947)      % typed line failed to cross, `relay` sat still and looked like proof that
  1608   (948)      % nothing was forwarded. It was proof of nothing at all: the two paths are
  1609   (949)      % different routines and only one of them had a counter.
  1610   (950)      %
  1611   (951)      % nFwdBad is the more valuable of the pair. forwardSaid stored sendToName's
  1612   (952)      % status into a variable and never looked at it, so a refused letter was
  1613   (953)      % perfectly silent - the one failure shape that cannot be seen from either
  1614   (954)      % end.
  1615   (955)      INTEGER  : nForwarded
  1616   (956)      INTEGER  : nFwdBad
  1617   (957)  
  1618   (958)      % How many repeats we recognised and threw away. On a CHAIN this stays at
  1619   (959)      % ZERO for ever - there is only one path between any two machines, so no
  1620   (960)      % line can arrive twice. It only starts moving once somebody wires a
  1621   (961)      % second path, and that is exactly when you want to see it moving.
  1622   (962)      INTEGER  : nDupes
  1623   (963)  
  1624   (964)      % The number we put on the next line typed HERE. Only this machine ever
  1625   (965)      % sets it; a relay passes an id on untouched.
  1626   (966)      %
  1627   (967)      % It wraps at 65536 and that is harmless: a number can only be mistaken
  1628   (968)      % for a live one if we sent 65536 lines while the first was still inside
  1629   (969)      % a 64-entry window, which cannot happen.
  1630   (970)      INTEGER  : myLineId
  1631   (971)  
  1632   (972)      % The lines we have already seen, as two parallel rows - the machine it
  1633   (973)      % came from and the number that machine gave it. BOTH are needed: every
  1634   (974)      % machine numbers from its own counter, so id 1 exists on all of them at
  1635   (975)      % once, and matching on the id alone would silently swallow other
  1636   (976)      % machines' lines.
  1637   (977)      INTEGER ARRAY  : seenSys(1:64)
  1638   (978)      INTEGER ARRAY  : seenId(1:64)
  1639   (979)      % Where the next one goes, 1..maxSeen, and how many rows are real.
  1640   (980)      % seenUsed matters because a fresh table is all zeros, and (0,0) is a pair
  1641   (981)      % a real line could otherwise be mistaken for.
  1642   (982)      INTEGER  : seenAt
  1643   (983)      INTEGER  : seenUsed
  1644   (984)  
  1645   (985)      % ---- THE COMMAND PORT, AND WHY THE LOOP HAD TO CHANGE ------------------
  1646   (986)      %
  1647   (987)      % XFRCV RECEIVES ON ONE NAMED PORT. There is no receive-on-any-port form -
  1648   (988)      % ND-60.134.2 section 4.3.2.10. This program used to block on myPort with
  1649   (989)      % XFWTF, and a server that keeps doing that NEVER SEES a message on a
  1650   (990)      % second port. Not failing, not logging: sitting in the lobby wait while
  1651   (991)      % CHATMON waits for an answer that cannot come.
  1652   (992)      %
  1653   (993)      % The manual's answer is XFWAK: a non-blocking receive arms a wake-up
  1654   (994)      % instead, and "this 'wake up' option can be enabled on more than one port
  1655   (995)      % at a time". MON 100B is StartRTProgram, so that wake-up RT-STARTS this
  1656   (996)      % program - which means there is no wait to choose at all. Drain both
  1657   (997)      % ports, and when both are empty, TERMINATE. A transmission to either one
  1658   (998)      % starts us again.
  1659   (999)      %
  1660  (1000)      % AND THE TRAP FROM THE SAME PARAGRAPH: "when the 'wake up' is done, the
  1661  (1001)      % message is not received, and so the receive must be repeated." The
  1662  (1002)      % wake-up is a DOORBELL, NOT A DELIVERY.
  1663  (1003)      INTEGER  : adminPort
  1664  (1004)  
  1665  (1005)      % CHAT-ADMIN is a NORMAL named port (XMPOPNM), NOT a connection port.
  1666  (1006)      % CHAT-LOBBY is a connection port and every arrival there spends one of the
  1667  (1007)      % free connections - a seat a human then cannot have. An operator asking
  1668  (1008)      % for STATUS must not cost the room a seat, and must still get in when the
  1669  (1009)      % room is full, which is exactly when they would want to ask.
  1670  (1010)      BYTES    : admName := 'CHAT-ADMIN'
  1671  (1011)      INTEGER  : lenAdmName := 10
  1672  (1012)  
  1673  (1013)      % ---- THE TRUNK PORT, AND WHY IT IS A THIRD PORT --------------------------
  1674  (1014)      %
  1675  (1015)      % Server-to-server traffic. Also XMPOPNM, and for the SAME reason the command
  1676  (1016)      % port is: a trunk must cost the room NO SEATS. A peer that is switched off
  1677  (1017)      % gets a HELLO every so often for as long as it is away, and if that spent a
  1678  (1018)      % free connection each time the room would fill up with machines that are not
  1679  (1019)      % there. The design calls this constraint 1: the trunk must never point at
  1680  (1020)      % the room port.
  1681  (1021)      %
  1682  (1022)      % A TRUNK IS NOT A CONNECTION. XMSG named ports are message passing - there is
  1683  (1023)      % no session to open, nothing to hold, and nothing that drops. A trunk is a
  1684  (1024)      % BELIEF that a peer is reachable, held as soft state and refreshed. Neither
  1685  (1025)      % side is the client: each is a server when a letter arrives and a client when
  1686  (1026)      % it sends one.
  1687  (1027)      BYTES    : trkName := 'CHAT-TRUNK'
  1688  (1028)      INTEGER  : lenTrkName := 10
  1689  (1029)      INTEGER  : trunkPort
  1690  (1030)  
  1691  (1031)      % The XSLET letter header XMPBLET builds when this server ORIGINATES a
  1692  (1032)      % message to another machine. Replies do not need it - they go straight to
  1693  (1033)      % the magic the message arrived with - so this exists only for trunks.
  1694  (1034)      BYTES    : letterBuf(0:63)
  1695  (1035)  
  1696  (1036)      % The peer's SYSTEM NAME, built at runtime from its number: 'D' and the
  1697  (1037)      % decimal digits. XMPBLET's system parameter is a NAME as BYTES, never a
  1698  (1038)      % number - see the long note on sendToName for the three compile cycles that
  1699  (1039)      % established it. Declared with explicit BOUNDS because it is filled at
  1700  (1040)      % runtime, which is also why it is passed as a subarray.
  1701  (1041)      % WHAT THIS MACHINE CALLS ITSELF, set by SET-NAME from CHAT-MON.
  1702  (1042)      %
  1703  (1043)      % EMPTY MEANS NO NAME, and then everything falls back to D<number> - which
  1704  (1044)      % is exactly what this server did before names existed. That fallback is
  1705  (1045)      % the whole reason this can go onto three running machines one at a time.
  1706  (1046)      %
  1707  (1047)      % It is NOT written to disk. The boot mode file already drives CHAT-MON and
  1708  (1048)      % already starts the trunks, so SET-NAME belongs beside those - one place
  1709  (1049)      % per machine, visible in the same file as the rest of its configuration,
  1710  (1050)      % and no new file handling in a server that has none.
  1711  (1051)      BYTES    : sysName(0:15)
  1712  (1052)      INTEGER  : lenSysName := 0
  1713  (1053)  
  1714  (1054)      % peerName is NOT that. It is the XROUT ADDRESS a letter is sent to -
  1715  (1055)      % 'D102' - and it must stay a D and a number whatever anybody is called,
  1716  (1056)      % or the trunks stop working. Named apart on purpose.
  1717  (1057)      BYTES    : peerName(0:15)
  1718  (1058)      BYTES    : dByte := 'D'
  1719  (1059)  
  1720  (1060)      % ---- THE PEER TABLE ------------------------------------------------------
  1721  (1061)      %
  1722  (1062)      % One row per trunked machine. SOFT STATE: nothing here is a connection, it
  1723  (1063)      % is what we currently BELIEVE about a peer, and the belief is refreshed by
  1724  (1064)      % anything arriving from it.
  1725  (1065)      %
  1726  (1066)      % EIGHT is plenty and the bound is deliberate. Trunks are configured by an
  1727  (1067)      % operator one at a time, not discovered, so a table that cannot overflow
  1728  (1068)      % quietly is worth more than a big one. TRUNK refuses out loud when it is
  1729  (1069)      % full.
  1730  (1070)      INTEGER        : maxPeers := 8
  1731  (1071)      INTEGER ARRAY  : peerSystem(1:8)      % XMSG system number, 0 = free row
  1732  (1072)  
  1733  (1073)      % WHAT EACH PEER CALLS ITSELF, learned from the name it puts in its Hello.
  1734  (1074)      % Empty until one arrives, and then D<number> is used for that peer exactly
  1735  (1075)      % as before - so a machine running the older build is simply a machine
  1736  (1076)      % without a name, not a machine that breaks anything.
  1737  (1077)      BYTES ARRAY    : pName(1:8, 1:16)
  1738  (1078)      INTEGER ARRAY  : pNameLen(1:8)
  1739  (1079)      INTEGER ARRAY  : peerState(1:8)       % see the three constants below
  1740  (1080)      INTEGER ARRAY  : peerHeard(1:8)       % loop ticks since we last heard from it
  1741  (1081)  
  1742  (1082)      % ---- WHO PUT THIS PEER HERE: the operator, or the wire? ----------------
  1743  (1083)      %
  1744  (1084)      % 1 for a row an operator asked for with START-TRUNK, 0 for one this server
  1745  (1085)      % LEARNED because a message arrived from a system it had never seen.
  1746  (1086)      %
  1747  (1087)      % WHY THE DISTINCTION EXISTS. markPeerUp adds any unknown system on first
  1748  (1088)      % contact, so the eight rows can be filled entirely by machines nobody
  1749  (1089)      % configured - and then a real START-TRUNK answers "no room for another
  1750  (1090)      % trunk". The note above this table says a table that cannot overflow
  1751  (1091)      % quietly is worth more than a big one, and that was only half true: the
  1752  (1092)      % OPERATOR path refuses out loud, the LEARNING path filled it in silence.
  1753  (1093)      %
  1754  (1094)      % With this flag an operator can always get a row - addPeer takes one back
  1755  (1095)      % from a learned peer rather than refusing - so the table stays small and
  1756  (1096)      % the loud refusal is reserved for the case where every row was asked for.
  1757  (1097)      INTEGER ARRAY  : peerFixed(1:8)
  1758  (1098)      INTEGER        : peerLearned := 0
  1759  (1099)      INTEGER        : peerAsked   := 1
  1760  (1100)  
  1761  (1101)      % ---- MAY THIS SERVER LEARN A PEER IT WAS NEVER TOLD ABOUT? -------------
  1762  (1102)      %
  1763  (1103)      % TRUE by default, which is exactly what the server has always done: any
  1764  (1104)      % system that reaches the trunk port becomes a peer. That keeps setting up
  1765  (1105)      % a lab easy - no list of friends to type - and nothing that works today
  1766  (1106)      % stops working.
  1767  (1107)      %
  1768  (1108)      % BE CLEAR ABOUT WHAT IT COSTS. An open server believes whatever a peer
  1769  (1109)      % says about who is speaking on it: a line arriving on the trunk port is
  1770  (1110)      % delivered as NAME@thatsystem, and the only thing standing between a
  1771  (1111)      % stranger and impersonating anybody is whether XMSG would carry their
  1772  (1112)      % letter at all. LOCK-PEERS is how an operator ends that once the lab is
  1773  (1113)      % up: the peers that are already known keep working, and no new one is
  1774  (1114)      % ever learned.
  1775  (1115)      %
  1776  (1116)      % It is deliberately NOT saved anywhere. A lock is a statement about the
  1777  (1117)      % running system, and a server that came back from a reboot silently
  1778  (1118)      % refusing its own trunks would be a worse failure than the one this
  1779  (1119)      % prevents.
  1780  (1120)      BOOLEAN        : peersOpen := TRUE
  1781  (1121)  
  1782  (1122)      % Peer states. NOT a BOOLEAN pair, because "we have never heard from it" and
  1783  (1123)      % "we heard from it and then it went quiet" are different things to an
  1784  (1124)      % operator reading LIST-TRUNKS: the first is probably a configuration
  1785  (1125)      % mistake, the second is a machine that went away.
  1786  (1126)      INTEGER        : peerUnknown := 0
  1787  (1127)      INTEGER        : peerUp      := 1
  1788  (1128)      INTEGER        : peerDown    := 2
  1789  (1129)  
  1790  (1130)      % How many idle loop ticks before a peer we have heard nothing from is
  1791  (1131)      % declared Down, and how often we say HELLO to one that is not Up.
  1792  (1132)      %
  1793  (1133)      % BACKOFF IS REQUIRED, NOT OPTIONAL - a machine that is switched off must not
  1794  (1134)      % be probed every few seconds for a week. This first cut uses a flat retry;
  1795  (1135)      % the design asks for 15s/30s/60s/5min and that is worth doing once the flat
  1796  (1136)      % one is proved on the wire. Written down here so it is not forgotten.
  1797  (1137)      % Seconds of silence before a peer we HAD is declared Down.
  1798  (1138)      INTEGER        : peerDeadAfter := 60
  1799  (1139)  
  1800  (1140)      % ---- BACKOFF, AND WHY IT IS NOT A FIXED INTERVAL ------------------------
  1801  (1141)      %
  1802  (1142)      % "Backoff is required, not optional" - the design, and it is right: a
  1803  (1143)      % machine that is switched off must not be probed every few seconds for a
  1804  (1144)      % week. Two peers left down over a weekend at a flat 20s would be about
  1805  (1145)      % 30,000 pointless letters.
  1806  (1146)      %
  1807  (1147)      % So the wait DOUBLES after every unanswered try, from 15 seconds up to a
  1808  (1148)      % cap of five minutes: 15, 30, 60, 120, 240, 300, 300, ... A machine that
  1809  (1149)      % comes back is found within five minutes at worst, and usually far sooner
  1810  (1150)      % because ITS OWN hello reaches us the moment it starts - the retry is the
  1811  (1151)      % fallback, not the mechanism.
  1812  (1152)      %
  1813  (1153)      % RESET ON EVERY transition to Up, not just the first, so a peer that flaps
  1814  (1154)      % does not inherit a five-minute wait from its last absence.
  1815  (1155)      INTEGER        : peerFirstWait := 15
  1816  (1156)      INTEGER        : peerMaxWait   := 300
  1817  (1157)      INTEGER ARRAY  : peerWait(1:8)        % seconds to wait before the next try
  1818  (1158)      INTEGER ARRAY  : peerCount(1:8)       % seconds left of that wait
  1819  (1159)  
  1820  (1160)      % ---- WHO IS ON THE OTHER MACHINES ---------------------------------------
  1821  (1161)      %
  1822  (1162)      % Phase C. A copy of what each peer last told us about its members, so /who
  1823  (1163)      % can answer across machines.
  1824  (1164)      %
  1825  (1165)      % ENTIRELY DERIVED, NEVER AUTHORITATIVE. Every row here was sent by the
  1826  (1166)      % machine that owns it, and is replaced wholesale the next time that machine
  1827  (1167)      % speaks. We never add, edit or reason about another machine's members - if
  1828  (1168)      % this disagrees with the far end, the far end is right and this is stale.
  1829  (1169)      %
  1830  (1170)      % KEYED BY THE OWNING SYSTEM so a peer going away can have all of its rows
  1831  (1171)      % dropped in one pass. A peer that is Down must not leave people apparently
  1832  (1172)      % chatting from a machine that is not there.
  1833  (1173)      INTEGER        : maxRemote := 32
  1834  (1174)      INTEGER ARRAY  : rmtSystem(1:32)      % owning machine, 0 = free row
  1835  (1175)      BYTES ARRAY    : rmtName(1:32, 1:16)
  1836  (1176)      INTEGER ARRAY  : rmtNameLen(1:32)
  1837  (1177)      BYTES ARRAY    : rmtRoom(1:32, 1:16)
  1838  (1178)      INTEGER ARRAY  : rmtRoomLen(1:32)
  1839  (1179)  
  1840  (1180)      % ---- THE PRESENCE DIFF, 2026-09-02 --------------------------------------
  1841  (1181)      %
  1842  (1182)      % A kTrkMbrs replaces a peer's rows WHOLESALE, and until tonight it did so
  1843  (1183)      % in silence: a person joining or leaving on another machine changed the
  1844  (1184)      % tables and told no local client anything, so the room's count and its
  1845  (1185)      % "has joined"/"has left" notices only ever covered THIS machine. These
  1846  (1186)      % arrays hold a snapshot of one peer's rows from BEFORE the replace, so
  1847  (1187)      % the difference can be announced to local seats as ordinary kJoined and
  1848  (1188)      % kLeft - the kinds the client already renders and counts. oldUsed is 1
  1849  (1189)      % for a snapshot row, and 2 once the diff has matched it to a new row.
  1850  (1190)      INTEGER ARRAY  : oldUsed(1:32)
  1851  (1191)      BYTES ARRAY    : oldNm(1:32, 1:16)
  1852  (1192)      INTEGER ARRAY  : oldNmLen(1:32)
  1853  (1193)      BYTES ARRAY    : oldRm(1:32, 1:16)
  1854  (1194)      INTEGER ARRAY  : oldRmLen(1:32)
  1855  (1195)  
  1856  (1196)      % One announcement's name and room, copied FLAT so a single announceGo
  1857  (1197)      % serves both directions of the diff - the joined side reads the rmt
  1858  (1198)      % tables, the left side reads the snapshot, and neither 2-D row can be
  1859  (1199)      % passed to a routine.
  1860  (1200)      BYTES          : annName(0:15)
  1861  (1201)      INTEGER        : annNameLen := 0
  1862  (1202)      BYTES          : annRoom(0:15)
  1863  (1203)      INTEGER        : annRoomLen := 0
  1864  (1204)  
  1865  (1205)      % Quiet seconds on an UP trunk before a keepalive hello goes out - see
  1866  (1206)      % peerTick. Two of these fit inside peerDeadAfter with room to spare.
  1867  (1207)      INTEGER        : peerPingAt := 20
  1868  (1208)  
  1869  (1209)      % TRUE once the ports are open. MODULE data lives in the RT segment and
  1870  (1210)      % SURVIVES the terminate above - the member table, the port numbers and the
  1871  (1211)      % magic are all still here on the next start. INISTACK is not: it runs
  1872  (1212)      % every time. So the opening work must happen ONCE, or the second start
  1873  (1213)      % tries to re-open a name this program already holds and fails.
  1874  (1214)      BOOLEAN  : portsOpen := FALSE
  1875  (1215)  
  1876  (1216)      % Which port the message being handled arrived on. Admin kinds are refused
  1877  (1217)      % on the lobby and room kinds on the command port - sharing one vocabulary
  1878  (1218)      % is not sharing the authority to use any of it anywhere.
  1879  (1219)      BOOLEAN  : fromAdmin := FALSE
  1880  (1220)  
  1881  (1221)      % Same idea for the trunk port. A kTrkHello arriving on the ROOM port is not
  1882  (1222)      % a trunk message, it is a member sending something they should not, and the
  1883  (1223)      % handler drops it rather than trusting it.
  1884  (1224)      BOOLEAN  : fromTrunk := FALSE
  1885  (1225)  
  1886  (1226)      % Which SYSTEM the message being handled came from, taken apart from the
  1887  (1227)      % sender's magic number with XMPFM2P. The trunk needs it because a peer is
  1888  (1228)      % identified by system number, and because an unsolicited HELLO has to be
  1889  (1229)      % answerable without anything having been configured first.
  1890  (1230)      INTEGER  : senderSystem := 0
  1891  (1231)      INTEGER  : senderPortNo := 0
  1892  (1232)      INTEGER  : senderRtInfo := 0
  1893  (1233)      INTEGER  : senderExtra  := 0
  1894  (1234)  
  1895  (1235)      % OUR OWN system number, taken apart from our own magic at startup.
  1896  (1236)      %
  1897  (1237)      % NEEDED BECAUSE WE HEAR OURSELVES. A hello addressed to a machine that has
  1898  (1238)      % no CHAT-TRUNK name comes straight BACK, arrives on our own trunk port
  1899  (1239)      % carrying OUR magic, and without this test the server adds a peer row for
  1900  (1240)      % itself and calls it up. MEASURED on D100 2026-08-22, the first time a
  1901  (1241)      % trunk was ever configured:
  1902  (1242)      %
  1903  (1243)      %     C-M: START-TRUNK 102
  1904  (1244)      %     C-M: LIST-TRUNKS   ->   102 unknown 100 up
  1905  (1245)      %
  1906  (1246)      % 100 is this machine. Nothing was up; a letter had bounced.
  1907  (1247)      INTEGER  : mySystem := 0
  1908  (1248)  
  1909  (1249)      % Direct messages delivered, and direct messages refused. On the STATUS
  1910  (1250)      % line beside the other counters, because a screen cannot tell you either
  1911  (1251)      % of these - a refusal is seen only by the one person who got it, and a
  1912  (1252)      % delivery is seen only by two.
  1913  (1253)      %
  1914  (1254)      % A CLIMBING refuse count with a flat sent count is the shape of a client
  1915  (1255)      % or a user getting the address form wrong, which is worth knowing without
  1916  (1256)      % having to ask anybody.
  1917  (1257)      INTEGER  : dmSent := 0
  1918  (1258)      INTEGER  : dmBad  := 0
  1919  (1259)  
  1920  (1260)      % WHAT AN ALIAS RESOLVED TO. Four answers, so they are variables and not a
  1921  (1261)      % return value - PLANC returns one thing.
  1922  (1262)      %
  1923  (1263)      %   dirCount   how many people matched. 0 refuses, 2 or more refuses with
  1924  (1264)      %              the candidates, exactly 1 is delivered.
  1925  (1265)      %   dirLocal   the local seat, or 0
  1926  (1266)      %   dirRemote  the row in the remote table, or 0
  1927  (1267)      %   dirSystem  the machine the one match is on
  1928  (1268)      INTEGER  : dirCount  := 0
  1929  (1269)      INTEGER  : dirLocal  := 0
  1930  (1270)      INTEGER  : dirRemote := 0
  1931  (1271)      INTEGER  : dirSystem := 0
  1932  (1272)  
  1933  (1273)      % For the idle sleep - see the empty-drain branch of the main loop.
  1934  (1274)      % sleepWhy comes back 0 when the time simply elapsed, 1 when a break
  1935  (1275)      % restarted us. Neither changes what this loop does next, but the call
  1936  (1276)      % needs somewhere to put it.
  1937  (1277)      INTEGER  : sleepUnits
  1938  (1278)      INTEGER  : sleepKind
  1939  (1279)      INTEGER  : sleepWhy
  1940  (1280)  
  1941  (1281)      % ---- THE TICK COMES OFF THE CLOCK, NOT OFF THE SLEEPS -------------
  1942  (1282)      %
  1943  (1283)      % peerTick has to run ONCE A SECOND: every counter it touches is in
  1944  (1284)      % seconds - peerHeard against peerDeadAfter, and peerWait doubling to a
  1945  (1285)      % five-minute ceiling. Ticking it too fast ages a quiet trunk out early
  1946  (1286)      % and re-dials it too often, which is a healthy trunk that looks like a
  1947  (1287)      % flapping one.
  1948  (1288)      %
  1949  (1289)      % IT USED TO BE COUNTED IN SLEEPS - ten sleeps of 100 ms to a second -
  1950  (1290)      % and that only measures time while EVERY SLEEP RUNS ITS FULL LENGTH.
  1951  (1291)      % The doorbell below breaks exactly that assumption: a wake-up cuts the
  1952  (1292)      % sleep short, so on a busy server ten "sleeps" could pass in a few
  1953  (1293)      % milliseconds and every trunk timeout would run fast. The counter had
  1954  (1294)      % to go before the doorbell could arrive.
  1955  (1295)      %
  1956  (1296)      % So the tick is now a CLOCK COMPARISON. MON11 is the machine's own
  1957  (1297)      % basic-unit counter; when 50 units (one second) have gone by since the
  1958  (1298)      % last tick, tick. This is right whatever the sleep does.
  1959  (1299)      % ALL THREE INTEGER4, DELIBERATELY. MON11 hands back an INTEGER4, and
  1960  (1300)      % ND-60.117.5 section 5.4 says a relational operator needs both operands
  1961  (1301)      % of the SAME data type - allowing modified integers to be mixed, which
  1962  (1302)      % probably covers this. "Probably" is a twenty-minute compile to test, and
  1963  (1303)      % the one shape PROVED to build on D100 keeps every term the same type:
  1964  (1304)      % CHATUI.PLNC declares lastAdd, nowTime AND addEvery as INTEGER4 for the
  1965  (1305)      % identical "IF nowTime - lastAdd >= addEvery" comparison. Copy what
  1966  (1306)      % compiled rather than what the manual permits.
  1967  (1307)      INTEGER4 : lastTick  := 0
  1968  (1308)      INTEGER4 : nowTicks  := 0
  1969  (1309)      INTEGER4 : tickUnits := 50
  1970  (1310)  
  1971  (1311)      % ---- THE DOORBELL, AND THE THING THAT TURNS IT OFF ----------------
  1972  (1312)      %
  1973  (1313)      % XFWAK arms a wake-up on a port: when a message lands, XMSG performs
  1974  (1314)      % an RT-start on this program. @RT's own rule 3 (ND-60.128.5, the entry
  1975  (1315)      % for @RT, whose related monitor call is RT/MON 100) says a program in
  1976  (1316)      % HOLD "is removed from that state", and TimeOut documents a restart
  1977  (1317)      % cause of -1 for exactly this. So the wake-up cuts the sleep short and
  1978  (1318)      % says so in sleepWhy. That is the whole point: one second of sleep for
  1979  (1319)      % a machine that is doing nothing, and no wait at all when a message
  1980  (1320)      % actually arrives.
  1981  (1321)      %
  1982  (1322)      % WHAT IS NOT PROVED, AND WHY THE COUNTER BELOW EXISTS: nothing we have
  1983  (1323)      % read says whether the restart flag is CLEARED when TimeOut consumes
  1984  (1324)      % it. If it is sticky, every following TimeOut returns instantly, the
  1985  (1325)      % sleep stops sleeping, and this program burns the machine - which is
  1986  (1326)      % the one outcome that must not happen while nobody is watching.
  1987  (1327)      %
  1988  (1328)      % So the doorbell disarms itself. Every wake with cause -1 that finds
  1989  (1329)      % all three ports still empty counts; a real message resets the count.
  1990  (1330)      % Twenty in a row is not a busy server, it is a flag that will not
  1991  (1331)      % clear - so drop back to flags 0 and the old 100 ms poll, log it, and
  1992  (1332)      % carry on. The product keeps working either way; the log says which
  1993  (1333)      % path it took.
  1994  (1334)      INTEGER  : wakeSpin    := 0
  1995  (1335)      INTEGER  : wakeSpinMax := 20
  1996  (1336)      BOOLEAN  : doorbellOn  := TRUE
  1997  (1337)  
  1998  (1338)      % XMROU, "routed message (via XROUT)", is what msgType carries when a letter
  1999  (1339)      % arrives. It is the ONLY thing that says a SEAT was spent: XROUT takes one
  2000  (1340)      % free connection to forward a letter, before this program has seen a byte
  2001  (1341)      % of it.
  2002  (1342)      %
  2003  (1343)      % SETTLED FROM ND'S OWN SAMPLE SERVERS, both of them, in the COSMOS
  2004  (1344)      % Programmer Guide (ND-60.164.3) appendix of sample programs. Each waits for
  2005  (1345)      % the forwarded letter and then refuses anything else:
  2006  (1346)      %
  2007  (1347)      %   PLANC     IF MSGTYPE >< XMROU THEN
  2008  (1348)      %                 OUTPUT(1, 'A', 'WRONG MESSAGE TYPE ')
  2009  (1349)      %
  2010  (1350)      %   FORTRAN   IF (MSGTYPE .NE. XMROU) THEN
  2011  (1351)      %                 WRITE(1,*)'WRONG MESSAGE TYPE'
  2012  (1352)      %
  2013  (1353)      % Both then read the CLIENT's magic number out of the arrived message to
  2014  (1354)      % answer it directly - which is the other half of the same fact, and the
  2015  (1355)      % reason the sender field is the client rather than XROUT.
  2016  (1356)      %
  2017  (1357)      % The name is used bare here, not declared as a literal, because the PLANC
  2018  (1358)      % sample uses it bare under the same two includes this file has.
  2019  (1359)  
  2020  (1360)      % Was the sender in the room BEFORE this message was handled? The seat
  2021  (1361)      % accounting below needs the answer from before, so it is taken early.
  2022  (1362)      INTEGER  : wasMember
  2023  (1363)      % Is the sender a member AFTER the letter was handled? That single question
  2024  (1364)      % decides whether the seat XROUT spent on the arrival goes back.
  2025  (1365)      INTEGER  : nowMember
  2026  (1366)      % Byte 1 of a routed arrival, kept BEFORE the payload is shifted down. 65
  2027  (1367)      % (XSLET) means a client sent it and a seat was spent; anything else is
  2028  (1368)      % XROUT answering one of our own calls, which spent nothing.
  2029  (1369)      INTEGER  : letterService
  2030  (1370)  
  2031  (1371)      % XSLET (65) IS NOT DECLARED HERE. It is already a CONSTANT in XMP-B02:DEFS
  2032  (1372)      % - "CONSTANT XSLET=65  % Send a letter" - and declaring our own would be a
  2033  (1373)      % redeclaration, which in this compiler is not a warning: it drew
  2034  (1374)      % IDENTIFIER ALREADY SPECIFIED and then ASSERT VIOLATION AT 136747B and took
  2035  (1375)      % the whole batch job with it. Checked against the listing before writing
  2036  (1376)      % this, not assumed.
  2037  (1377)      INTEGER  : remotePortHash
  2038  (1378)      INTEGER  : nBytes
  2039  (1379)      % What XMPFRCV put in nBytes, kept before XMPFMST overwrites it. It is the
  2040  (1380)      % message length normally and the REASON FOR RETURN on an XMTRE.
  2041  (1381)      INTEGER  : receiveStat
  2042  (1382)      INTEGER  : readLength
  2043  (1383)      INTEGER  : readAmount
  2044  (1384)      % An INTEGER dumpAmount clamped the 48-byte arrival dump. Both are gone;
  2045  (1385)      % see the note in the receive loop for what that dump found.
  2046  (1386)      % Where the chat message starts inside a letter that came via XROUT - that
  2047  (1387)      % is, the first byte after the XSLET header. Derived from the header's own
  2048  (1388)      % length word on every arrival, never assumed.
  2049  (1389)      INTEGER  : payloadAt
  2050  (1390)      INTEGER  : writtenLength
  2051  (1391)      INTEGER  : returnStatus
  2052  (1392)      INTEGER4 : senderMagic
  2053  (1393)  
  2054  (1394)      % OUR OWN port's magic number, read once with XMPFP2M after the port opens.
  2055  (1395)      % Kept only so an arrival can be tested against it, because the thing that
  2056  (1396)      % actually happened - our own answer arriving back at this very port - is not
  2057  (1397)      % something any amount of reading the guide would have suggested, and without
  2058  (1398)      % this comparison it cannot be told apart from a real peer's message.
  2059  (1399)      INTEGER4 : myMagic := 0
  2060  (1400)      BOOLEAN  : uniqueName := TRUE
  2061  (1401)  
  2062  (1402)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2063  (1403)      % THE LOG. An RT program has no terminal, so every OUTPUT(1, ...) in this
  2064  (1404)      % file goes NOWHERE once CHATSER is started with @RT.
  2065  (1405)      %
  2066  (1406)      % That is not a tidiness problem, it is why a whole afternoon was spent
  2067  (1407)      % guessing on 2026-08-20. Two clients sat in one room and neither heard
  2068  (1408)      % the other; the one line that would have said why - "SV: snd failed st="
  2069  (1409)      % from sendTo - was printed to a device that is not there. The fault was
  2070  (1410)      % looked for in the room table, the wire, the seat counter and the build,
  2071  (1411)      % in that order, because the program's own account of itself was thrown
  2072  (1412)      % away.
  2073  (1413)      %
  2074  (1414)      % SO EVERY DIAGNOSTIC GOES TO A FILE AS WELL AS THE TERMINAL. Run as
  2075  (1415)      % @CHATSV you see it on screen AND in the file; run as @RT CHATSER the
  2076  (1416)      % screen half goes nowhere and the file is all there is.
  2077  (1417)      %
  2078  (1418)      % OPEN, WRITE, CLOSE PER LINE - and that is deliberate, not laziness.
  2079  (1419)      % A crash leaves an open file's block buffer unwritten, so anything still
  2080  (1420)      % in it is lost - which is exactly the moment the log has to survive. One
  2081  (1421)      % line per open costs a file system round trip, and these lines are only
  2082  (1422)      % written on errors and at startup, so the cost is paid when something is
  2083  (1423)      % already wrong.
  2084  (1424)      %
  2085  (1425)      % ACCESS 5 IS SEQUENTIAL WRITE APPEND, from the OpenFile table in
  2086  (1426)      % ND-860228-2-EN. Access 0 would truncate at CLOSE and each line would
  2087  (1427)      % erase the one before it - and it truncates at CLOSE rather than at OPEN,
  2088  (1428)      % which is the trap CHAT.PLNC already carries a note about.
  2089  (1429)      %
  2090  (1430)      % AN RT PROGRAM'S DEFAULT USER IS RT, NOT SYSTEM. The file is named
  2091  (1431)      % without a user, so it resolves against RT first and then SYSTEM. If it
  2092  (1432)      % is never created, logging simply does nothing - see logBroken.
  2093  (1433)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2094  (1434)      % CHATLG2, NOT CHATLOG, AND THE REASON MATTERS. The first log this server
  2095  (1435)      % ever wrote went out as plain 7-bit ASCII, before logPut existed. Access 5
  2096  (1436)      % APPENDS, so the parity-correct lines written afterwards landed BEHIND 107
  2097  (1437)      % bytes SINTRAN's own utilities refuse to read, and LIST-FILE stopped at the
  2098  (1438)      % first of them without a word - the whole file reads as empty. The file
  2099  (1439)      % belongs to user RT and SYSTEM has no directory access to delete it, so the
  2100  (1440)      % only way out is a name that has never been written the wrong way.
  2101  (1441)      % Measured on D100, 2026-08-20.
  2102  (1442)      BYTES   : logName := 'CHATLG2'
  2103  (1443)      BYTES   : logType := 'SYMB'
  2104  (1444)  
  2105  (1445)      % Sequential write APPEND. See above - 0 would truncate.
  2106  (1446)      INTEGER : logAppend := 5
  2107  (1447)  
  2108  (1448)      % Set once the log has failed, and never cleared. A server that cannot
  2109  (1449)      % write its log must not spend a file system call per diagnostic finding
  2110  (1450)      % that out again, and must certainly not fail because of it.
  2111  (1451)      BOOLEAN : logBroken := FALSE
  2112  (1452)  
  2113  (1453)      % Digits, LEAST significant first as they are produced, written out in
  2114  (1454)      % reverse. Ten is enough for any 16-bit value with room to spare.
  2115  (1455)      BYTES   : logDigits(0:9)
  2116  (1456)  
  2117  (1457)      % Single characters the log writes, as NUMBERS. A carriage return has no
  2118  (1458)      % spelling inside a PLANC string literal, and CHAT.PLNC already carries
  2119  (1459)      % its carriageRet the same way, so all three are written alike rather
  2120  (1460)      % than two one way and one the other.
  2121  (1461)      INTEGER : logMinus := 45
  2122  (1462)      INTEGER : logCr    := 13
  2123  (1463)      INTEGER : logLf    := 10
  2124  (1464)  
  2125  (1465)      % The full filespec, for the create. CreateFile takes the WHOLE thing in
  2126  (1466)      % one 64-byte buffer, unlike MON50 which takes name and type separately.
  2127  (1467)      BYTES   : logFull     := 'CHATLG2:SYMB'
  2128  (1468)      INTEGER : lenLogFull  := 12
  2129  (1469)      INTEGER : sizeNameBuf := 64
  2130  (1470)      BYTES   : nameBuf(0:63)
  2131  (1471)      BYTES   : blankByte   := ' '
  2132  (1472)      INTEGER4 : startAddr  := 0
  2133  (1473)      INTEGER4 : noOfPages  := 0
  2134  (1474)  
  2135  (1475)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2136  (1476)      % Ask SINTRAN to create CHATLOG:SYMB.
  2137  (1477)      %
  2138  (1478)      % MON50 CANNOT CREATE A FILE - measured in CHAT.PLNC, where quoting the
  2139  (1479)      % name did nothing at all. CreateFile is 221B and it is a separate call.
  2140  (1480)      % It has no MONn routine, which is why it goes through MONITOR_CALL; that
  2141  (1481)      % works here because MON-CALL-1B-A00 supplies 5MON_P, and this program
  2142  (1482)      % already relies on it for TimeOut.
  2143  (1483)      %
  2144  (1484)      % The buffer is filled with SPACES first and the filespec copied over the
  2145  (1485)      % front, so whatever was in the rest is never read as part of the name.
  2146  (1486)      % Both size parameters are zero: an ordinary indexed file that grows as
  2147  (1487)      % it is written, which is what a log wants.
  2148  (1488)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2149  (1489)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID : makeLog
  2150  (1490)          INTEGER : i
  2151  (1491)          FOR i IN 1:sizeNameBuf DO
  2152  (1492)              blankByte(0) =: nameBuf(i - 1)
  2153  (1493)          ENDFOR
  2154  (1494)          FOR i IN 1:lenLogFull DO
  2155  (1495)              logFull(i - 1) =: nameBuf(i - 1)
  2156  (1496)          ENDFOR
  2157  (1497)          MONITOR_CALL(221B, nameBuf, startAddr, noOfPages)
  2158  (1498)      ENDROUTINE
  2159  (1499)  
  2160  (1500)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2161  (1501)      % Open the log for append, creating it the first time.
  2162  (1502)      %
  2163  (1503)      % Returns the device number, or 0 when the log is not usable. THE CALLER
  2164  (1504)      % MUST TEST THE BOOLEAN, not the number - a failed MON50 leaves 46 in it,
  2165  (1505)      % SINTRAN's "no such file", which is the defect CHAT.PLNC records at
  2166  (1506)      % length. This routine turns that into a plain 0 so no caller can repeat
  2167  (1507)      % it.
  2168  (1508)      %
  2169  (1509)      % THE FLAG IS CLEARED AFTER THE ON BLOCK, NOT BEFORE. Measured in
  2170  (1510)      % CHAT.PLNC on 2026-08-19: cleared before, the handler body had already
  2171  (1511)      % run and every open reported failure while returning a good device.
  2172  (1512)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2173  (1513)      ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER : logOpen
  2174  (1514)          INTEGER : dev
  2175  (1515)          BOOLEAN : openFailed
  2176  (1516)  
  2177  (1517)          IF logBroken THEN
  2178  (1518)              0 RETURN
  2179  (1519)          ENDIF
  2180  (1520)  
  2181  (1521)          0 =: dev
  2182  (1522)          ON ROUTINEERROR DO
  2183  (1523)              TRUE =: openFailed
  2184  (1524)          ENDON
  2185  (1525)          FALSE =: openFailed
  2186  (1526)  
  2187  (1527)          MON50(logName, logType, logAppend) =: dev
  2188  (1528)  
  2189  (1529)          IF openFailed THEN
  2190  (1530)              % Not there yet. Make it and open it for real. A create that
  2191  (1531)              % fails needs no message of its own - the second open fails too
  2192  (1532)              % and that is what sets logBroken.
  2193  (1533)              ON ROUTINEERROR DO
  2194  (1534)              ENDON
  2195  (1535)              makeLog
  2196  (1536)  
  2197  (1537)              ON ROUTINEERROR DO
  2198  (1538)                  TRUE =: openFailed
  2199  (1539)              ENDON
  2200  (1540)              FALSE =: openFailed
  2201  (1541)              MON50(logName, logType, logAppend) =: dev
  2202  (1542)          ENDIF
  2203  (1543)  
  2204  (1544)          IF openFailed THEN
  2205  (1545)              TRUE =: logBroken
  2206  (1546)              0 RETURN
  2207  (1547)          ENDIF
  2208  (1548)  
  2209  (1549)          dev RETURN
  2210  (1550)      ENDROUTINE
  2211  (1551)  
  2212  (1552)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2213  (1553)      % Write one line: a tag, a number, and an end of line.
  2214  (1554)      %
  2215  (1555)      % The tag is passed as a slice of a BYTES with its length, the same shape
  2216  (1556)      % every other routine here uses, because PLANC's OUTPUT needs the width in
  2217  (1557)      % the format string and a routine cannot have a literal one.
  2218  (1558)      %
  2219  (1559)      % NOTHING HERE IS ALLOWED TO KILL THE SERVER. Every failure sets the flag
  2220  (1560)      % and returns. A logger that can take down the thing it is watching is
  2221  (1561)      % worse than no logger.
  2222  (1562)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2223  (1563)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2224  (1564)      % Write one byte to the log. PLAIN 7-BIT ASCII, and the parity version of
  2225  (1565)      % this routine has been TAKEN OUT.
  2226  (1566)      %
  2227  (1567)      % IT USED TO SET EVEN PARITY IN BIT 7, on a conclusion that was WRONG. The
  2228  (1568)      % reasoning was: the log filled up correctly - 107 bytes, five opens, every
  2229  (1569)      % size right - and then
  2230  (1570)      %
  2231  (1571)      %   @LIST-FILE SVLOG:SYMB,,     printed the header and NOTHING ELSE
  2232  (1572)      %   @QED  R SVLOG:SYMB          PARITY ERROR AT LINE 1, thirteen times
  2233  (1573)      %
  2234  (1574)      % which reads as "the bytes are there and the tools refuse them", so the
  2235  (1575)      % bytes must be at fault.
  2236  (1576)      %
  2237  (1577)      % THE CONTROL RUN THAT WAS NEVER DONE. The same two commands against
  2238  (1578)      % CHATSV:MODE - the build file @MODE compiles this whole server from, which
  2239  (1579)      % is unquestionably good - answer EXACTLY THE SAME WAY: LIST-FILE prints the
  2240  (1580)      % header and nothing else, QED cries PARITY ERROR thirteen times. Both tools
  2241  (1581)      % do this to every file our FA push puts on the machine. They were never
  2242  (1582)      % evidence about the log at all.
  2243  (1583)      %
  2244  (1584)      % And the parity code did not even do what it claimed: measured on the wire,
  2245  (1585)      % EVERY byte came back with bit 7 set - S as 0xD3, T as 0xD4, space as 0xA0,
  2246  (1586)      % CR as 0x8D - so the "is the count odd" test was true every time. It was
  2247  (1587)      % wrong code justifying itself with a wrong measurement.
  2248  (1588)      %
  2249  (1589)      % TO READ THIS LOG, PULL IT OFF THE MACHINE AND LOOK AT THE BYTES:
  2250  (1590)      %
  2251  (1591)      %   @COPY-FILE "SVLOG2:SYMB",(RT)CHATLG2:SYMB     (RT owns it; SYSTEM cannot
  2252  (1592)      %                                                  delete or chmod it)
  2253  (1593)      %   Xmsg.Live.Runner --config topology-d19999-hdlc-server.json \
  2254  (1594)      %       --pull 'SVLOG2:SYMB' --pull-from 100 --pull-to svlog2.raw \
  2255  (1595)      %       --originate-from-seed
  2256  (1596)      %
  2257  (1597)      % That works, and it is how the broadcast question was finally answered.
  2258  (1598)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2259  (1599)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER, INTEGER) : logPut(dev, value)
  2260  (1600)          BYTE : b
  2261  (1601)  
  2262  (1602)          % Masked to seven bits so a stray high bit in a caller's value cannot
  2263  (1603)          % put a character in the file that nothing will render.
  2264  (1604)          value AND 127 =: b
  2265  (1605)          b MON2(dev)
  2266  (1606)      ENDROUTINE
  2267  (1607)  
  2268  (1608)      % The tag length is DERIVED, not passed - see buildAdmText for the whole
  2269  (1609)      % argument. Nineteen callers used to type the count beside the literal.
  2270  (1610)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID (BYTES, INTEGER) : logLine(tag, value)
  2271  (1611)          INTEGER : dev, i, left, rest, digit, digitCount, tagLen
  2272  (1612)          BYTE    : b
  2273  (1613)          BOOLEAN : writeFailed
  2274  (1614)  
  2275  (1615)          MAXINDEX(tag, 1) + 1 =: tagLen
  2276  (1616)          logOpen =: dev
  2277  (1617)          IF dev = 0 THEN
  2278  (1618)              RETURN
  2279  (1619)          ENDIF
  2280  (1620)  
  2281  (1621)          % ONE FLAG FOR THE WHOLE LINE, not a message per byte. CHAT.PLNC
  2282  (1622)          % learned that the hard way: a handler round a write loop that prints
  2283  (1623)          % inside it says the same thing once per character.
  2284  (1624)          ON ROUTINEERROR DO
  2285  (1625)              TRUE =: writeFailed
  2286  (1626)          ENDON
  2287  (1627)          FALSE =: writeFailed
  2288  (1628)  
  2289  (1629)          FOR i IN 1:tagLen DO
  2290  (1630)              tag(i - 1) =: b
  2291  (1631)              logPut(dev, b)
  2292  (1632)          ENDFOR
  2293  (1633)  
  2294  (1634)          % The number, most significant digit first. Built backwards into
  2295  (1635)          % logDigits and then written forwards, because dividing gives the
  2296  (1636)          % LAST digit first and a log line read by a person wants them the
  2297  (1637)          % other way round.
  2298  (1638)          %
  2299  (1639)          % Negative values are printed with a minus and then their magnitude.
  2300  (1640)          % A status word can be negative on this wire and "-34" is readable
  2301  (1641)          % where the unsigned form is not.
  2302  (1642)          value =: left
  2303  (1643)          IF left < 0 THEN
  2304  (1644)              logPut(dev, logMinus)
  2305  (1645)              0 - left =: left
  2306  (1646)          ENDIF
  2307  (1647)  
  2308  (1648)          % NO "MOD" - it appears nowhere in this file and is not going to be
  2309  (1649)          % introduced on a guess. putNumber above gets its digits by dividing
  2310  (1650)          % and subtracting, so this does the same.
  2311  (1651)          0 =: digitCount
  2312  (1652)          % A do-while shape, so zero prints as "0" rather than as nothing.
  2313  (1653)          DO
  2314  (1654)              left / 10 =: rest
  2315  (1655)              left - rest * 10 =: digit
  2316  (1656)              zeroByte(0) + digit =: logDigits(digitCount)
  2317  (1657)              digitCount + 1 =: digitCount
  2318  (1658)              rest =: left
  2319  (1659)              WHILE left > 0
  2320  (1660)          ENDDO
  2321  (1661)  
  2322  (1662)          FOR i IN 1:digitCount DO
  2323  (1663)              logDigits(digitCount - i) =: b
  2324  (1664)              logPut(dev, b)
  2325  (1665)          ENDFOR
  2326  (1666)  
  2327  (1667)          % THE CARRIAGE RETURN MATTERS - a SINTRAN text file is expected to
  2328  (1668)          % have one, and without it every line runs into the next. It carries
  2329  (1669)          % parity like every other byte; QED counts it as part of the line.
  2330  (1670)          logPut(dev, logCr)
  2331  (1671)          logPut(dev, logLf)
  2332  (1672)  
  2333  (1673)          % CLOSE, ALWAYS, including after a failed write - see the note on the
  2334  (1674)          % open. An unclosed file keeps its old contents.
  2335  (1675)          MON43(dev)
  2336  (1676)  
  2337  (1677)          IF writeFailed THEN
  2338  (1678)              TRUE =: logBroken
  2339  (1679)          ENDIF
  2340  (1680)      ENDROUTINE
  2341  (1681)  
  2342  (1682)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2343  (1683)      % Compare a name in the room against one in a buffer, ignoring case.
  2344  (1684)      %
  2345  (1685)      % ChatRoom.cs compares ignoring case, so this does too - the two doors into
  2346  (1686)      % one room must not disagree about whether ANNA and anna are the same
  2347  (1687)      % person.
  2348  (1688)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2349  (1689)      ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (INTEGER, INTEGER, INTEGER) : sameName(slot, at, length)
  2350  (1690)          INTEGER : j, a, b
  2351  (1691)          IF memberLen(slot) >< length THEN
  2352  (1692)              0 RETURN
  2353  (1693)          ENDIF
  2354  (1694)          FOR j IN 1:length DO
  2355  (1695)              memberName(slot, j) =: a
  2356  (1696)              inBuf(at + j - 1) =: b
  2357  (1697)              IF a > 96 THEN a - 32 =: a ENDIF        % fold lower case to upper
  2358  (1698)              IF b > 96 THEN b - 32 =: b ENDIF
  2359  (1699)              IF a >< b THEN
  2360  (1700)                  0 RETURN
  2361  (1701)              ENDIF
  2362  (1702)          ENDFOR
  2363  (1703)          1 RETURN
  2364  (1704)      ENDROUTINE
  2365  (1705)  
  2366  (1706)      ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (INTEGER4) : findByMagic(magic)
  2367  (1707)          INTEGER : i
  2368  (1708)          FOR i IN 1:maxSeats DO
  2369  (1709)              IF memberUsed(i) >< 0 THEN
  2370  (1710)                  IF memberMagic(i) = magic THEN
  2371  (1711)                      i RETURN
  2372  (1712)                  ENDIF
  2373  (1713)              ENDIF
  2374  (1714)          ENDFOR
  2375  (1715)          0 RETURN
  2376  (1716)      ENDROUTINE
  2377  (1717)  
  2378  (1718)      ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (INTEGER, INTEGER) : findByName(at, length)
  2379  (1719)          INTEGER : i
  2380  (1720)          FOR i IN 1:maxSeats DO
  2381  (1721)              IF memberUsed(i) >< 0 THEN
  2382  (1722)                  IF sameName(i, at, length) = 1 THEN
  2383  (1723)                      i RETURN
  2384  (1724)                  ENDIF
  2385  (1725)              ENDIF
  2386  (1726)          ENDFOR
  2387  (1727)          0 RETURN
  2388  (1728)      ENDROUTINE
  2389  (1729)  
  2390  (1730)      ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER : freeSlot
  2391  (1731)          INTEGER : i
  2392  (1732)          FOR i IN 1:maxSeats DO
  2393  (1733)              IF memberUsed(i) = 0 THEN
  2394  (1734)                  i RETURN
  2395  (1735)              ENDIF
  2396  (1736)          ENDFOR
  2397  (1737)          0 RETURN
  2398  (1738)      ENDROUTINE
  2399  (1739)  
  2400  (1740)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2401  (1741)      % Build a message into outBuf. Returns its total length.
  2402  (1742)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2403  (1743)      ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (INTEGER, INTEGER, INTEGER, INTEGER, INTEGER) : &
  2404  (1744)              buildMessage(kind, nameAt, nameLength, textAt, textLength)
  2405  (1745)          INTEGER : i, at
  2406  (1746)          kind =: outBuf(0)
  2407  (1747)          nameLength =: outBuf(1)
  2408  (1748)          2 =: at
  2409  (1749)          FOR i IN 1:nameLength DO
  2410  (1750)              inBuf(nameAt + i - 1) =: outBuf(at)
  2411  (1751)              at + 1 =: at
  2412  (1752)          ENDFOR
  2413  (1753)          0 =: outBuf(at)                       % text length, high byte
  2414  (1754)          textLength =: outBuf(at + 1)          % text length, low byte
  2415  (1755)          at + 2 =: at
  2416  (1756)          FOR i IN 1:textLength DO
  2417  (1757)              inBuf(textAt + i - 1) =: outBuf(at)
  2418  (1758)              at + 1 =: at
  2419  (1759)          ENDFOR
  2420  (1760)          at RETURN
  2421  (1761)      ENDROUTINE
  2422  (1762)  
  2423  (1763)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2424  (1764)      % Print a name that arrived in inBuf, one byte at a time.
  2425  (1765)      %
  2426  (1766)      % Byte by byte because 'ALn' formats a STRING: handing it a single byte
  2427  (1767)      % prints the byte's NUMBER, not the character. A one-element BYTES is the
  2428  (1768)      % way across, the same trick the space separator in buildWho uses.
  2429  (1769)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2430  (1770)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2431  (1771)      % Write a small number into outBuf as decimal digits, advancing at.
  2432  (1772)      %
  2433  (1773)      % By hand because there is no formatter that writes into a BYTES array -
  2434  (1774)      % OUTPUT goes to a device, not a buffer. Handles 0..999, which covers a
  2435  (1775)      % seat count and every counter this status line is likely to grow.
  2436  (1776)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2437  (1777)      ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (INTEGER, INTEGER) : putNumber(value, at)
  2438  (1778)          INTEGER : left, digit, p
  2439  (1779)          BOOLEAN : started
  2440  (1780)  
  2441  (1781)          % RETURNS THE NEW CURSOR rather than writing through an INTEGER WRITE
  2442  (1782)          % parameter. The WRITE form was assumed to advance the CALLER's variable
  2443  (1783)          % and did not: the status line came out "SEATS 016" instead of
  2444  (1784)          % "SEATS 0/16" - the separator was written where the previous digit had
  2445  (1785)          % gone, and the length came out short. MEASURED on D100 2026-08-20.
  2446  (1786)          %
  2447  (1787)          % Returning the cursor is the idiom buildMessage and buildWho already
  2448  (1788)          % use in this file ("at RETURN"), and it cannot be got wrong by the
  2449  (1789)          % caller in the same silent way.
  2450  (1790)          at =: p
  2451  (1791)          value =: left
  2452  (1792)          FALSE =: started
  2453  (1793)  
  2454  (1794)          % Leading digits are skipped until a non-zero one appears, so 7
  2455  (1795)          % prints as "7" and not "00007". Zero itself still has to print, which
  2456  (1796)          % the final digit below always does.
  2457  (1797)          %
  2458  (1798)          % FIVE DIGITS, NOT THREE. Until 2026-09-02 this started at the hundreds,
  2459  (1799)          % and a counter past 999 printed its "hundreds digit" as value/100 -
  2460  (1800)          % 63 for 6345 - added to '0', which is the letter 'o'. STATUS on D100
  2461  (1801)          % read "empty o45" and, an hour later, "empty r36": a climbing count
  2462  (1802)          % disguised as a mystery letter. An INTEGER is 16-bit signed, so 32767
  2463  (1803)          % is the most this can ever be asked to print, and five digits cover it.
  2464  (1804)          left / 10000 =: digit
  2465  (1805)          IF digit > 0 THEN
  2466  (1806)              zeroByte(0) + digit =: outBuf(p)
  2467  (1807)              p + 1 =: p
  2468  (1808)              TRUE =: started
  2469  (1809)              left - digit * 10000 =: left
  2470  (1810)          ENDIF
  2471  (1811)  
  2472  (1812)          left / 1000 =: digit
  2473  (1813)          IF digit > 0 OR started THEN
  2474  (1814)              zeroByte(0) + digit =: outBuf(p)
  2475  (1815)              p + 1 =: p
  2476  (1816)              TRUE =: started
  2477  (1817)              left - digit * 1000 =: left
  2478  (1818)          ENDIF
  2479  (1819)  
  2480  (1820)          left / 100 =: digit
  2481  (1821)          IF digit > 0 OR started THEN
  2482  (1822)              zeroByte(0) + digit =: outBuf(p)
  2483  (1823)              p + 1 =: p
  2484  (1824)              TRUE =: started
  2485  (1825)              left - digit * 100 =: left
  2486  (1826)          ENDIF
  2487  (1827)  
  2488  (1828)          left / 10 =: digit
  2489  (1829)          IF digit > 0 OR started THEN
  2490  (1830)              zeroByte(0) + digit =: outBuf(p)
  2491  (1831)              p + 1 =: p
  2492  (1832)              left - digit * 10 =: left
  2493  (1833)          ENDIF
  2494  (1834)  
  2495  (1835)          zeroByte(0) + left =: outBuf(p)
  2496  (1836)          p + 1 =: p
  2497  (1837)          p RETURN
  2498  (1838)      ENDROUTINE
  2499  (1839)  
  2500  (1840)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2501  (1841)      % Write a system number into peerName and return the new cursor.
  2502  (1842)      %
  2503  (1843)      % A SEPARATE ROUTINE FROM putNumber, and not a tidiness failure: putNumber
  2504  (1844)      % writes into outBuf, which is the message being built. A system name is not
  2505  (1845)      % part of any message - it goes to XMPBLET - so it needs its own buffer, and
  2506  (1846)      % a routine that took the buffer as a parameter would be handing a BYTES
  2507  (1847)      % formal parameter around, which this compiler is fussy about in exactly the
  2508  (1848)      % area that already cost three cycles here.
  2509  (1849)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2510  (1850)      % =======================================================================
  2511  (1851)      % WHAT TO CALL A MACHINE
  2512  (1852)      %
  2513  (1853)      % The name if we know one, and D<number> if we do not. Four places used to
  2514  (1854)      % write the D and the digits by hand and they are now all this routine, so
  2515  (1855)      % a machine cannot be called one thing in /who and another in the chat.
  2516  (1856)      %
  2517  (1857)      % THE NUMBER IS STILL WHAT DECIDES WHICH MACHINE IT IS. A name is only a
  2518  (1858)      % LABEL looked up from that number, and the number comes from the kernel's
  2519  (1859)      % own magic, never from anything a sender claims. So a machine can choose
  2520  (1860)      % what it is called and still cannot claim to BE another machine - which is
  2521  (1861)      % the property the trunk protocol was built around and must not lose.
  2522  (1862)      %
  2523  (1863)      % THE PEER SCAN IS WRITTEN OUT HERE rather than calling findPeer, and that
  2524  (1864)      % is not laziness: findPeer is declared 1800 lines further down, and PLANC
  2525  (1865)      % wants a routine declared before it is called. Two of the four callers sit
  2526  (1866)      % above it.
  2527  (1867)      % =======================================================================
  2528  (1868)      ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (INTEGER, INTEGER) : putSysLabel(sysNum, at)
  2529  (1869)          INTEGER : p, i, j, row
  2530  (1870)  
  2531  (1871)          at =: p
  2532  (1872)  
  2533  (1873)          IF sysNum = mySystem THEN
  2534  (1874)              IF lenSysName > 0 THEN
  2535  (1875)                  FOR i IN 1:lenSysName DO
  2536  (1876)                      sysName(i - 1) =: outBuf(p)
  2537  (1877)                      p + 1 =: p
  2538  (1878)                  ENDFOR
  2539  (1879)                  p RETURN
  2540  (1880)              ENDIF
  2541  (1881)          ELSE
  2542  (1882)              0 =: row
  2543  (1883)              FOR i IN 1:maxPeers DO
  2544  (1884)                  IF peerSystem(i) = sysNum THEN
  2545  (1885)                      IF row = 0 THEN
  2546  (1886)                          i =: row
  2547  (1887)                      ENDIF
  2548  (1888)                  ENDIF
  2549  (1889)              ENDFOR
  2550  (1890)              IF row > 0 THEN
  2551  (1891)                  IF pNameLen(row) > 0 THEN
  2552  (1892)                      FOR j IN 1:pNameLen(row) DO
  2553  (1893)                          pName(row, j) =: outBuf(p)
  2554  (1894)                          p + 1 =: p
  2555  (1895)                      ENDFOR
  2556  (1896)                      p RETURN
  2557  (1897)                  ENDIF
  2558  (1898)              ENDIF
  2559  (1899)          ENDIF
  2560  (1900)  
  2561  (1901)          % Nobody has said what it is called. The number is what the kernel
  2562  (1902)          % vouches for, so that is what gets printed.
  2563  (1903)          dByte(0) =: outBuf(p)
  2564  (1904)          p + 1 =: p
  2565  (1905)          putNumber(sysNum, p) =: p
  2566  (1906)          p RETURN
  2567  (1907)      ENDROUTINE
  2568  (1908)  
  2569  (1909)      ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (INTEGER, INTEGER) : putPeerDigits(value, at)
  2570  (1910)          INTEGER : left, digit, p
  2571  (1911)          BOOLEAN : started
  2572  (1912)  
  2573  (1913)          at =: p
  2574  (1914)          value =: left
  2575  (1915)          FALSE =: started
  2576  (1916)  
  2577  (1917)          left / 10000 =: digit
  2578  (1918)          IF digit > 0 THEN
  2579  (1919)              zeroByte(0) + digit =: peerName(p)
  2580  (1920)              p + 1 =: p
  2581  (1921)              TRUE =: started
  2582  (1922)              left - digit * 10000 =: left
  2583  (1923)          ENDIF
  2584  (1924)  
  2585  (1925)          left / 1000 =: digit
  2586  (1926)          IF digit > 0 OR started THEN
  2587  (1927)              zeroByte(0) + digit =: peerName(p)
  2588  (1928)              p + 1 =: p
  2589  (1929)              TRUE =: started
  2590  (1930)              left - digit * 1000 =: left
  2591  (1931)          ENDIF
  2592  (1932)  
  2593  (1933)          left / 100 =: digit
  2594  (1934)          IF digit > 0 OR started THEN
  2595  (1935)              zeroByte(0) + digit =: peerName(p)
  2596  (1936)              p + 1 =: p
  2597  (1937)              TRUE =: started
  2598  (1938)              left - digit * 100 =: left
  2599  (1939)          ENDIF
  2600  (1940)  
  2601  (1941)          left / 10 =: digit
  2602  (1942)          IF digit > 0 OR started THEN
  2603  (1943)              zeroByte(0) + digit =: peerName(p)
  2604  (1944)              p + 1 =: p
  2605  (1945)              left - digit * 10 =: left
  2606  (1946)          ENDIF
  2607  (1947)  
  2608  (1948)          zeroByte(0) + left =: peerName(p)
  2609  (1949)          p + 1 =: p
  2610  (1950)          p RETURN
  2611  (1951)      ENDROUTINE
  2612  (1952)  
  2613  (1953)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2614  (1954)      % Write a fixed word into outBuf and return the new cursor.
  2615  (1955)      %
  2616  (1956)      % Same "at RETURN" idiom as putNumber, and for the same reason: a WRITE
  2617  (1957)      % parameter looks like it advances the caller's cursor and does not.
  2618  (1958)      %
  2619  (1959)      % SUBSCRIPT FROM ZERO - a BYTES from a literal starts at index 0. See
  2620  (1960)      % buildAdmText.
  2621  (1961)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2622  (1962)      % The length is DERIVED, not passed - see buildAdmText for the whole
  2623  (1963)      % argument. Twenty-nine callers used to type the count beside the literal.
  2624  (1964)      ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER (BYTES, INTEGER) : putWord(text, at)
  2625  (1965)          INTEGER : i, p, textLen
  2626  (1966)          MAXINDEX(text, 1) + 1 =: textLen
  2627  (1967)          at =: p
  2628  (1968)          FOR i IN 1:textLen DO
  2629  (1969)              text(i - 1) =: outBuf(p)
  2630  (1970)              p + 1 =: p
  2631  (1971)          ENDFOR
  2632  (1972)          p RETURN
  2633  (1973)      ENDROUTINE
  2634  (1974)  
  2635  (1975)      ROUTINE VOID, VOID (INTEGER, INTEGER) : showName(nameAt, nameLength)
  2636  (1976)          INTEGER : i
  2637  (1977)          FOR i IN 1:nameLength DO
  2638  (1978)              inBuf(nameAt + i - 1) =: oneByte
  2639  (1979)              OUTPUT(1, 'AL1', oneByte)
  2640  (1980)          ENDFOR
  2641  (1981)      ENDROUTINE
  2642  (1982)  
  2643  (1983)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2644  (1984)      % Build the answer to an AdminStatus: how the room stands, as readable
  2645  (1985)      % text.
  2646  (1986)      %
  2647  (1987)      % TEXT, NOT A STRUCT OF COUNTERS, and that is a decision rather than
  2648  (1988)      % laziness. What is worth reporting grows with every phase of federation -
  2649  (1989)      % peers, trunk states, hop counts - and a text answer lets it grow without
  2650  (1990)      % changing the wire and re-pinning the golden bytes each time.
  2651  (1991)      %
  2652  (1992)      % The name field is EMPTY: the server is answering, not a member.
  2653  (1993)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2654  (1994)      ROUTINE VOID, INTEGER : buildStatus
  2655  (1995)          INTEGER : i, j, at, seated, textAt, textLen
  2656  (1996)          % kbTotal counts BLOCKS while it is being summed and is turned into KB
  2657  (1997)          % once, at the end. held is how many messages a room actually has.
  2658  (1998)          INTEGER : kbTotal, held
  2659  (1999)  
  2660  (2000)          0 =: seated
  2661  (2001)          FOR i IN 1:maxSeats DO
  2662  (2002)              IF memberUsed(i) = 1 THEN
  2663  (2003)                  seated + 1 =: seated
  2664  (2004)              ENDIF
  2665  (2005)          ENDFOR
  2666  (2006)  
  2667  (2007)          kAdmRepl =: outBuf(0)
  2668  (2008)          0 =: outBuf(1)                        % no name: the SERVER is answering
  2669  (2009)          2 =: at
  2670  (2010)          0 =: outBuf(at)                       % text length, high byte
  2671  (2011)          0 =: outBuf(at + 1)                   % text length, low byte, filled below
  2672  (2012)          at + 2 =: textAt
  2673  (2013)          textAt =: at
  2674  (2014)  
  2675  (2015)          % "SEATS n/m" - taken and offered. Two numbers, because "3 seats used"
  2676  (2016)          % does not say whether the room is full, and full is the case an
  2677  (2017)          % operator is asking about.
  2678  (2018)          FOR j IN 0:5 DO
  2679  (2019)              statusWord(j) =: outBuf(at)
  2680  (2020)              at + 1 =: at
  2681  (2021)          ENDFOR
  2682  (2022)          putNumber(seated, at) =: at
  2683  (2023)          slashByte(0) =: outBuf(at)
  2684  (2024)          at + 1 =: at
  2685  (2025)          putNumber(maxSeats, at) =: at
  2686  (2026)  
  2687  (2027)          % THIS SERVER'S OWN BUILD - beside the seat count so a stale client
  2688  (2028)          % and a fresh server show up together in one command, not two
  2689  (2029)          % FILE-STATISTICS calls compared by eye.
  2690  (2030)          putWord('  build ', at) =: at
  2691  (2031)          FOR j IN 1:lenServerVer DO
  2692  (2032)              serverVersion(j - 1) =: outBuf(at)
  2693  (2033)              at + 1 =: at
  2694  (2034)          ENDFOR
  2695  (2035)  
  2696  (2036)          % ---- AND WHAT THE HISTORY IS DOING ---------------------------------
  2697  (2037)          %
  2698  (2038)          % Ronny asked for three things here, 2026-08-23: what each room keeps
  2699  (2039)          % and holds, what a NEW room would get, and how much disk it all comes
  2700  (2040)          % to. He chose reporting over a total that refuses - eight rooms at the
  2701  (2041)          % 200 ceiling is 800KB, which is nothing on this disk, and a second
  2702  (2042)          % limit is a second thing that can turn down a reasonable request.
  2703  (2043)          %
  2704  (2044)          %   SEATS 2/16  default 50  disk 31KB  LOBBY 50/12 GENERAL 20/3
  2705  (2045)          %
  2706  (2046)          % BLOCKS ARE COUNTED, NOT FILE SIZES. A ring's file is its full size
  2707  (2047)          % from the day it is made, so asking the file system would report what
  2708  (2048)          % was RESERVED. What an operator wants to know is what it will cost,
  2709  (2049)          % and that is size x 512 whether the ring is full yet or not.
  2710  (2050)          %
  2711  (2051)          % HELD is "how many are actually in there" - histNext when the ring has
  2712  (2052)          % not wrapped, the size once it has. Two numbers because 50/12 says a
  2713  (2053)          % room is a quarter full, where 12 alone does not say what of.
  2714  (2054)          putWord('  default ', at) =: at
  2715  (2055)          putNumber(histDefault, at) =: at
  2716  (2056)  
  2717  (2057)          0 =: kbTotal
  2718  (2058)          FOR i IN 1:maxHistRooms DO
  2719  (2059)              IF topicRmLen(i) > 0 THEN
  2720  (2060)                  kbTotal + histSize(i) =: kbTotal
  2721  (2061)              ENDIF
  2722  (2062)          ENDFOR
  2723  (2063)          putWord('  disk ', at) =: at
  2724  (2064)          putNumber(kbTotal / 2, at) =: at        % blocks of 512 bytes -> KB
  2725  (2065)          putWord('KB', at) =: at
  2726  (2066)  
  2727  (2067)          % ---- THE THREE COUNTS THAT USED TO BE A FLOOD ----------------------
  2728  (2068)          % empty  routed letters with no payload. One per seat returned, because
  2729  (2069)          %        XROUT's answer comes back on our own port looking like that.
  2730  (2070)          %        Climbing while the room is quiet is the seat feedback loop.
  2731  (2071)          % bounce messages that came home with nobody there - dead clients.
  2732  (2072)          % refuse joins turned away, whatever the reason.
  2733  (2073)          % relay  lines passed ON to a further peer. A machine in the MIDDLE of
  2734  (2074)          %        a chain shows this climbing; an END machine shows it flat at
  2735  (2075)          %        zero, so a non-zero count on an end machine means the topology
  2736  (2076)          %        is not what somebody believes it is.
  2737  (2077)          %
  2738  (2078)          % Before this they printed a line each, every time, and the terminal
  2739  (2079)          % filled with the two that are expected. A number you can ask for beats
  2740  (2080)          % a line you cannot avoid.
  2741  (2081)          putWord('  empty ', at) =: at
  2742  (2082)          putNumber(nEmptyLet, at) =: at
  2743  (2083)          putWord(' bounce ', at) =: at
  2744  (2084)          putNumber(nBounced, at) =: at
  2745  (2085)          putWord(' relay ', at) =: at
  2746  (2086)          putNumber(nRelayed, at) =: at
  2747  (2087)  
  2748  (2088)          % FWD is what a LOCAL speaker sent onward; BAD is how many of those the
  2749  (2089)          % send refused. relay above is a different path entirely - see the note
  2750  (2090)          % on nForwarded.
  2751  (2091)          putWord(' fwd ', at) =: at
  2752  (2092)          putNumber(nForwarded, at) =: at
  2753  (2093)          putWord(' fwdbad ', at) =: at
  2754  (2094)          putNumber(nFwdBad, at) =: at
  2755  (2095)          putWord(' dupe ', at) =: at
  2756  (2096)          putNumber(nDupes, at) =: at
  2757  (2097)          putWord(' refuse ', at) =: at
  2758  (2098)          putNumber(nRefused, at) =: at
  2759  (2099)  
  2760  (2100)          FOR i IN 1:maxHistRooms DO
  2761  (2101)              IF topicRmLen(i) > 0 THEN
  2762  (2102)                  % Bounded like every other builder here: a name plus two
  2763  (2103)                  % numbers is at most 25 bytes, and outBuf's text field is 250.
  2764  (2104)                  IF at - textAt < 215 THEN
  2765  (2105)                      spaceByte(0) =: outBuf(at)
  2766  (2106)                      at + 1 =: at
  2767  (2107)                      FOR j IN 1:topicRmLen(i) DO
  2768  (2108)                          topicRoom(i, j) =: outBuf(at)
  2769  (2109)                          at + 1 =: at
  2770  (2110)                      ENDFOR
  2771  (2111)                      spaceByte(0) =: outBuf(at)
  2772  (2112)                      at + 1 =: at
  2773  (2113)                      putNumber(histSize(i), at) =: at
  2774  (2114)                      slashByte(0) =: outBuf(at)
  2775  (2115)                      at + 1 =: at
  2776  (2116)  
  2777  (2117)                      histNext(i) =: held
  2778  (2118)                      IF held > histSize(i) THEN
  2779  (2119)                          histSize(i) =: held     % wrapped: it holds a full ring
  2780  (2120)                      ENDIF
  2781  (2121)                      putNumber(held, at) =: at
  2782  (2122)                  ENDIF
  2783  (2123)              ENDIF
  2784  (2124)          ENDFOR
  2785  (2125)  
  2786  (2126)          at - textAt =: textLen
  2787  (2127)          textLen =: outBuf(3)                  % low byte; it cannot reach 256
  2788  (2128)          at RETURN
  2789  (2129)      ENDROUTINE
  2790  (2130)  
  2791  (2131)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  2792  (2132)      % Build a kAdmRepl carrying one fixed sentence.
  2793  (2133)      %
  2794  (2134)      % SUBSCRIPT FROM ZERO. A BYTES declared from a literal has its first
  2795  (2135)      % character at index 0, so "text(i)" for i in 1:len drops the first letter
  2796  (2136)      % and reads one byte past the end. That defect turned RONNY into ONNY on the
  2797  (2137)      % wire once already; see the note in refuse().
