  7436  (6776)  
  7437  (6777)          IF kind = kSay THEN
  7438  (6778)              % The speaker is named by the ROOM, never by what the sender put in
  7439  (6779)              % the message - otherwise anyone could speak as anyone.
  7440  (6780)              buildFromSlot(kSaid, slot, textAt, textLength) =: length
  7441  (6781)              broadcast(length, slot, 0)            % their room, speaker too
  7442  (6782)  
  7443  (6783)              % PHASE D: AND ON TO THE OTHER MACHINES.
  7444  (6784)              %
  7445  (6785)              % AFTER the local broadcast, deliberately. The people in the room
  7446  (6786)              % here are the ones waiting on this keystroke; a peer that is slow
  7447  (6787)              % or absent must not delay them. It also means a forward that fails
  7448  (6788)              % costs the local room nothing.
  7449  (6789)              %
  7450  (6790)              % forwardSaid reads inBuf, which still holds the arrival - the local
  7451  (6791)              % broadcast above only wrote outBuf.
  7452  (6792)              forwardSaid(slot, textAt, textLength)
  7453  (6793)  
  7454  (6794)              % ---- AND REMEMBER IT ----------------------------------------
  7455  (6795)              %
  7456  (6796)              % LAST, after the room and the peers have both been served, for
  7457  (6797)              % the same reason the forward goes after the broadcast: the people
  7458  (6798)              % waiting on this keystroke come first, and a disk write must never
  7459  (6799)              % sit between somebody pressing return and the room hearing it.
  7460  (6800)              %
  7461  (6801)              % claimTopic, despite the name - it is the room table, and history
  7462  (6802)              % shares its slots. It allocates on first use, so the file for a
  7463  (6803)              % room is created the first time anybody speaks in it rather than
  7464  (6804)              % when the room is invented, and a room nobody ever speaks in costs
  7465  (6805)              % nothing at all.
  7466  (6806)              %
  7467  (6807)              % A FULL TABLE RETURNS 0 AND histSave DOES NOTHING. Nine rooms
  7468  (6808)              % means the ninth keeps no history; it does not lose its messages.
  7469  (6809)              claimTopic(slot) =: histSlot
  7470  (6810)              histSave(histSlot, slot, textAt, textLength)
  7471  (6811)              RETURN
  7472  (6812)          ENDIF
  7473  (6813)  
  7474  (6814)          % A DIRECT MESSAGE. A SEAT IS REQUIRED - not because the target needs
  7475  (6815)          % to be in a room, they do not, but because the SENDER has to have a
  7476  (6816)          % name for the message to be from. This sits below the slot = 0 test
  7477  (6817)          % with kSay for that reason.
  7478  (6818)          IF kind = kDirect THEN
  7479  (6819)              handleDirect(slot, textAt, textLength)
  7480  (6820)              RETURN
  7481  (6821)          ENDIF
  7482  (6822)  
  7483  (6823)          IF kind = kRename THEN
  7484  (6824)              IF nameLength = 0 THEN
  7485  (6825)                  refuse(nameAt, nameLength, reasonNeedNick, lenNeedNick)
  7486  (6826)                  RETURN
  7487  (6827)              ENDIF
  7488  (6828)              IF sameName(slot, nameAt, nameLength) = 1 THEN
  7489  (6829)                  RETURN                            % already your name: not news
  7490  (6830)              ENDIF
  7491  (6831)              findByName(nameAt, nameLength) =: taken
  7492  (6832)              IF taken >< 0 THEN
  7493  (6833)                  refuseNick(nameAt, nameLength)
  7494  (6834)                  RETURN
  7495  (6835)              ENDIF
  7496  (6836)  
  7497  (6837)              % Renamed carries the NEW name and the OLD one as its text, so a
  7498  (6838)              % transcript on somebody's screen can be connected up. Built before
  7499  (6839)              % the slot changes, while the old name is still there.
  7500  (6840)              buildRenamed(slot, nameAt, nameLength) =: length
  7501  (6841)              copyNameIn(slot, nameAt, nameLength)
  7502  (6842)              broadcast(length, slot, 0)            % their room, asker too
  7503  (6843)  
  7504  (6844)              % AND ON TO THE OTHER MACHINES, or they go on advertising the
  7505  (6845)              % OLD name. AFTER the local broadcast for the same reason
  7506  (6846)              % forwardSaid is: the people in this room are the ones waiting,
  7507  (6847)              % and a peer that is slow or absent must not delay them.
  7508  (6848)              %
  7509  (6849)              % It sends the WHOLE list rather than a rename notice - see
  7510  (6850)              % tellMbrs - so a peer that missed an earlier change is repaired
  7511  (6851)              % by this one too.
  7512  (6852)              tellMbrs
  7513  (6853)              RETURN
  7514  (6854)          ENDIF
  7515  (6855)  
  7516  (6856)          % /who - answered to the ASKER ALONE, never broadcast. Everybody else
  7517  (6857)          % asking would then get a list they did not want, and the room would
  7518  (6858)          % spend a message on each of them.
  7519  (6859)          IF kind = kWho THEN
  7520  (6860)              % slot, not senderMagic: the answer is filtered to the ASKER'S
  7521  (6861)              % ROOM, so buildWho needs a seat to compare against.
  7522  (6862)              buildWho(slot) =: length
  7523  (6863)              sendTo(senderMagic, length)
  7524  (6864)              RETURN
  7525  (6865)          ENDIF
  7526  (6866)  
  7527  (6867)  
  7528  (6868)          % /list is NOT here any more - it moved ABOVE the membership guard,
  7529  (6869)          % where the registry always said it belonged. See the note there.
  7530  (6870)  
  7531  (6871)          % /topic - ask with an empty text, set with a non-empty one.
  7532  (6872)          %
  7533  (6873)          % A SET IS BROADCAST, AN ASK IS NOT. Everybody in the room needs to see
  7534  (6874)          % a topic change as it happens - it is a change to the room, not an
  7535  (6875)          % answer to one person - while an ask is nobody else's business.
  7536  (6876)          IF kind = kTopic THEN
  7537  (6877)              IF textLength = 0 THEN
  7538  (6878)                  buildTopic(slot) =: length
  7539  (6879)                  sendTo(senderMagic, length)
  7540  (6880)                  RETURN
  7541  (6881)              ENDIF
  7542  (6882)  
  7543  (6883)              % TRUNCATED, NOT REFUSED. The length arrives from the peer and
  7544  (6884)              % PLANC checks no array bound, so a long topic would write straight
  7545  (6885)              % through the rest of the table. The client already stops at 64, so
  7546  (6886)              % this only ever bites a peer that is not our client - which is
  7547  (6887)              % exactly the case a bound has to survive.
  7548  (6888)              IF textLength > maxTopicLen THEN
  7549  (6889)                  maxTopicLen =: textLength
  7550  (6890)              ENDIF
  7551  (6891)  
  7552  (6892)              claimTopic(slot) =: taken
  7553  (6893)              IF taken = 0 THEN
  7554  (6894)                  % The table is full. SAY SO rather than dropping it: a topic
  7555  (6895)                  % that silently does not stick is the kind of failure somebody
  7556  (6896)                  % retries for ten minutes. refuse() sends for itself.
  7557  (6897)                  refuse(nameAt, nameLength, reasonTopicFull, lenTopicFull)
  7558  (6898)                  RETURN
  7559  (6899)              ENDIF
  7560  (6900)  
  7561  (6901)              FOR i IN 1:textLength DO
  7562  (6902)                  inBuf(textAt + i - 1) =: topicText(taken, i)
  7563  (6903)              ENDFOR
  7564  (6904)              textLength =: topicTextLen(taken)
  7565  (6905)  
  7566  (6906)              buildTopic(slot) =: length
  7567  (6907)              broadcast(length, slot, 0)        % 0 = leave nobody out
  7568  (6908)              RETURN
  7569  (6909)          ENDIF
  7570  (6910)  
  7571  (6911)          IF kind = kLeave THEN
  7572  (6912)              buildFromSlot(kLeft, slot, 0, 0) =: length
  7573  (6913)              0 =: memberUsed(slot)
  7574  (6914)              broadcast(length, slot, slot)         % their room, not them
  7575  (6915)  
  7576  (6916)              % AND TELL THE OTHER MACHINES THEY HAVE GONE. AFTER the seat
  7577  (6917)              % is released above, so the list we build no longer holds
  7578  (6918)              % them - tellMbrs reports the table as it stands.
  7579  (6919)              %
  7580  (6920)              % A STALE ENTRY HERE IS THE WORSE DIRECTION: a peer that still
  7581  (6921)              % believes somebody is seated will accept a private message
  7582  (6922)              % for them and send it across the trunk. Since 6.3b the far end
  7583  (6923)              % no longer drops that in silence - takeRemDirect answers with
  7584  (6924)              % kTrkDBad and the sender is told - but a refusal after the fact
  7585  (6925)              % is a worse answer than not sending at all, so the peers are
  7586  (6926)              % still told at once.
  7587  (6927)              tellMbrs
  7588  (6928)  
  7589  (6929)              % Give the seat back to XROUT, or the room fills up permanently and
  7590  (6930)              % XROUT stops forwarding joins long after anybody left.
  7591  (6931)              xmpinfc(0, myPort, 1, 0) =: returnStatus
  7592  (6932)              RETURN
  7593  (6933)          ENDIF
  7594  (6934)      ENDROUTINE
  7595  (6935)  
  7596  (6936)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  7597  (6937)      % The program.
  7598  (6938)      % -----------------------------------------------------------------------
  7599  (6939)      PROGRAM : chatServer
  7600  (6940)          XMMSGIDENTIFIER : msgIdent
  7601  (6941)          INTEGER : i
  7602  (6942)          % readStatus - 2026-09-01. returnStatus is one shared variable
  7603  (6943)          % reused by every XMSG call in this loop, so by the time anything
  7604  (6944)          % downstream could look at it, XMPFREL has already overwritten
  7605  (6945)          % whatever XMPFREA itself answered. Nothing checked the READ
  7606  (6946)          % succeeding before handleMessage trusted what it read into.
  7607  (6947)          INTEGER : readStatus
  7608  (6948)  
  7609  (6949)          % INISTACK RUNS EVERY START, and it must - the stack does not survive
  7610  (6950)          % the terminate. Everything below it is guarded instead.
  7611  (6951)          INISTACK stack
  7612  (6952)  
  7613  (6953)          % ---- THE HISTORY TABLE, CLEARED BY HAND -----------------------------
  7614  (6954)          %
  7615  (6955)          % PLANC DOES NOT PROMISE A MODULE VARIABLE STARTS AT ZERO, and this
  7616  (6956)          % file already carries one scar from assuming it did - see the note by
  7617  (6957)          % typedLen in the client. Three of these would be quietly fatal rather
  7618  (6958)          % than merely wrong:
  7619  (6959)          %
  7620  (6960)          %   histDev   a stale non-zero would be taken for an OPEN FILE and
  7621  (6961)          %             handed to MN120 as a device number;
  7622  (6962)          %   histSize  a stale ZERO is a DIVIDE BY ZERO the first time anybody
  7623  (6963)          %             speaks, because the ring is id MOD size;
  7624  (6964)          %   histNext  a stale value writes the first message into the middle of
  7625  (6965)          %             the ring and replays blocks that were never written.
  7626  (6966)          %
  7627  (6967)          %   topicRmLen  ADDED 2026-09-02, and it is the "empty list on join"
  7628  (6968)          %             bug. This is the marker claimTopic reads as "slot free"
  7629  (6969)          %             (zero) and findTopic reads as "which room". Left as
  7630  (6970)          %             garbage on a fresh RT segment, claimTopic finds no free
  7631  (6971)          %             slot and returns 0, so histSave does NOTHING and nothing
  7632  (6972)          %             is ever stored to replay - or findTopic mismatches and a
  7633  (6973)          %             real room never finds its history. The neighbours above
  7634  (6974)          %             were cleared; this one was missed, and it depends on what
  7635  (6975)          %             the freshly allocated segment happened to hold, which is
  7636  (6976)          %             why it reads as intermittent. topicTextLen goes with it,
  7637  (6977)          %             for the same reason a stale topic would otherwise show.
  7638  (6978)          %
  7639  (6979)          % Cleared here, before anything can be received.
  7640  (6980)          FOR i IN 1:maxHistRooms DO
  7641  (6981)              0 =: histDev(i)
  7642  (6982)              0 =: histNext(i)
  7643  (6983)              histDefault =: histSize(i)
  7644  (6984)          ENDFOR
  7645  (6985)          FOR i IN 1:maxTopics DO
  7646  (6986)              0 =: topicRmLen(i)
  7647  (6987)              0 =: topicTextLen(i)
  7648  (6988)          ENDFOR
  7649  (6989)  
  7650  (6990)          % ---- NEVER XFWTF. XFWAK ONLY AS A DOORBELL. -----------------------
  7651  (6991)          %
  7652  (6992)          % The history below is kept because both flags have been in this line
  7653  (6993)          % before and both took the server down. Read it before changing them.
  7654  (6994)          %
  7655  (6995)          % XFWTF was never right here: a task already blocked in XFRCV is ACTIVE,
  7656  (6996)          % and RT-starting an active program does not queue a second run, so a
  7657  (6997)          % wake-up arriving during a block is simply LOST.
  7658  (6998)          %
  7659  (6999)          % CORRECTED 2026-08-28: THE PARAGRAPH BELOW BLAMES THE WRONG FLAG.
  7660  (7000)          % Carved from git history - XFWAK appears ZERO times in the version
  7661  (7001)          % before 2026-08-21 and first entered this file on 2026-08-25, FOUR
  7662  (7002)          % DAYS AFTER the outage. That build had ONE xmpfrcv, on myPort, with
  7663  (7003)          % 2**XFWTF - the BLOCKING flag - and no admin receive at all. So the
  7664  (7004)          % server was parked by the BLOCKING receive, which is what the first
  7665  (7005)          % paragraph above already says. A non-blocking XFWAK parks nothing and
  7666  (7006)          % planc-lint no longer refuses it.
  7667  (7007)          %
  7668  (7008)          % STILL UNEXPLAINED, and worth re-checking before trusting a doorbell:
  7669  (7009)          % the LIST-PORTS capture quoted below shows WAK 1 on the admin port,
  7670  (7010)          % and the committed source of the day has neither an admin receive nor
  7671  (7011)          % XFWAK. Either the running build was uncommitted, or something else
  7672  (7012)          % sets that bit.
  7673  (7013)          %
  7674  (7014)          % BUT XFWAK IS ALSO WRONG NOW, AND IT HUNG THE SERVER. Measured on
  7675  (7015)          % D100, 2026-08-21, with one client trying to join:
  7676  (7016)          %
  7677  (7017)          %   X-C LIST-PORTS
  7678  (7018)          %    No  Address  Owner-task   Qhead  Qlen  Chain WAK
  7679  (7019)          %     6  160057 152616 BAK06  161762     1      0   0   <- the ROOM port
  7680  (7020)          %    14  160147 152616 BAK06       0     0      6   1   <- the ADMIN port
  7681  (7021)          %
  7682  (7022)          % A message SITTING on the room port, and the task parked on the admin
  7683  (7023)          % port's wake-up instead. The server had logged its two START lines and
  7684  (7024)          % then written nothing more; the client waited for ever at "not in a
  7685  (7025)          % room yet". XFRCV CANNOT WAIT ON TWO PORTS - arming a wake on each does
  7686  (7026)          % not make one call watch both, and the wake-up does NOT deliver the
  7687  (7027)          % message even when it fires.
  7688  (7028)          %
  7689  (7029)          % XFWAK WAS LEFT OVER FROM A DESIGN THAT NO LONGER EXISTS. It belonged
  7690  (7030)          % to the original loop, which RETURNED when both ports were empty and
  7691  (7031)          % relied on the next message RT-starting the program again. That was
  7692  (7032)          % replaced by the MON 267B sleep below - the program now STAYS ALIVE and
  7693  (7033)          % goes round - and once it stays alive there is nothing for a wake-up to
  7694  (7034)          % do. Keeping it bought nothing and cost the whole server.
  7695  (7035)          %
  7696  (7036)          % ---- AND NOW XFWAK IS BACK, FOR A DIFFERENT REASON ----------------
  7697  (7037)          %
  7698  (7038)          % Everything above stays true: XFWAK cannot make one XFRCV watch two
  7699  (7039)          % ports, and it does not deliver the message. It is a DOORBELL, and
  7700  (7040)          % nothing more. What changed is that there is now something for a
  7701  (7041)          % doorbell to ring: the program STAYS ALIVE in a one-second TimeOut
  7702  (7042)          % instead of terminating, and TimeOut's own restart cause (-1) is the
  7703  (7043)          % bell being heard. See the declarations of wakeSpin for the manual
  7704  (7044)          % lines this rests on, and for the counter that disarms it if the
  7705  (7045)          % restart flag turns out to be sticky.
  7706  (7046)          %
  7707  (7047)          % It is still NOT blocking - XFWTF is not set, so all three ports are
  7708  (7048)          % polled every pass exactly as before. Take this word out and the
  7709  (7049)          % server keeps working; it just waits up to a second again.
  7710  (7050)          IF doorbellOn THEN
  7711  (7051)              2**XFWAK =: waitFlags
  7712  (7052)          ELSE
  7713  (7053)              0 =: waitFlags
  7714  (7054)          ENDIF
  7715  (7055)          2**XFSEC =: sendFlags
  7716  (7056)  
  7717  (7057)          % ---- OPEN ONCE, NOT ONCE PER START ---------------------------------
  7718  (7058)          IF NOT portsOpen THEN
  7719  (7059)              FOR i IN 1:maxSeats DO
  7720  (7060)                  0 =: memberUsed(i)
  7721  (7061)                  0 =: memberDead(i)
  7722  (7062)              ENDFOR
  7723  (7063)  
  7724  (7064)              % A CONNECTION port: XROUT holds the free-connection counter and
  7725  (7065)              % turns the joiner past maxSeats away for us, before this program
  7726  (7066)              % is woken. That is the whole reason no client knows the number.
  7727  (7067)              % ONE PORT, CALLED *CHAT. Every service on this machine is starred
  7728  (7068)              % and short - *FA-SERVER, *XFTRA, *COSPO, *TADADM - and the chat
  7729  (7069)              % server is one of them.
  7730  (7070)              %
  7731  (7071)              % IT USED TO BE CHAT-LOBBY, and that was two mistakes in one name.
  7732  (7072)              % CHAT-LOBBY is a ROOM name doing duty as a SERVICE name, and the
  7733  (7073)              % client built a DIFFERENT port name per room - "CHAT-" plus the
  7734  (7074)              % room - so every room needed its own registered port. That made
  7735  (7075)              % the set of rooms XROUT's data rather than the server's, with no
  7736  (7076)              % call to read it back; it made a room expensive to create; it
  7737  (7077)              % fragmented the seat count per port; and it turned a federated
  7738  (7078)              % room into the SAME NAME registered on two machines, which is a
  7739  (7079)              % collision rather than shared state.
  7740  (7080)              %
  7741  (7081)              % Now: one port, and the ROOM TRAVELS IN THE MESSAGE. LOBBY goes
  7742  (7082)              % back to being an ordinary room. See the_room_model in
  7743  (7083)              % DOC/protocols/chat-wire.json.
  7744  (7084)              xmpopcn(0, '*CHAT', uniqueName, maxSeats, myPort) &
  7745  (7085)                  =: returnStatus
  7746  (7086)              IF returnStatus >< XMOK THEN
  7747  (7087)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL29', 'CHATSV: cannot open the room$')
  7748  (7088)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL11', 'SV: opn st=')
  7749  (7089)                  OUTPUT(1, 'I6', returnStatus)
  7750  (7090)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL1', '$')
  7751  (7091)                  RETURN
  7752  (7092)              ENDIF
  7753  (7093)  
  7754  (7094)              % The command port. XMPOPNM - a NORMAL named port, so an operator
  7755  (7095)              % costs the room nothing. See the declaration for why that matters.
  7756  (7096)              xmpopnm(0, admName(0:lenAdmName - 1), adminPort) =: returnStatus
  7757  (7097)              IF returnStatus >< XMOK THEN
  7758  (7098)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL28', 'CHATSV: no command port, st=')
  7759  (7099)                  OUTPUT(1, 'I6', returnStatus)
  7760  (7100)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL1', '$')
  7761  (7101)                  % NOT fatal. A server that will not start because nobody can
  7762  (7102)                  % ask it for STATUS is worse than one that runs unattended.
  7763  (7103)              ENDIF
  7764  (7104)  
  7765  (7105)              % The trunk port. XMPOPNM for the same reason the command port is -
  7766  (7106)              % a peer that is switched off must cost the room nothing however
  7767  (7107)              % long it stays away.
  7768  (7108)              xmpopnm(0, trkName(0:lenTrkName - 1), trunkPort) =: returnStatus
  7769  (7109)              IF returnStatus >< XMOK THEN
  7770  (7110)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL26', 'CHATSV: no trunk port, st=')
  7771  (7111)                  OUTPUT(1, 'I6', returnStatus)
  7772  (7112)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL1', '$')
  7773  (7113)                  % NOT fatal, same judgement as the command port: a server that
  7774  (7114)                  % refuses to serve its own room because it cannot federate is
  7775  (7115)                  % worse than one that serves the room and cannot federate.
  7776  (7116)              ENDIF
  7777  (7117)  
  7778  (7118)              % Our own magic number, so an arrival can be tested against it.
  7779  (7119)              xmpfp2m(0, myPort, myMagic) =: returnStatus
  7780  (7120)              IF returnStatus >< XMOK THEN
  7781  (7121)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL18', 'SV: p2m failed st=')
  7782  (7122)                  OUTPUT(1, 'I6', returnStatus)
  7783  (7123)                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL1', '$')
  7784  (7124)              ENDIF
  7785  (7125)  
  7786  (7126)              % ...and our own SYSTEM number out of it, so the trunk can tell our
  7787  (7127)              % own bounced letters from a peer saying hello. See mySystem.
  7788  (7128)              xmpfm2p(0, myMagic, senderPortNo, mySystem, &
  7789  (7129)                  senderRtInfo, senderExtra) =: returnStatus
  7790  (7130)  
  7791  (7131)              % ONE BANNER, AND IT STAYS. An operator has to be able to tell a
  7792  (7132)              % server that is waiting from one that never got its room, and the
  7793  (7133)              % port number is what LIST-PORTS is matched against when it does
  7794  (7134)              % not. It prints on the FIRST start only - this program is now
  7795  (7135)              % started again by every arrival, and a banner per message would
  7796  (7136)              % bury the machine.
  7797  (7137)              0 =: nEmptyLet
  7798  (7138)              0 =: nRelayed
  7799  (7139)              0 =: nForwarded
  7800  (7140)              0 =: nFwdBad
  7801  (7141)              0 =: nDupes
  7802  (7142)              0 =: myLineId
  7803  (7143)              1 =: seenAt
  7804  (7144)              0 =: seenUsed
  7805  (7145)              0 =: nBounced
  7806  (7146)              0 =: nRefused
  7807  (7147)  
  7808  (7148)              OUTPUT(1, 'AL24', 'CHATSV: room open, port ')
  7809  (7149)              OUTPUT(1, 'I6', myPort)
  7810  (7150)              OUTPUT(1, 'AL9', ' command ')
  7811  (7151)              OUTPUT(1, 'I6', adminPort)
  7812  (7152)              OUTPUT(1, 'AL7', ' trunk ')
  7813  (7153)              OUTPUT(1, 'I6', trunkPort)
  7814  (7154)              OUTPUT(1, 'AL1', '$')
  7815  (7155)              logLine('SV START room prt=', myPort)
  7816  (7156)              logLine('SV START admin prt', adminPort)
  7817  (7157)              logLine('SV START trunk prt', trunkPort)
  7818  (7158)  
  7819  (7159)              TRUE =: portsOpen
  7820  (7160)          ENDIF
  7821  (7161)  
  7822  (7162)          DO
  7823  (7163)              % WAIT. XFWTF is what makes this block rather than spin; an RT
  7824  (7164)              % program that polls burns the machine for nothing. Without the
  7825  (7165)              % flag the status would come back MXNEXTM when nothing is waiting.
  7826  (7166)              % ---- DRAIN BOTH PORTS, THEN TERMINATE --------------------------
  7827  (7167)              %
  7828  (7168)              % The lobby first, because that is the busy one. If it is empty,
  7829  (7169)              % the command port. If BOTH are empty, both wake-ups are now armed
  7830  (7170)              % and there is nothing to do: RETURN, and the next transmission to
  7831  (7171)              % either port RT-starts this program again.
  7832  (7172)              %
  7833  (7173)              % HOW "EMPTY" IS TOLD FROM "GOT ONE", which is the whole hazard of
  7834  (7174)              % dropping XFWTF and is MEASURED rather than reasoned:
  7835  (7175)              %
  7836  (7176)              %   XMOK (0)        a SUCCESSFUL receive, with the type in the
  7837  (7177)              %                   msgType out-parameter. Vendor Appendix G tests
  7838  (7178)              %                   "IF RETURNSTATUS >< XMOK THEN bad status", and
  7839  (7179)              %                   D100 printed "SV: rcv st= 0 t= 2 len= 25".
  7840  (7180)              %   XMTNO..XMTPS    also a successful receive.
  7841  (7181)              %   XMXENTM (16896) an EMPTY port. Neither of the above.
  7842  (7182)              %
  7843  (7183)              % The manual's "a zero status is returned" for an empty port
  7844  (7184)              % describes the RAW MON call. XMPFRCV remaps it - the library's
  7845  (7185)              % empty answer is 16896, not 0 - so zero is unambiguous here. Had
  7846  (7186)              % that not been so, this loop could not have told an empty port
  7847  (7187)              % from a message and would have terminated on live traffic.
  7848  (7188)              FALSE =: fromAdmin
  7849  (7189)              FALSE =: fromTrunk
  7850  (7190)              xmpfrcv(waitFlags, myPort, msgType, remotePortHash, msgIdent, nBytes) &
  7851  (7191)                  =: returnStatus
  7852  (7192)  
  7853  (7193)              IF returnStatus = XMXENTM THEN
  7854  (7194)                  TRUE =: fromAdmin
  7855  (7195)                  xmpfrcv(waitFlags, adminPort, msgType, remotePortHash, &
  7856  (7196)                      msgIdent, nBytes) =: returnStatus
  7857  (7197)  
  7858  (7198)                  % ---- AND THEN THE TRUNK PORT ------------------------------
  7859  (7199)                  % Third in the round, and last on purpose: people in the room
  7860  (7200)                  % come before an operator, and an operator before a machine
  7861  (7201)                  % saying hello. Trunk traffic is the least urgent thing here.
  7862  (7202)                  IF returnStatus = XMXENTM THEN
  7863  (7203)                      FALSE =: fromAdmin
  7864  (7204)                      TRUE =: fromTrunk
  7865  (7205)                      xmpfrcv(waitFlags, trunkPort, msgType, remotePortHash, &
  7866  (7206)                          msgIdent, nBytes) =: returnStatus
  7867  (7207)                  ENDIF
  7868  (7208)  
  7869  (7209)                  IF returnStatus = XMXENTM THEN
  7870  (7210)                      % ---- BOTH EMPTY: SLEEP, DO NOT END ------------------
  7871  (7211)                      %
  7872  (7212)                      % THIS USED TO "RETURN", AND THAT WAS REFUTED ON THE
  7873  (7213)                      % MACHINE. MEASURED on D100 2026-08-20: @RT CHATSER ran,
  7874  (7214)                      % drained both empty ports, returned - and
  7875  (7215)                      % LIST-RT-DESCRIPTION then said PASSIVE with 0 CPU units
  7876  (7216)                      % while LIST-NAMES showed NEITHER CHAT-LOBBY NOR
  7877  (7217)                      % CHAT-ADMIN. SINTRAN CLOSES A TASK'S PORTS WHEN THE TASK
  7878  (7218)                      % TERMINATES, so the XFWAK wake-up had nothing left to
  7879  (7219)                      % arrive on and the server could never be restarted.
  7880  (7220)                      %
  7881  (7221)                      % The manual's wake-up mechanism is real, but it assumes a
  7882  (7222)                      % task that STAYS ALIVE to be woken. Both halves of that
  7883  (7223)                      % were already written down here and were not put together.
  7884  (7224)                      %
  7885  (7225)                      % So: sleep and go round. MON 267B suspends the program for
  7886  (7226)                      % a given time and continues after the call - no spin, no
  7887  (7227)                      % termination, ports stay open. Unit type 1 is basic time
  7888  (7228)                      % units of 1/50 second, so 50 is one second: long enough to
  7889  (7229)                      % cost nothing, short enough that an operator typing STATUS
  7890  (7230)                      % does not notice the wait.
  7891  (7231)                      %
  7892  (7232)                      % A BARE LOOP WITH NO SLEEP IS NOT AN OPTION. When XMSG
  7893  (7233)                      % died under this server the receive stopped blocking and
  7894  (7234)                      % this loop burned the machine until somebody pressed ESC.
  7895  (7235)                      % A FULL SECOND WHEN THE DOORBELL IS ARMED, because a
  7896  (7236)                      % wake-up is what ends it early; 100 ms when it is not,
  7897  (7237)                      % which is the poll this loop used before and the state it
  7898  (7238)                      % falls back to. sleepWhy comes back 0 for the time simply
  7899  (7239)                      % elapsing, 1 for a break, and -1 for the RT-start that a
  7900  (7240)                      % waiting message performs.
  7901  (7241)                      IF doorbellOn THEN
  7902  (7242)                          50 =: sleepUnits
  7903  (7243)                      ELSE
  7904  (7244)                          5 =: sleepUnits
  7905  (7245)                      ENDIF
  7906  (7246)                      1 =: sleepKind
  7907  (7247)                      MONITOR_CALL('TimeOut', sleepUnits, sleepKind, sleepWhy)
  7908  (7248)  
  7909  (7249)                      % ---- DID THE BELL RING, OR DID THE TIME RUN OUT? ------
  7910  (7250)                      %
  7911  (7251)                      % This is the measurement the whole change exists for, and
  7912  (7252)                      % it costs one comparison. A -1 here that found all three
  7913  (7253)                      % ports empty is a wake with nothing behind it; twenty in a
  7914  (7254)                      % row means the restart flag is not clearing, so disarm and
  7915  (7255)                      % go back to polling rather than spin.
  7916  (7256)                      IF doorbellOn AND sleepWhy < 0 THEN
  7917  (7257)                          wakeSpin + 1 =: wakeSpin
  7918  (7258)                          IF wakeSpin >= wakeSpinMax THEN
  7919  (7259)                              FALSE =: doorbellOn
  7920  (7260)                              0 =: waitFlags
  7921  (7261)                              logLine('SV doorbell off   ', wakeSpin)
  7922  (7262)                          ENDIF
  7923  (7263)                      ENDIF
  7924  (7264)  
  7925  (7265)                      % ---- HAS A SECOND GONE BY? AGE THE TRUNKS --------------
  7926  (7266)                      %
  7927  (7267)                      % Asked of the CLOCK, not of how many times we have been
  7928  (7268)                      % round here. That distinction used to not matter, because
  7929  (7269)                      % every sleep ran its full 100 ms and ten of them were a
  7930  (7270)                      % second by construction. A doorbell that cuts a sleep
  7931  (7271)                      % short destroys that, and a busy room must not make peers
  7932  (7272)                      % time out faster - so the question is now MON11's.
  7933  (7273)                      %
  7934  (7274)                      % It still lives inside the all-ports-empty branch: a
  7935  (7275)                      % server with traffic never sleeps, and its trunks are
  7936  (7276)                      % plainly alive, so there is nothing to age.
  7937  (7277)                      MON11 =: nowTicks
  7938  (7278)                      IF nowTicks - lastTick >= tickUnits THEN
  7939  (7279)                          nowTicks =: lastTick
  7940  (7280)                          peerTick
  7941  (7281)                      ENDIF
  7942  (7282)                  ENDIF
  7943  (7283)              ENDIF
  7944  (7284)  
  7945  (7285)              % ---- A REAL MESSAGE CLEARS THE DOORBELL'S SUSPICION ------------
  7946  (7286)              %
  7947  (7287)              % wakeSpin counts wake-ups that turned out to have nothing behind
  7948  (7288)              % them, and it must count them CONSECUTIVELY - otherwise a busy
  7949  (7289)              % server, where nearly every wake IS a message, would still reach
  7950  (7290)              % twenty eventually and disarm a doorbell that was working
  7951  (7291)              % perfectly. Anything other than an empty port is proof the
  7952  (7292)              % mechanism is delivering, so the count goes back to zero.
  7953  (7293)              IF returnStatus >< XMXENTM THEN
  7954  (7294)                  0 =: wakeSpin
  7955  (7295)              ENDIF
  7956  (7296)  
  7957  (7297)              % A RECEIVE IS A MESSAGE ONLY WHEN THE STATUS IS A MESSAGE TYPE.
  7958  (7298)              % Not "= XMOK" and not "> 0". The library remaps statuses: 0 is OK
  7959  (7299)              % for other calls but is NOT a message type, so comparing to it
  7960  (7300)              % here discards every message; and 16896 (XMXENTM, nothing there)
  7961  (7301)              % and every error are positive, so "> 0" accepts an empty port.
  7962  (7302)              % The constants file settles it - "Message types: Returned as
  7963  (7303)              % successfull status from XFRCV", XMTNO=1 through XMTPS=6.
  7964  (7304)              % PRINT THE STATUS OF EVERY RECEIVE, OUTSIDE THE TEST.
  7965  (7305)              %
  7966  (7306)              % This line is here because the test below threw the message away
  7967  (7307)              % and every diagnostic was INSIDE it, so the server looked as if it
  7968  (7308)              % had never woken at all. MEASURED 2026-08-18: CHAT-LOBBY went from
  7969  (7309)              % 8 free seats to 6 across two joins, which proves XROUT accepted
  7970  (7310)              % both letters and forwarded them here - "XROUT will decrement the
  7971  (7311)              % counter and forward the whole message to portName" - while this
  7972  (7312)              % program printed nothing and went straight back to waiting.
  7973  (7313)              %
  7974  (7314)              % A gate must never be the only thing that can tell you it closed.
  7975  (7315)              % This printed on EVERY receive during bring-up. It now prints only
  7976  (7316)              % when the receive is NOT one this server accepts - which is the case
  7977  (7317)              % the original comment was really about. A message that arrives and is
  7978  (7318)              % handled says so by what it does; a message thrown away by the test
  7979  (7319)              % below still has nothing else to speak for it.
  7980  (7320)              % ---- KNOW WHEN TO STOP -------------------------------------
  7981  (7321)              % MEASURED 2026-08-18, and it is why this block exists at all.
  7982  (7322)              % XMSG went down under a running server. XMPFRCV then stopped
  7983  (7323)              % blocking - there is nothing to wait ON - and returned 16933,
  7984  (7324)              % XMXENRU, "XMSG not running", instantly, for ever. This loop
  7985  (7325)              % printed that line as fast as the terminal could take it and
  7986  (7326)              % burned the machine until somebody pressed ESC.
  7987  (7327)              %
  7988  (7328)              % A WAIT THAT CANNOT WAIT IS A SPIN. The XFWTF flag is what makes
  7989  (7329)              % this loop cheap, and it quietly stops meaning anything the moment
  7990  (7330)              % the thing it waits on is gone. Any program built around a
  7991  (7331)              % blocking call needs an answer for that, and "print and go round
  7992  (7332)              % again" is not one.
  7993  (7333)              %
  7994  (7334)              % Two ways out, and they are different on purpose:
  7995  (7335)              %
  7996  (7336)              %   XMXENRU        STOP. Nothing this program can do brings XMSG
  7997  (7337)              %                  back - an operator has to restart it - so
  7998  (7338)              %                  retrying is not persistence, it is a busy loop
  7999  (7339)              %                  with no end. Say so plainly and leave.
  8000  (7340)              %
  8001  (7341)              %   anything else  RETRY, but not for ever. An unknown status may
  8002  (7342)              %                  well clear; the same one arriving maxBadRuns
  8003  (7343)              %                  times running has not cleared and will not.
  8004  (7344)              %
  8005  (7345)              % A GOOD RECEIVE CLEARS THE COUNT, so the limit is on a RUN of
  8006  (7346)              % failures rather than on failures for the life of the program. A
  8007  (7347)              % server that has been up for a week and sees twenty scattered odd
  8008  (7348)              % statuses is healthy; one that sees twenty in a row is not.
  8009  (7349)              IF returnStatus = XMOK OR &
  8010  (7350)                 (returnStatus >= XMTNO AND returnStatus <= XMTPS) THEN
  8011  (7351)                  0 =: badRuns
  8012  (7352)              ELSE
  8013  (7353)                  IF returnStatus >< XMXENTM THEN
  8014  (7354)                      badRuns + 1 =: badRuns
  8015  (7355)  
  8016  (7356)                      % Print the first few and then stop shouting. The whole
  8017  (7357)                      % fault above was a message repeated without limit, and a
  8018  (7358)                      % diagnostic that fills the screen hides the one line that
  8019  (7359)                      % matters.
  8020  (7360)                      IF badRuns <= maxNoisy THEN
  8021  (7361)                          OUTPUT(1, 'AL23', 'SV: receive refused st=')
  8022  (7362)                          OUTPUT(1, 'I6', returnStatus)
  8023  (7363)                          OUTPUT(1, 'AL1', '$')
  8024  (7364)                          logLine('SV rcv refused st=', returnStatus)
  8025  (7365)                      ENDIF
  8026  (7366)  
  8027  (7367)                      IF returnStatus = XMXENRU THEN
  8028  (7368)                          OUTPUT(1, 'AL31', 'CHATSV: XMSG is not running - $')
  8029  (7369)                          OUTPUT(1, 'AL31', 'CHATSV: stopping, restart XMSG$')
  8030  (7370)                          RETURN
  8031  (7371)                      ENDIF
  8032  (7372)  
  8033  (7373)                      IF badRuns > maxBadRuns THEN
  8034  (7374)                          OUTPUT(1, 'AL30', 'CHATSV: too many bad receives$')
  8035  (7375)                          OUTPUT(1, 'I6', returnStatus)
  8036  (7376)                          OUTPUT(1, 'AL1', '$')
  8037  (7377)                          RETURN
  8038  (7378)                      ENDIF
  8039  (7379)                  ENDIF
  8040  (7380)              ENDIF
  8041  (7381)  
  8042  (7382)              % ACCEPT XMOK TOO - Appendix G's SERVER does exactly this.
  8043  (7383)              %
  8044  (7384)              % The old test was the range XMTNO..XMTPS alone, defended by a
  8045  (7385)              % comment claiming 0 "is NOT a message type, so comparing to it here
  8046  (7386)              % discards every message". The vendor sample says otherwise: after
  8047  (7387)              % XMPFRCV it tests "IF RETURNSTATUS >< XMOK THEN ... bad status" and
  8048  (7388)              % only THEN looks at the MSGTYPE out-parameter to check for XMROU.
  8049  (7389)              % So XMOK is a successful receive, and the message type belongs in
  8050  (7390)              % msgType, not in the status.
  8051  (7391)              %
  8052  (7392)              % Both forms are accepted here rather than one being swapped for the
  8053  (7393)              % other, because the print above will say which one actually turns up
  8054  (7394)              % and guessing is what put the range test here in the first place.
  8055  (7395)              IF returnStatus = XMOK OR &
  8056  (7396)                 (returnStatus >= XMTNO AND returnStatus <= XMTPS) THEN
  8057  (7397)                  % The FULL magic number of the sender - XMPFRCV hands back only
  8058  (7398)                  % a hashed port, and we must be able to answer.
  8059  (7399)                  % A "got t= len=" line printed here to separate "never woke"
  8060  (7400)                  % from "woke, replied, reply lost". It said to remove it once the
  8061  (7401)                  % join works, and the join works.
  8062  (7402)                  % THE RECEIVE'S OWN nBytes, KEPT BEFORE ANYTHING OVERWRITES IT.
  8063  (7403)                  % For an ordinary message it is the length; for a RETURNED one
  8064  (7404)                  % the guide says it is something else entirely - "If msgType is
  8065  (7405)                  % XMTRE, msgLengthOrStat contains the error status" - and
  8066  (7406)                  % xmpfmst below writes over it either way.
  8067  (7407)                  nBytes =: receiveStat
  8068  (7408)  
  8069  (7409)                  xmpfmst(0, msgIdent, msgType, senderMagic, nBytes) =: returnStatus
  8070  (7410)  
  8071  (7411)                  % WHICH MACHINE SENT THIS. A magic number is a port and a system
  8072  (7412)                  % packed together, and XMPFM2P is the library's own way of taking
  8073  (7413)                  % it apart - "Take a magic number apart into port number and
  8074  (7414)                  % system number" (xmp-api.json, transcribed from XMP-B02:DEFS).
  8075  (7415)                  %
  8076  (7416)                  % The TRUNK needs it and nothing else does: a peer is identified
  8077  (7417)                  % by system number, and an unsolicited HELLO from a machine we
  8078  (7418)                  % have never been told about has to be answerable without any
  8079  (7419)                  % configuration having happened first. Reading it from the magic
  8080  (7420)                  % means there is nothing to remember and nothing to go stale.
  8081  (7421)                  0 =: senderSystem
  8082  (7422)                  xmpfm2p(0, senderMagic, senderPortNo, senderSystem, &
  8083  (7423)                      senderRtInfo, senderExtra) =: returnStatus
  8084  (7424)  
  8085  (7425)                  % CLAMP BEFORE READING. nBytes is the sender's message size,
  8086  (7426)                  % handed back by XMSG - not a size this program chose. Passing
  8087  (7427)                  % it straight in lets any peer that sends more than inBufSize
  8088  (7428)                  % bytes write past the end of inBuf. That is a WRITE overflow
  8089  (7429)                  % and it happens before a single field is parsed, so none of
  8090  (7430)                  % the careful field checks in handleMessage can save it.
  8091  (7431)                  nBytes =: readAmount
  8092  (7432)                  IF readAmount > inBufSize THEN
  8093  (7433)                      inBufSize =: readAmount
  8094  (7434)                  ENDIF
  8095  (7435)  
  8096  (7436)                  xmpfrea(0, 0, &
  8097  (7437)                      ADDR(inBuf(0)) FORCE XMUSERADDRESS, &
  8098  (7438)                      0, readAmount, readLength) =: returnStatus
  8099  (7439)                  returnStatus =: readStatus
  8100  (7440)  
  8101  (7441)                  xmpfrel(0, msgIdent) =: returnStatus
  8102  (7442)  
  8103  (7443)                  % A 48-BYTE RAW DUMP OF EVERY ARRIVAL STOOD HERE, and it is the
  8104  (7444)                  % single diagnostic that solved this program. It is worth saying
  8105  (7445)                  % what it did before it goes, because the lesson outlives it.
  8106  (7446)                  %
  8107  (7447)                  % It began at 8 bytes and printed nothing useful for the arrival
  8108  (7448)                  % that mattered. Widened to 48 it showed the join in full:
  8109  (7449)                  %
  8110  (7450)                  %   123  65  0 12 | 255 10 'CHAT-LOBBY' | 1 5 'RONNY' 0 0
  8111  (7451)                  %
  8112  (7452)                  % Byte 0 was 123 - the SERIAL NUMBER this program's own client
  8113  (7453)                  % hands to XMPBLET. So what arrives starts with the XSLET LETTER
  8114  (7454)                  % HEADER, and handleMessage had been reading that header as if it
  8115  (7455)                  % were a chat message all along. The header skip below is the fix,
  8116  (7456)                  % and the layout under it is read off those bytes.
  8117  (7457)                  %
  8118  (7458)                  % The general form: a dump of what actually arrived beat every
  8119  (7459)                  % re-reading of the code, and it beat the manual too - the manual
  8120  (7460)                  % was right and was being applied to the wrong offset.
  8121  (7461)  
  8122  (7462)                  % A "fromSelf YES/no" line printed here. It earned its place once:
  8123  (7463)                  % the answer came back YES, the server's own reply arriving
  8124  (7464)                  % straight back at this port, which no amount of reading the guide
  8125  (7465)                  % would have suggested. That is recorded rather than printed now.
  8126  (7466)                  % THE SENDER'S MAGIC NUMBER AND OUR OWN were printed side by side
  8127  (7467)                  % here, to answer one suspicion: the welcome was being sent with
  8128  (7468)                  % status 0 and never arriving, so was senderMagic - taken from
  8129  (7469)                  % XMPFMST on a letter that came via XROUT - an address we could
  8130  (7470)                  % actually send BACK to?
  8131  (7471)                  %
  8132  (7472)                  % IT WAS. The two numbers were consistent and the reply address
  8133  (7473)                  % was never the bug. What that ruled out is what sent the search
  8134  (7474)                  % to the CLIENT's receive test, where XMPFRCV was returning XMOK,
  8135  (7475)                  % a "1..6 only" test was rejecting it, and the welcome was being
  8136  (7476)                  % dequeued and dropped in silence.
  8137  (7477)                  %
  8138  (7478)                  % A diagnostic that clears a suspicion is worth as much as one
  8139  (7479)                  % that confirms it, and this one cost a push-and-compile round
  8140  (7480)                  % trip to add. That is why it is written down instead of deleted.
  8141  (7481)  
  8142  (7482)                  % ---- A LETTER ARRIVES WEARING ITS XROUT HEADER --------------
  8143  (7483)                  % XROUT forwards THE WHOLE MESSAGE, header included. So a letter
  8144  (7484)                  % that came via XROUT has the XSLET header in front of the chat
  8145  (7485)                  % message, and the chat message has to be found behind it.
  8146  (7486)                  %
  8147  (7487)                  % ND-60.164.3 EN COSMOS Programmer Guide, appendix B, "XROUT
  8148  (7488)                  % Message Format", states the layout - this is read from the
  8149  (7489)                  % manual, not inferred from one capture:
  8150  (7490)                  %
  8151  (7491)                  %   byte 0     serial number, echoed back unchanged by XROUT
  8152  (7492)                  %   byte 1     the service number (XSLET = 65); XROUT overwrites
  8153  (7493)                  %              it with the return status on a reply
  8154  (7494)                  %   bytes 2-3  LENGTH OF THE REMAINDER OF THE MESSAGE in bytes,
  8155  (7495)                  %              "followed by a sequence of parameter blocks"
  8156  (7496)                  %
  8157  (7497)                  % So the parameter blocks occupy exactly the bytes that length
  8158  (7498)                  % counts, and OUR data starts right after them:
  8159  (7499)                  %
  8160  (7500)                  %   payloadAt = 4 + the length word
  8161  (7501)                  %
  8162  (7502)                  % The manual is explicit that the user data is untouched: "The
  8163  (7503)                  % remainder of the message will not be looked at by XROUT ... thus
  8164  (7504)                  % the user data can be coded in any format legible to the
  8165  (7505)                  % receiving (server) task."
  8166  (7506)                  %
  8167  (7507)                  % MEASURED 2026-08-18, the 25-byte join, which agrees:
  8168  (7508)                  %   123  65  0 12 | 255 10 'CHAT-LOBBY' | 1 5 'RONNY' 0 0
  8169  (7509)                  %    ^serial ^len=12   ^param 1: string, 10 bytes  ^payload at 16
  8170  (7510)                  %
  8171  (7511)                  % DERIVED, NEVER HARD-CODED. 16 is only right for a ten-character
  8172  (7512)                  % room name; the parameter block moves with the name, and a
  8173  (7513)                  % constant here would break the moment /join names a longer room.
  8174  (7514)                  %
  8175  (7515)                  % The alternative reading - 6 + the name length - fits this one
  8176  (7516)                  % capture just as well and is WRONG in general: it cannot see a
  8177  (7517)                  % second parameter block, and XSLET has an optional third.
  8178  (7518)                  %
  8179  (7519)                  % The payload is slid down to offset 0 so that handleMessage
  8180  (7520)                  % parses one layout and one only. Every other route into it -
  8181  (7521)                  % an ordinary XMTNO message straight from a magic number - has no
  8182  (7522)                  % header at all, and this is the single place that knows that.
  8183  (7523)                  % ---- A MESSAGE OF OURS THAT COULD NOT BE DELIVERED ----------
  8184  (7524)                  % This is what XFSEC bought, and it has to be caught BEFORE
  8185  (7525)                  % handleMessage: the bytes in inBuf are our own outgoing Said or
  8186  (7526)                  % Joined coming home, so parsing them as an arrival would have
  8187  (7527)                  % the room answering itself.
  8188  (7528)                  %
  8189  (7529)                  % THE QUESTION THIS BRANCH WAS BUILT TO ANSWER, AND THE ANSWER.
  8190  (7530)                  %
  8191  (7531)                  % A returned message carries what WE wrote, and what we wrote
  8192  (7532)                  % names the SPEAKER, not the member it could not reach - so it
  8193  (7533)                  % was not obvious that anything here identifies the destination.
  8194  (7534)                  % XMPFMST hands back a magic number for an ordinary arrival; the
  8195  (7535)                  % guide never says what that field holds on a returned one.
  8196  (7536)                  %
  8197  (7537)                  % MEASURED 2026-08-18 on D100, and it IS the destination:
  8198  (7538)                  %
  8199  (7539)                  %   SV: returned msg, st=  16915
  8200  (7540)                  %   SV: it names slot          2
  8201  (7541)                  %   SV: reaped dead slot       2
  8202  (7542)                  %
  8203  (7543)                  % 16915 is XMXEIMA, "Invalid magic number" - the reason the
  8204  (7544)                  % message came home. Note where that code turned up: as the
  8205  (7545)                  % REASON ON A RETURN, not as the status of the send. The first
  8206  (7546)                  % attempt at this fix tested XMPSEND's own status for exactly
  8207  (7547)                  % that number and never fired, because XMPSEND answered XMOK and
  8208  (7548)                  % handed the message to a port that was already gone.
  8209  (7549)                  %
  8210  (7550)                  % The slot print stays. It costs nothing - a member has to die
  8211  (7551)                  % before it runs - and it separates "one of ours bounced" from
  8212  (7552)                  % "something bounced", which is the first thing an operator
  8213  (7553)                  % needs to know and the one thing the reap line cannot say.
  8214  (7554)                  IF msgType = XMTRE THEN
  8215  (7555)                      nBounced + 1 =: nBounced
  8216  (7556)                      logLine('SV bounced msg st=', receiveStat)
  8217  (7557)  
  8218  (7558)                      findByMagic(senderMagic) =: wasMember
  8219  (7559)  
  8220  (7560)                      % PRINT ONLY WHEN IT IS ONE OF OURS. The note above says the
  8221  (7561)                      % point of this line is to separate "one of ours bounced"
  8222  (7562)                      % from "something bounced", and only the first is worth
  8223  (7563)                      % waking an operator for - it means a member has died. The
  8224  (7564)                      % other case is now just a number in STATUS, because it was
  8225  (7565)                      % arriving often enough to bury everything else.
  8226  (7566)                      IF wasMember >< 0 THEN
  8227  (7567)                          OUTPUT(1, 'AL22', 'SV: returned msg, st= ')
  8228  (7568)                          OUTPUT(1, 'I6', receiveStat)
  8229  (7569)                          OUTPUT(1, 'AL1', '$')
  8230  (7570)                          OUTPUT(1, 'AL22', 'SV: it names slot     ')
  8231  (7571)                          OUTPUT(1, 'I6', wasMember)
  8232  (7572)                          OUTPUT(1, 'AL1', '$')
  8233  (7573)                      ENDIF
  8234  (7574)  
  8235  (7575)                      IF wasMember >< 0 THEN
  8236  (7576)                          1 =: memberDead(wasMember)
  8237  (7577)                          1 =: deadFound
  8238  (7578)                      ENDIF
  8239  (7579)                  ELSE
  8240  (7580)                  IF msgType = XMROU THEN
  8241  (7581)                      % THE SERVICE BYTE, TAKEN BEFORE ANYTHING MOVES. The payload
  8242  (7582)                      % is slid down to offset 0 below, so by the time the seat
  8243  (7583)                      % rule runs inBuf(1) is chat data, not the header. Reading
  8244  (7584)                      % it there is how the feedback loop went unnoticed in
  8245  (7585)                      % review - the test looked right and was inspecting the
  8246  (7586)                      % wrong bytes.
  8247  (7587)                      inBuf(1) =: letterService
  8248  (7588)  
  8249  (7589)                      4 + inBuf(2) * 256 + inBuf(3) =: payloadAt
  8250  (7590)  
  8251  (7591)                      % A letter with nothing behind the header is a connect this
  8252  (7592)                      % program has no answer for. Dropping it says so; the seat is
  8253  (7593)                      % already spent either way. Note this is >=, not >: a payload
  8254  (7594)                      % that starts exactly AT the end is an empty payload.
  8255  (7595)                      IF payloadAt >= readLength THEN
  8256  (7596)                          % COUNTED, NOT PRINTED. This is an EXPECTED arrival, not
  8257  (7597)                          % a fault: XROUT's answer to a seat return comes back on
  8258  (7598)                          % this very port as a routed message with no payload, so
  8259  (7599)                          % there is one of these per seat returned. Printing it
  8260  (7600)                          % each time buried the terminal - STATUS reports the
  8261  (7601)                          % count, and a count that climbs while the room is quiet
  8262  (7602)                          % is the feedback loop described below coming back.
  8263  (7603)                          nEmptyLet + 1 =: nEmptyLet
  8264  (7604)                      ELSE
  8265  (7605)                          % (the seat for this arrival is settled after
  8266  (7606)                          %  handleMessage, by the one rule below)
  8267  (7607)                          readLength - payloadAt =: readLength
  8268  (7608)                          FOR i IN 1:readLength DO
  8269  (7609)                              inBuf(payloadAt + i - 1) =: inBuf(i - 1)
  8270  (7610)                          ENDFOR
  8271  (7611)  
  8272  (7612)                          % ---- SEAT ACCOUNTING, and it belongs HERE ----------
  8273  (7613)                          % The seat is spent by the ARRIVAL of a routed message,
  8274  (7614)                          % not by any particular kind of message inside it. Doing
  8275  (7615)                          % it per kind is what the C# side got wrong: a letter
  8276  (7616)                          % carrying anything but a join, or a letter too damaged
  8277  (7617)                          % to decode at all, never reaches a handler - so no
  8278  (7618)                          % handler can give its seat back, and the room silently
  8279  (7619)                          % loses a seat for good.
  8280  (7620)                          %
  8281  (7621)                          % XROUT decremented its free-connection counter to get
  8282  (7622)                          % this letter here, so a seat is already spent by the
  8283  (7623)                          % time this line runs. There are now exactly two places
  8284  (7624)                          % that give one back, and between them they cover both
  8285  (7625)                          % ways a member can stop being one:
  8286  (7626)                          %
  8287  (7627)                          %   handleMessage, kLeave   they said goodbye;
  8288  (7628)                          %   reapDead                they did not, and a send to
  8289  (7629)                          %                           them came back saying the
  8290  (7630)                          %                           port is gone.
  8291  (7631)                          findByMagic(senderMagic) =: wasMember
  8292  (7632)  
  8293  (7633)                          % THE POINT OF readStatus. A read that did not
  8294  (7634)                          % actually succeed has not put anything new in
  8295  (7635)                          % inBuf - what is there is whatever the LAST
  8296  (7636)                          % successful read left, and dispatching it again is
  8297  (7637)                          % how one real message would come back as every
  8298  (7638)                          % arrival after it.
  8299  (7639)                          IF readStatus = XMOK THEN
  8300  (7640)                              handleMessage
  8301  (7641)                          ENDIF
  8302  (7642)                      ENDIF
  8303  (7643)  
  8304  (7644)                      % ---- THE SEAT BELONGS TO THE ARRIVAL, SO SETTLE IT HERE ----
  8305  (7645)                      % MEASURED 2026-08-18 by a load run: twenty C# clients joined
  8306  (7646)                      % at once and CHAT-LOBBY went from 16 free seats to 2 and
  8307  (7647)                      % stayed there, even though every welcomed member left again
  8308  (7648)                      % cleanly. Fourteen seats gone.
  8309  (7649)                      %
  8310  (7650)                      % The comment a few lines up has described this hole all
  8311  (7651)                      % along - "a letter carrying anything but a join, or a letter
  8312  (7652)                      % too damaged to decode at all, never reaches a handler - so
  8313  (7653)                      % no handler can give its seat back" - and named it as
  8314  (7654)                      % something the C# side got wrong. It was wrong HERE too. The
  8315  (7655)                      % code only ever returned a seat on a clean /leave or a reap,
  8316  (7656)                      % so an empty letter, a join refused because the name was
  8317  (7657)                      % taken, and a letter from a stranger each cost a seat for
  8318  (7658)                      % good. Under a burst that is most of them.
  8319  (7659)                      %
  8320  (7660)                      % ONE RULE, APPLIED TO EVERY LETTER: XROUT spent a seat to
  8321  (7661)                      % deliver this, so if the sender is not sitting in the room
  8322  (7662)                      % once we have finished with it, nobody is holding that seat
  8323  (7663)                      % and it must go straight back.
  8324  (7664)                      %
  8325  (7665)                      % This does NOT double-count with the /leave and reapDead
  8326  (7666)                      % paths. A leave arrives as an ORDINARY message, port to
  8327  (7667)                      % port, and never comes through this branch at all - only the
  8328  (7668)                      % JOIN is a letter. So each member's seat is returned exactly
  8329  (7669)                      % once, by whichever of the three applies.
  8330  (7670)                      %
  8331  (7671)                      % KNOWN GAP, said plainly rather than left to be discovered:
  8332  (7672)                      % a letter from somebody who IS already a member keeps its
  8333  (7673)                      % seat and should not. Clients send port to port after
  8334  (7674)                      % joining, so it does not arise in normal use, and guessing a
  8335  (7675)                      % fix for a case never seen would be worse than naming it.
  8336  (7676)                      % ---- ONLY A REAL LETTER SPENT A SEAT --------------------
  8337  (7677)                      % MEASURED, and it is the worst kind of bug: a FEEDBACK
  8338  (7678)                      % LOOP that this very block created. Four users joined and
  8339  (7679)                      % left, and CHAT-LOBBY went from 16 free seats to 3470 while
  8340  (7680)                      % the terminal filled with "DROP empty letter" for ever.
  8341  (7681)                      %
  8342  (7682)                      % Here is the loop. Returning a seat means sending XMPINFC
  8343  (7683)                      % to XROUT. XROUT ANSWERS, and its answer arrives back on
  8344  (7684)                      % this very port as a routed message with no chat payload -
  8345  (7685)                      % which the code above drops as an empty letter, and which
  8346  (7686)                      % this rule then read as "a letter whose sender is not a
  8347  (7687)                      % member", so it returned ANOTHER seat, which produced
  8348  (7688)                      % ANOTHER answer. The note at the foot of this file has
  8349  (7689)                      % warned since the first version that the XMPINFC reply
  8350  (7690)                      % "comes back later on our own port as an ordinary message"
  8351  (7691)                      % and that ignoring it was "safe by accident until somebody
  8352  (7692)                      % says so". It stopped being safe the moment this rule
  8353  (7693)                      % started acting on every routed arrival.
  8354  (7694)                      %
  8355  (7695)                      % THE DISCRIMINATOR IS BYTE 1. A client's letter carries the
  8356  (7696)                      % XSLET service number, 65. XROUT OVERWRITES that byte with
  8357  (7697)                      % a return status when it answers - the header comment in
  8358  (7698)                      % the receive loop says so, and it is the same test the C#
  8359  (7699)                      % load client uses to spot a refused letter. So a letter
  8360  (7700)                      % with 65 in byte 1 came from a client and spent a seat;
  8361  (7701)                      % anything else is XROUT talking to us and spent nothing.
  8362  (7702)                      IF letterService = XSLET THEN
  8363  (7703)                          findByMagic(senderMagic) =: nowMember
  8364  (7704)                          IF nowMember = 0 THEN
  8365  (7705)                              xmpinfc(0, myPort, 1, 0) =: returnStatus
  8366  (7706)                              IF returnStatus >< XMOK THEN
  8367  (7707)                                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL22', 'SV: seat return failed')
  8368  (7708)                                  OUTPUT(1, 'I6', returnStatus)
  8369  (7709)                                  OUTPUT(1, 'AL1', '$')
  8370  (7710)                                  logLine('SV seat back st=  ', returnStatus)
  8371  (7711)                              ENDIF
  8372  (7712)                          ENDIF
  8373  (7713)                      ENDIF
  8374  (7714)                  ELSE
  8375  (7715)                      % No header on this path: an ordinary message sent straight
  8376  (7716)                      % to our magic number is exactly the bytes the peer wrote.
  8377  (7717)                      % Same readStatus guard as the routed path above - see the
  8378  (7718)                      % note there.
  8379  (7719)                      IF readStatus = XMOK THEN
  8380  (7720)                          handleMessage
  8381  (7721)                      ENDIF
  8382  (7722)                  ENDIF
  8383  (7723)                  ENDIF
  8384  (7724)  
  8385  (7725)                  % ---- AND NOW BURY ANYBODY WHO TURNED OUT TO BE GONE ---------
  8386  (7726)                  % One place, after everything the arrival caused has finished,
  8387  (7727)                  % because that is the only depth at which outBuf is free. Both
  8388  (7728)                  % branches above lead here on purpose: a member can be found
  8389  (7729)                  % dead by any broadcast, and the routed and direct paths both
  8390  (7730)                  % broadcast.
  8391  (7731)                  IF deadFound >< 0 THEN
  8392  (7732)                      reapDead
  8393  (7733)                  ENDIF
  8394  (7734)  
  8395  (7735)                  % ---- AND STOP, IF AN OPERATOR ASKED ------------------------
  8396  (7736)                  % Here rather than where the AdminStop arrived, so the reply
  8397  (7737)                  % path and the reaping above finish first. A stop that abandons
  8398  (7738)                  % a half-sent message is not a clean stop.
  8399  (7739)                  %
  8400  (7740)                  % This RETURN is different from the one on an empty drain. That
  8401  (7741)                  % one goes to sleep and is woken by the next arrival; this one
  8402  (7742)                  % ends the program for good - the ports go with it and SINTRAN
  8403  (7743)                  % clears the names, measured 2026-08-18.
  8404  (7744)                  IF stopWanted THEN
  8405  (7745)                      OUTPUT(1, 'AL17', 'CHATSV: stopping$')
  8406  (7746)                      RETURN
  8407  (7747)                  ENDIF
  8408  (7748)              ENDIF
  8409  (7749)          ENDDO
  8410  (7750)      ENDROUTINE
  8411  (7751)  
  8412  (7752)  ENDMODULE
  8413  (7753)  
  8414  (7754)  % ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
  8415  (7755)  % STILL NOT DONE, and worth saying rather than leaving to be met:
  8416  (7756)  %
  8417  (7757)  %  1. THIS ITEM USED TO READ "Nothing has been compiled", and it is kept here
  8418  (7758)  %     in corrected form rather than deleted, because everything it worried about
  8419  (7759)  %     has now been settled by the compiler itself rather than by argument:
  8420  (7760)  %     INTEGER4, two-dimensional BYTES ARRAY, the implicit extra ARRAY keyword on
  8421  (7761)  %     a BYTES declaration, BOOLEAN := TRUE, and the XMMSGIDENTIFIER and
  8422  (7762)  %     XMUSERADDRESS names out of XMP-B02:IMPT. All of it compiles and runs.
  8423  (7763)  %
  8424  (7764)  %     The one thing the list did NOT anticipate is the one that cost the most:
  8425  (7765)  %     a letter arrives with its XSLET header still in front of it. No amount of
  8426  (7766)  %     checking declarations against a manual would have found that - only the
  8427  (7767)  %     raw dump did.
  8428  (7768)  %
  8429  (7769)  %  2. The seat returned on Leave uses XMPINFC(flags, portNumber, extraConn,
  8430  (7770)  %     serialNumber). Its reply comes back later on our own port as an ordinary
  8431  (7771)  %     message, which handleMessage will see and not understand. Decide what to
  8432  (7772)  %     do with it - ignoring an unknown kind is already the behaviour, so this
  8433  (7773)  %     is safe, but it is safe by accident until somebody says so.
  8434  (7774)  %
  8435  (7775)  %  3. Each reason string carries its length as a separate constant, because
  8436  (7776)  %     PLANC has no LENGTH routine and SIZE's meaning for a BYTES parameter is
  8437  (7777)  %     not established. A string and its number must be changed together.
  8438  (7778)  % ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
  8439  (7779)
